### [1] [Finite-Data Error Bounds for Approximating the Koopman Operator: Sampling Measures, Super-Polynomial Convergence and Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31817)
*Daniel Fassler,Rachel Morris,Jason Bramburger,Simone Brugiapaglia*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This work establishes finite-data convergence bounds for the EDMD Galerkin approximation of the Koopman operator, achieving Monte Carlo rates and, in structured settings, super-polynomial rates, plus recovery guarantees in the undersampled regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EDMD is widely used for data-driven Koopman operator approximation without knowledge of the underlying dynamics, but existing convergence analyses are mostly asymptotic in data. Practical finite-data convergence results remain incomplete in general settings, motivating sharper non-asymptotic guarantees.

Method: The authors prove finite-data error bounds for the EDMD Galerkin approximation of the Koopman operator, with rates proportional to the inverse root of the number of samples (Monte Carlo rate). The analysis covers discrete and continuous dynamics, including stochastic systems, and leverages compressed sensing ideas for an EDMD variant in the undersampled regime.

Result: Finite-data bounds scaling as the Monte Carlo rate are established for multiple problem classes (discrete/continuous and stochastic dynamics). In more structured settings, super-polynomial rates are shown both theoretically and computationally. Recovery guarantees are also derived for an EDMD variant under undersampling.

Conclusion: The paper provides practical finite-data convergence guarantees for EDMD, generalizing beyond asymptotic analyses, and demonstrates that structured problems admit much faster rates, with compressed-sensing-inspired recovery in undersampled regimes.

Abstract: The Koopman operator is a well-established framework for lifting nonlinear dynamical systems to an infinite-dimensional space where dynamics are linear. Extended Dynamic Mode Decomposition (EDMD) is a widely used method in data-driven dynamics as it provides a Galerkin approximation of the Koopman operator on the finite-dimensional span of a prescribed dictionary of observable functions. In particular, EDMD only requires samples from the dynamical system, describing the Koopman operator without knowledge of the underlying dynamics. While many studies analyze asymptotic convergence results for EDMD in terms of data, the question of more practical finite-data convergence results remains incomplete in general settings. In this work, we prove bounds for the finite-data EDMD Galerkin approximation of the Koopman operator by a rate proportional to the inverse of the root of the number of samples (the Monte Carlo rate). These results apply to multiple classes of problems, i.e., for discrete or continuous dynamics, including stochastic systems. In certain more structured setting, we demonstrate super-polynomial rates are achievable both theoretically and computationally. Finally, we derive recovery guarantees for an EDMD variant in the undersampled regime inspired by compressed sensing techniques.

</details>


### [2] [Mapped Multi-Patch Spectral Extreme Learning Machine for Partial Differential Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31909)
*Yiran Wang,Suchuan Dong*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper studies Spectral-ELM, a fixed-feature solver combining Chebyshev-Gauss-Lobatto differentiation matrices with extreme learning machine trial functions, using mapped multi-patch formulations for curved domains, and evaluates its accuracy against several alternative methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work seeks to develop an accurate and efficient fixed-feature spectral solver for smooth PDEs, particularly on curved domains, while addressing near-linear dependence among discrete features and enabling high-order discretizations.

Method: CGL differentiation matrices are applied to nodal values of an ELM trial function. Curved domains are handled via a mapped multi-patch formulation using reference squares, metric terms, and continuity of solution and normal flux across patches. Local QR orthogonalization reduces feature dependence. Comparisons include direct CGL collocation, analytic ELM differentiation, a global Random Feature Method, and TransNet sampling.

Result: For a smooth elliptic problem on a square, direct CGL collocation yields the smallest error; spectral and analytic differentiation give comparable errors for the same trial space. On curved-domain problems, mapped direct CGL collocation and mapped Spectral-ELM achieve similar accuracy with comparable unknowns. Tests include a matched KdV case, a small 3D example, and a Poisson problem on a curved domain with a hole.

Conclusion: The formulation is well suited to smooth PDEs in one to three dimensions, where tensor-product grids support accurate high-order discretizations. Sparse-grid and dimension-adaptive strategies are promising for extending to higher-dimensional problems.

Abstract: We study a fixed-feature solver, referred to as Spectral-ELM, in which Chebyshev--Gauss--Lobatto (CGL) differentiation matrices are applied to the nodal values of an extreme learning machine (ELM) trial function. Curved domains are treated using a mapped multi-patch formulation. Each curved quadrilateral patch is represented as the image of a reference square, physical derivatives are computed from the associated metric terms, and adjacent patches are coupled by enforcing continuity of the solution and its normal flux. Local QR orthogonalization is used to reduce near-linear dependence among the discrete features. The numerical study includes comparisons with direct CGL collocation, an analytically differentiated ELM, a global Random Feature Method, and a TransNet sampling strategy. It also includes a matched KdV test, a small three-dimensional example, and a Poisson problem on a curved domain containing a hole. For a smooth elliptic problem on a square, direct CGL collocation gives the smallest error. When applied to the same fixed-feature trial space, spectral and analytic differentiation produce comparable errors. For the curved-domain problem, mapped direct CGL collocation and mapped Spectral-ELM achieve similar accuracy with comparable numbers of unknowns. The present formulation is particularly well suited to smooth PDEs in one to three dimensions, where tensor-product grids enable accurate high-order discretizations, while sparse-grid and dimension-adaptive strategies offer promising directions for extension to higher-dimensional problems.

</details>


### [3] [Accurate Density of States Estimation: A Comparative Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31953)
*Dong Min Roh,Chao Yang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes estimating the density of states (DOS) of large sparse symmetric matrices by fitting the cumulative DOS (CDOS) and differentiating the fit, using stochastic Lanczos with monotone piecewise-cubic interpolation or Gaussian-process regression, avoiding fixed-bandwidth Gaussian smoothing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing DOS estimators typically rely on Gaussian smoothing with a fixed bandwidth, which couples accuracy to a chosen smoothing scale. The authors seek a practical method that produces nonnegative, normalized DOS without such a fixed bandwidth constraint, using cumulative interpolation.

Method: Stochastic Lanczos supplies Ritz values and weights, averaged over runs to build CDOS midpoint data. Monotone piecewise-cubic interpolation gives a differentiable smooth CDOS, whose derivative yields a nonnegative, normalized, piecewise-quadratic DOS. Alternatively, GPR with single- and double-Gaussian covariance kernels approximates CDOS with uncertainty, and analytic differentiation gives the DOS. Estimators are compared against Gaussian-broadened stochastic Lanczos and Jackson-damped kernel polynomial approximations.

Result: Experiments on matrices from diverse scientific applications, evaluated at a common Gaussian validation resolution using local, integrated, and overall spectral shape error measures, show the midpoint spline produces accurate DOS approximations with relatively few matrix--vector products.

Conclusion: Cumulative interpolation of Lanczos-derived CDOS data is a practical and accurate DOS estimation approach, with the midpoint spline achieving good accuracy at low computational cost.

Abstract: We study density of states (DOS) estimation for large sparse real symmetric matrices by fitting the cumulative density of states (CDOS) and differentiating the fit. We use a stochastic Lanczos method to supply Ritz values and weights, which we average across Lanczos runs to construct CDOS midpoint data. Monotone piecewise-cubic interpolation of these data yields a smooth CDOS approximation that can be easily differentiated to yield a nonnegative, normalized, piecewise-quadratic DOS without requiring a Gaussian smoothing with a fixed bandwidth in its construction. We also consider approximating CDOS by using Gaussian-process regression (GPR) with single- and double-Gaussian covariance kernels, yielding an approximation with uncertainty information. DOS approximation is then obtained by analytic derivative of the GPR-based CDOS. Experiments on matrices from diverse scientific applications are performed to compare these estimators with Gaussian-broadened stochastic Lanczos and Jackson-damped kernel polynomial approximations. At a common Gaussian validation resolution, we use error measures that assess local discrepancies, integrated errors, and agreement in the overall spectral shape. The results demonstrate that the midpoint spline can produce accurate approximations to the DOS with relatively few matrix--vector products, supporting the use of cumulative interpolation as a practical DOS estimation.

</details>


### [4] [Finite element error estimates for a bilinear optimal control problem with pointwise tracking governed by a semilinear elliptic PDE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31987)
*Enrique Otarola,Daniel Quero,Matias Sasso*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper studies finite element approximations for a pointwise tracking optimal control problem governed by a semilinear elliptic PDE with bilinear control and point evaluations in the cost functional, establishing convergence and a priori error bounds for semidiscrete and fully discrete schemes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optimal control problems with pointwise tracking and bilinear (reaction-coefficient) controls governed by semilinear elliptic PDEs require rigorous numerical analysis, yet finite element error estimates for such settings remain underexplored.

Method: Two discretization schemes are proposed: a semidiscrete scheme (control not discretized) and a fully discrete scheme (control discretized with piecewise constant functions), with convergence and a priori error analysis.

Result: In 2D and 3D, both schemes yield error bounds of O(h|log h|) in L^2(Ω) for approximating a locally optimal control. In 2D, bounds improve to O(h) (fully discrete) and O(h^2|log h|^2) (semidiscrete). Numerical experiments confirm the performance.

Conclusion: The proposed finite element schemes are convergent with established a priori error bounds, and the sharpened 2D estimates plus numerical experiments validate their effectiveness for this pointwise tracking optimal control problem.

Abstract: We study finite element approximations for an optimal control problem with pointwise tracking governed by a semilinear elliptic PDE. The control variable enters the state equation as a reaction coefficient, resulting in a bilinear control, and the cost functional includes point evaluations of the state variable. We propose two discretization schemes: a semidiscrete scheme, where the control variable is not discretized, and a fully discrete scheme, where the control variable is discretized using piecewise constant functions. In two and three dimensions, for both schemes, we establish convergence of discrete solutions and prove a priori error bounds that behave as $\mathcal{O}(h|\log h|)$ in the $L^2(Ω)$-norm for approximating a locally optimal control. In two dimensions, these bounds are improved to $\mathcal{O}(h)$ for the fully discrete scheme and $\mathcal{O}(h^{2}|\log h|^2)$ for the semidiscrete scheme. We conclude with numerical experiments that demonstrate the performance of the proposed schemes.

</details>


### [5] [Distance-Residual Physics-Informed Neural Networks: A Deep Learning Framework for Differential and Partial Differential Inclusions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32043)
*Maria Filipkovska,Juan José Marín,Isil Oner,Francisco Periago,Krzysztof Rykaczewski*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: DR-PINNs approximate solutions of differential inclusions by replacing the pointwise residual with the squared distance from the differential operator to the admissible set, with proven consistency and efficient computability via convex quadratic programming.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard physics-informed neural networks rely on a pointwise PDE/ODE residual where the differential operator equals a prescribed function. Many governing laws instead constrain the operator to lie in a set-valued map (differential inclusions), which the classical residual cannot handle directly. A framework is needed to learn solutions of such set-valued/constrained systems.

Method: DR-PINNs replace the classical pointwise residual with the squared distance from the differential operator to the admissible set. For closed convex sets, this squared distance is differentiable with gradient given by the metric projection; when the set depends on the network state, that dependence enters via the chain rule. For admissible sets given as convex hulls of finitely many vertices, projection reduces to a small convex quadratic program, making the loss efficient to compute in the training loop.

Result: For both ordinary and partial DIs with set-valued reaction terms, the authors prove consistency: under closedness, measurability, convexity, and growth assumptions, any sequence of candidates satisfying initial (and, in the parabolic case, boundary) conditions whose continuous distance-residual functional tends to zero admits a subsequence converging to an exact solution. Numerical experiments show high accuracy on differential-inclusion benchmarks.

Conclusion: DR-PINNs provide a principled, efficient framework for solving differential inclusions. However, the consistency results are conditional on the continuous distance-residual functional and do not cover the finite-collocation training loss, optimizer behavior, or convergence rates.

Abstract: We introduce Distance-Residual Physics-Informed Neural Networks (DR-PINNs), a physics-informed learning framework for approximating solutions of ordinary and partial differential inclusions (DIs), governing laws in which a differential operator is constrained to lie in a set-valued map rather than equaling a prescribed function. The method replaces the classical pointwise PDE/ODE residual by the squared distance from the differential operator to the admissible set. This distance vanishes exactly when the inclusion is satisfied and measures the infimal correction needed for the operator to enter the admissible set. For a fixed closed convex admissible set, the squared distance is differentiable with respect to the operator value, with gradient given by the metric projection. When the admissible set also depends on the network state, that dependence is included through the chain rule. The framework encompasses ordinary and partial DIs with set-valued reaction terms. For both settings we prove consistency: under the stated closedness, measurability, convexity, and growth assumptions, any sequence of candidates satisfying the initial (and, in the parabolic case, boundary) conditions whose continuous distance-residual functional tends to zero admits a subsequence converging to an exact solution of the target inclusion. These are conditional statements for the continuous distance-residual functional; they do not cover the finite-collocation training loss, the behavior of the optimizer, or convergence rates. For admissible sets given as convex hulls of finitely many vertices, projection onto the set reduces to a small convex quadratic program, making the loss efficiently computable inside the training loop. Numerical experiments demonstrate high accuracy on the differential-inclusion benchmarks.

</details>


### [6] [Optimal convergence analysis of arbitrary Lagrangian-Eulerian finite element methods for two-phase Stokes flow problems with moving interface](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32066)
*Yi Liang,Cheng Wang,Pengtao Sun,Yan Chen,Jiarui Han*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: An ALE-based FEM in a monolithic framework is developed and analyzed for two-phase Stokes flow with moving interfaces and jump coefficients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two-phase Stokes flow problems with moving interfaces and jump coefficients pose significant analytical and numerical challenges, motivating a rigorous mixed finite element treatment.

Method: A monolithic ALE-based finite element method with semi- and fully discrete mixed schemes. The key technique is an H^1-projection tied to the ALE-induced mesh motion from the evolving interface.

Result: Optimal convergence of the H^1-projection and its ALE temporal derivative is proved in H^1 and L^2 norms; optimal error estimates are obtained for both semi- and fully discrete approximations in H^1 and L^2. Numerical experiments validate the theory.

Conclusion: The approach extends to Taylor-Hood and MINI mixed elements and to more general two-phase flow problems.

Abstract: In this paper, an arbitrary Lagrangian-Eulerian (ALE)-based finite element method (FEM) is developed and studied in a monolithic framework for a class of two-phase Stokes flow problems with moving interfaces and jump coefficients, where the mixed finite element approximation to Stokes moving interface problems is established and analyzed in both semi- and fully discrete schemes based on the ALE formulation. The key analytical technique involves a specific H^1-projection associated with the ALE-induced mesh motion due to the evolving interface. Optimal convergence properties of the proposed H^1-projection and its ALE temporal derivative are proved in both H^1 and L^2 norms, with which optimal error estimates are obtained for both semi- and fully discrete mixed finite element approximations to the studied Stokes moving interface problem in both H1 and L2 norms as well. Numerical experiments are carried out to validate all derived theoretical results. The developed analytical approach can be extended to Taylor-Hood and MINI mixed elements, as well as to more general two-phase flow problems.

</details>


### [7] [A merged-cell discontinuous Galerkin method for scalar conservation laws on curved domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32107)
*Yong Liu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A discontinuous Galerkin method for 2D scalar conservation laws on curved domains using cell merging, with proven optimal L² error estimates O(h^{k+1}) for all k≥1, independent of cut-cell area fractions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Handling curved domains on Cartesian grids typically produces small cut cells that cause time-step restrictions and/or loss of accuracy/stability. The paper aims for a cut-cell method that avoids these issues and yields provably optimal convergence independent of small cut-cell fractions.

Method: Small cut-cell intersections on a Cartesian grid are merged with neighboring cells into rectangular physical merged cells. Local space is Q_k restricted to each merged cell. An entropy-conservative flux is augmented with face viscosity and, for odd k, a skew derivative-jump correction on unmerged interior squares. The analysis combines a physical-cell L² projection with cancellation of regular-face projection errors.

Result: Exact semidiscrete L² dissipation and optimal error estimates for every k≥1, with O(h^{k+1}) for smooth solutions with homogeneous boundary trace; constants independent of cut-cell area fractions. A separate semidiscrete result covers linear transport with upwind inflow data. Burgers on a disk and linear advection on a smooth star show orders near k+1 for k=1,2,3.

Conclusion: The cell-merging DG framework provides stable, optimally convergent schemes on curved domains with robustness to small cut cells, validated by theory and numerical experiments.

Abstract: We analyse a discontinuous Galerkin method for two-dimensional scalar conservation laws on curved domains. Small intersections of a Cartesian grid with the domain are combined by rectangular cell merging. The local space is the restriction of $\mathbb Q_k$ to each physical merged cell. An entropy-conservative flux is supplemented by face viscosity and, for odd degrees, a skew derivative-jump correction on unmerged interior squares. We prove exact semidiscrete $L^2$ dissipation and an optimal error estimate for every fixed $k\ge1$. For smooth solutions with homogeneous trace on the entire boundary, the semi-discrete method satisfies an $O(h^{k+1})$ error bound. The constants are independent of the original cut-cell area fractions. The proof combines a physical-cell $L^2$ projection and cancellation of regular-face projection errors. A separate semidiscrete result covers linear transport with upwind inflow data. Computations for Burgers' equation on a disk and linear advection on a smooth star exhibit orders close to $k+1$ for $k=1,2,3$.

</details>


### [8] [Wave Breaking and Structure-Preserving Fully Discrete Method for Stochastic Camassa--Holm Equation with Additive Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32128)
*Liying Sun,Zizhao Sun,Liying Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper studies the stochastic Camassa–Holm equation with additive noise and shows that finite-time breakdown is almost surely due to wave breaking (slope → -∞) while amplitude and positive slope stay bounded. It also provides an initial-slope condition for wave breaking with positive probability, formulates a stochastic Hamiltonian/multi-symplectic structure, and proposes a structure-preserving fully discrete numerical method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The stochastic Camassa–Holm equation models nonlinear shallow-water waves under random external forcing, and understanding whether finite-time breakdown occurs via wave breaking—and how to compute it reliably—is important for both theory and numerics.

Method: Establishes almost-sure wave-breaking characterization; derives an initial-slope condition under a moment assumption; formulates the equation as a stochastic Hamiltonian PDE, proves a stochastic multi-symplectic conservation law, and derives the averaged H^1 energy evolution. Proposes a fully discrete scheme combining Fourier–Galerkin, symplectic Runge–Kutta, and exact solution of the linear stochastic subsystem.

Result: Wave breaking occurs with bounded amplitude and positive slope part, while minimum slope → -∞; an initial-slope condition guarantees breaking with positive probability; the numerical method preserves an integrated discrete stochastic multi-symplectic conservation law and linear growth of discrete averaged H^1 energy; under suitable regularity/resolution assumptions, the discrete dynamics retain the slope-steepening mechanism.

Conclusion: The stochastic Camassa–Holm equation exhibits wave breaking as its breakdown mechanism, and the proposed structure-preserving scheme provides a discrete counterpart of this continuous behavior.

Abstract: In this article, we investigate the stochastic Camassa--Holm equation driven by additive noise, which models nonlinear shallow-water waves under random external forcing. We establish that, almost surely, any finite-time breakdown occurs through wave breaking: the wave amplitude and the positive part of the slope remain bounded, whereas the minimum slope tends to $-\infty$. Under an additional moment assumption, we derive an initial-slope condition ensuring wave breaking with positive probability. We also formulate the equation as a stochastic Hamiltonian PDE, establish its stochastic multi-symplectic conservation law, and derive the evolution law for the averaged $H^1$ energy. Combining the Fourier--Galerkin method, symplectic Runge--Kutta integration, and exact solution of the linear stochastic subsystem, we propose a fully discrete method which preserves an integrated discrete stochastic multi-symplectic conservation law and the linear growth of the discrete averaged \(H^1\) energy. Under suitable regularity and resolution assumptions, we present that the fully discrete dynamics retain the slope-steepening mechanism responsible for wave breaking, providing a discrete counterpart of the continuous behavior.

</details>


### [9] [Positivity-preserving semi-Lagrangian discontinuous Galerkin methods for multi-species Vlasov-Ampère models of plasma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32206)
*Dauda Gambo,James A. Rossmanith*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A high-order, positivity-preserving semi-Lagrangian discontinuous Galerkin (SLDG) kinetic solver is developed for the multi-species Vlasov-Ampère system, using per-species phase-space meshes, operator splitting, and local positivity limiters, and validated on a range of single- and two-species plasma tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kinetic simulation of multi-species Vlasov-Ampère systems demands efficiency and accuracy across stiff, multi-scale regimes with strong ion-electron scale separation, which standard solvers struggle to handle while maintaining physical positivity of the distribution function.

Method: The solver is built in a semi-Lagrangian discontinuous Galerkin (SLDG) framework. Phase space is discretized with high-order discontinuous Galerkin finite elements, while time integration uses high-order operator splitting and a sequence of one-dimensional, unconditionally stable semi-Lagrangian updates. Each plasma species is represented on its own phase-space mesh to improve accuracy and efficiency. Local positivity-preserving limiters are applied per element and per species to eliminate spurious undershoots and rigorously guarantee positivity of cell averages at the next time step.

Result: The method's accuracy and robustness are verified on several standard single- and two-species test cases: manufactured solutions, two-stream instability, weak and strong Landau damping, ion-acoustic wave propagation, ion-acoustic shock formation, and KEEN wave dynamics.

Conclusion: The proposed high-order, positivity-preserving SLDG kinetic solver provides an accurate, robust, and efficient approach for multi-species Vlasov-Ampère simulations, including challenging stiff and multi-scale regimes.

Abstract: We develop a high-order and positivity-preserving kinetic solver for the multi-species Vlasov-Ampère system that is built within a semi-Lagrangian discontinuous Galerkin (SLDG) framework. The method is designed for efficiency and accuracy across a broad range of conditions, including stiff, multi-scale regimes with strong ion-electron scale separation. Phase space is discretized via high-order discontinuous Galerkin finite elements; time stepping is handled via high-order operator splitting and a series of one-dimensional, unconditionally stable, semi-Lagrangian updates. To improve both accuracy and efficiency, each plasma species is represented on a different phase-space mesh. On every element and for each species, local positivity-preserving limiters are applied to prevent spurious undershoots and rigorously ensure positivity of the cell averages at the next time step. The accuracy and robustness of the resulting method are verified on several standard single- and two-species test cases, including manufactured solutions, two-stream instability, weak and strong Landau damping, ion-acoustic wave propagation, ion-acoustic shock formation, and KEEN wave dynamics.

</details>


### [10] [Finite Expression Approximation of High-Dimensional PDEs Without the Curse of Dimensionality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32229)
*Zhi Heng Liu,Haizhao Yang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Finite expressions form a symbolic representation class that breaks the curse of dimensionality for several high-dimensional PDE classes, achieving polynomial-in-dimension evaluation cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcoming the curse of dimensionality in high-dimensional PDEs has mainly been addressed via neural-network architectures. The paper asks whether structured symbolic representations (finite expressions) can provide comparable dimension-robust complexity guarantees.

Method: For semilinear heat equations, finite expression approximations of the terminal condition and nonlinearity are propagated through the multilevel Picard framework to yield randomized pointwise approximations. For semilinear Kolmogorov equations (Laplacian diffusion, zero drift), diagonal Black-Scholes equations, and the Laplace Dirichlet problem on a half-space, deterministic finite expression approximations are constructed. A key ingredient is building finite expressions that approximate the PDE solution while preserving the growth and Lipschitz structures required by stochastic solution theory.

Result: Randomized pointwise approximations with prescribed RMS accuracy are obtained for semilinear heat equations. Deterministic finite expression approximations with arbitrarily small spatial L^p error are built for the other PDE classes. Under suitable growth and regularity assumptions, evaluation cost is bounded polynomially in both dimension and reciprocal accuracy.

Conclusion: Dimension-robust approximation of high-dimensional PDEs is not restricted to neural networks: structured symbolic expressions from a fixed dictionary achieve comparable polynomial-complexity guarantees, providing a rigorous foundation for finite expression methods as a representation paradigm in high-dimensional scientific computing.

Abstract: We establish that finite expressions form a symbolic representation class capable of overcoming the curse of dimensionality for several classes of high-dimensional partial differential equations. For semilinear heat equations, we show how finite expression approximations of the terminal condition and nonlinearity can be propagated through the multilevel Picard framework to produce randomized pointwise approximations with prescribed root-mean-square accuracy. For semilinear Kolmogorov equations with Laplacian diffusion and zero drift, diagonal Black--Scholes equations, and the Laplace Dirichlet problem on a half-space, we construct deterministic finite expression approximations with arbitrarily small spatial $L^p$ error. Under suitable growth and regularity assumptions, the evaluation cost of the resulting approximants is bounded polynomially in the dimension and the reciprocal accuracy. A key ingredient in the nonlinear setting is the construction of finite expressions that approximate the PDE solution while preserving the growth and Lipschitz structures required by the stochastic solution theory. More broadly, our results show that dimension-robust approximation of high-dimensional PDEs is not restricted to conventional neural-network architectures: structured symbolic expressions generated from a fixed dictionary can achieve comparable polynomial-complexity guarantees. This provides a rigorous foundation for finite expression methods as a representation paradigm for high-dimensional scientific computing.

</details>


### [11] [Isothermal walls in the compressible Navier-Stokes equations: affine entropy, ballistic energy, and entropy-stable discrete formulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32249)
*Matteo Parsani,Rasha AlJahdali,Lisandro Dalcin*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Develops entropy-stable isothermal-wall boundary conditions for compressible Navier–Stokes using an affine entropy modification, with two DG-SBP-SAT formulations validated via theory and numerical tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Isothermal no-slip walls are common in compressible simulations, but enforcing them can break entropy stability (suppressing heat transfer or creating spurious entropy production). The paper seeks wall conditions that remain entropy-stable while correctly allowing heat flux.

Method: Adds total-energy density divided by wall temperature to the canonical convex entropy, which cancels the wall term without suppressing heat transfer while preserving the entropy Hessian, relative entropy, and volume entropy production. Two wall formulations are built on a common DG discretization with SBP and SAT: a complete-residual formulation (penalizing full wall-data residual, balancing energy via complete numerical wall flux) and a Fourier-energy-exact formulation (entropy-neutral skew correction plus momentum-projected penalty, matching one-sided Fourier heat flux at every wall node/resolution).

Result: Pointwise tests confirm local identities; manufactured-solution studies with entropy-stable interior coupling show convergence consistent with order p_s+1 in discrete L2 norm; thermal-relaxation tests verify semi-discrete energy and adapted-entropy balances; 3D tests show balance closure in closed domains, curved-wall trace convergence, and applicability to complex supersonic separated flow.

Conclusion: The affine entropy modification enables entropy-stable isothermal-wall conditions at continuous and semi-discrete levels, with both DG formulations giving correct (zero/nonpositive) wall contributions and demonstrated accuracy and robustness on challenging flows.

Abstract: We develop entropy-stable isothermal-wall conditions for the compressible Navier--Stokes equations at continuous and semi-discrete levels. For a stationary, impermeable, no-slip wall at constant positive temperature, adding total-energy density divided by wall temperature to the canonical convex entropy cancels the wall contribution without suppressing heat transfer. This affine modification preserves the entropy Hessian, relative entropy, and volume entropy production. It is proportional to total ballistic-energy density and yields a conditional $L_2$-type bound based on the initial field under the stated assumptions. Two wall formulations use a common discontinuous Galerkin discretization with the summation-by-parts property and simultaneous approximation terms. Both give zero adapted wall contribution without a penalty and a nonpositive contribution with it. The complete-residual formulation penalizes the full wall-data residual and balances fluid energy through its complete numerical wall flux. The Fourier-energy-exact formulation combines an entropy-neutral skew correction with a momentum-projected penalty so that the complete numerical outward energy flux equals the one-sided numerical Fourier heat flux at every wall node and spatial resolution. Pointwise tests verify the local identities. Manufactured-solution studies with entropy-stable interior coupling demonstrate convergence rates consistent with order $p_s+1$ in the discrete $L_2$ norm, where $p_s$ is the solution polynomial degree. Thermal-relaxation calculations verify the semi-discrete energy and adapted-entropy balances. Three-dimensional calculations demonstrate balance closure in a closed domain, convergence of curved-wall traces, and applicability to a complex supersonic separated flow.

</details>


### [12] [Application of Machine Learning to the Computation of Parameters in the SUPG Method for Unstructured and Anisotropic Meshes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32358)
*Petr Knobloch,Manoj Prakash*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper numerically studies a machine-learning approach for computing optimal stabilization parameters in the SUPG method, extending it to unstructured and anisotropically adapted meshes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stabilized methods for convection-dominated problems require stabilization parameters whose optimal choice is unknown yet strongly affects the quality of the approximate solution.

Method: The authors numerically investigate their previously proposed machine-learning approach for computing stabilization parameters, applied to the SUPG method for convection-diffusion equations, on unstructured meshes, and extend it to meshes from anisotropic mesh adaptation.

Result: The ML-based parameter computation approach is studied numerically on unstructured meshes and extended to anisotropically adapted meshes.

Conclusion: The machine-learning approach for determining stabilization parameters proves applicable beyond structured settings, working with unstructured and anisotropic adaptive meshes.

Abstract: Solution of convection-dominated problems using stabilized methods often requires to specify stabilization parameters whose optimal choice is not known but which considerably influence the quality of the approximate solution. In a recent work of the authors, an approach for computing these parameters by machine learning was proposed and applied to the streamline upwind/Petrov-Galerkin (SUPG) method for convection-diffusion equations. In the present paper, this approach is studied numerically for unstructured meshes and extended to meshes obtained by anisotropic mesh adaptation.

</details>


### [13] [Active Subspace-Guided Free-Form Deformation with Sinkhorn Autoencoders for Reduced-Order Modelling of Parametrised Shape Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32373)
*G. Padula,C. Giovannini,G. Rozza,A. Dashtimanesh*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A two-stage reduction pipeline combines Active Subspace-guided control-point selection with a Sinkhorn Autoencoder trained on reduced FFD weights, then compares non-intrusive ROMs mapping full FFD weights, AS-reduced weights, or SAE latent codes to quantities of interest on two PDE test cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geometrically parametrised PDEs (e.g., shape optimisation) require solving PDEs over many deformed domains, and Free-form Deformation yields a high-dimensional parameter space where most directions barely affect the QoI, so dimension reduction is needed.

Method: Stage 1: Active Subspace-guided structured control-point selection identifies a low-dimensional subspace of FFD weights driving the QoI. Stage 2: a Sinkhorn Autoencoder learns the distribution of reduced weights in a compact latent space by minimising the Wasserstein distance between aggregated posterior and prior. Three non-intrusive ROMs (Random Forests, Gaussian Processes, K-Nearest Neighbours) map full FFD weights, AS-reduced weights, or SAE latent codes to the QoI.

Result: Tested on a non-linear heat equation with Gaussian random Robin boundary data on a deformed Stanford Bunny, and on drag prediction for the DTC Hull bulb under FFD deformations. The generative model captures the bulk of the QoI distribution over comparable support, thinning the shoulder opposite the design objective, with extremes in the design-relevant direction matching the FFD sample. SAE latent-space ROMs yield smaller test errors than full FFD weights in most regressor-metric combinations, while AS-reduced weights give no comparable gain. Only the latent representation attains a positive predictive coefficient Q².

Conclusion: The SAE latent representation outperforms both full FFD weights and AS-reduced weights as inputs to non-intrusive ROMs, achieving smaller errors and the only positive Q², validating the proposed two-stage FFD reduction pipeline.

Abstract: Geometrically parametrised PDEs arise in shape optimisation, where solving a PDE over many deformed domains at manageable cost is a central challenge. Free-form Deformation (FFD) parametrises shape variations through a lattice of control points, but the resulting parameter space is typically high-dimensional, with most directions barely affecting the quantity of interest (QoI). We address this with a two-stage reduction pipeline. An Active Subspace (AS)-guided structured control-point selection identifies the low-dimensional subspace of FFD weights driving the QoI. A Sinkhorn Autoencoder (SAE) is then trained on the reduced weights, learning their distribution in a compact latent space by minimising the Wasserstein distance between aggregated posterior and prior. We compare three non-intrusive Reduced-order Models (ROMs), mapping full FFD weights, AS-reduced weights, or SAE latent codes to the QoI, each using Random Forests, Gaussian Processes, and K-Nearest Neighbours. We test the methodology on a non-linear heat equation with Gaussian random Robin boundary data on a deformed Stanford Bunny, and on drag prediction for the DTC Hull bulb under FFD deformations. In both cases, the generative model captures the bulk of the QoI distribution over a comparable support, thinning the shoulder opposite the design objective, while its extremes in the design-relevant direction match the FFD sample within numerical and sampling uncertainty. ROMs learned in the SAE latent space yield smaller test errors than those on full FFD weights in most regressor-metric combinations, whereas AS-reduced weights give no comparable gain. Only the latent representation attains a positive predictive coefficient $Q^2$.

</details>


### [14] [A Structure-Preserving LOBPCG Method for Computing Several of the Largest Generalized Singular Triplets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32406)
*Xinyu Shan*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper develops a structure-preserving LOBPCG method for computing the largest positive generalized singular values and associated singular vectors of a full-column-rank matrix pair (A, B), avoiding normal-equation matrices and using componentwise search subspaces with an adapted IHL trick.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Computing several largest generalized singular values and vectors of (A, B) typically requires forming normal-equation matrices, which can be costly and numerically undesirable. Existing unstructured or Gram-matrix-based LOBPCG implementations do not exploit the special structure of the problem.

Method: The method is derived from the Hermitian-definite Jordan-Wielandt pencil to avoid forming normal-equation matrices. It exploits the pencil's block structure and spectral symmetry to build separate search subspaces for left and right generalized singular vectors. The Rayleigh-Ritz procedure needs only an SVD of a small projected cross matrix. An improved Hetmaniuk-Lehoucq (IHL) trick is adapted to construct stable conjugate search directions in the two component spaces. An explicit correspondence between componentwise and augmented Rayleigh-Ritz coefficient matrices is established, allowing the componentwise IHL construction within an augmented, structure-preserving LOBPCG formulation without assembling the augmented basis.

Result: Numerical experiments show the proposed method has computational advantages over unstructured and Gram-matrix-based LOBPCG implementations.

Conclusion: The structure-preserving LOBPCG approach offers an efficient and stable way to compute dominant generalized singular values/vectors, leveraging the Jordan-Wielandt pencil and componentwise IHL construction.

Abstract: We develop a structure-preserving locally optimal block preconditioned conjugate gradient(LOBPCG) method for computing several of the largest positive generalized singular values of a matrix pair \((A, B)\), where \(B\) has full column rank, together with the associated generalized singular vectors. The method is derived from the Hermitian-definite Jordan--Wielandt pencil and therefore avoids explicitly forming the normal-equation matrices. By exploiting the block structure and spectral symmetry of this pencil, we construct separate search subspaces for the left and right generalized singular vectors. The resulting Rayleigh--Ritz procedure requires only a singular value decomposition of a small projected cross matrix. We adapt the improved Hetmaniuk--Lehoucq (IHL) trick to construct stable conjugate search directions in the two component spaces. We also establish an explicit correspondence between the coefficient matrices produced by the componentwise and augmented Rayleigh--Ritz procedures. This correspondence permits the componentwise IHL construction to be used in an augmented, structure-preserving LOBPCG formulation without explicitly assembling the augmented basis. Numerical experiments demonstrate the computational advantages of the proposed method over unstructured and Gram-matrix-based LOBPCG implementations.

</details>


### [15] [Spectral Reality for Certain Fourth-Order Nonsymmetric Finite-Difference Laplacians](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32410)
*Yizhe Feng,Weiguo Gao,Meiyue Shao*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Two fourth-order finite-difference schemes for the Laplace operator with Dirichlet boundary conditions produce nonsymmetric discrete Laplacians that nonetheless have purely real, strictly positive spectra—though this property fails for higher-order extensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spectral properties of discrete Laplacians govern stability, convergence, and physical fidelity of numerical solvers. Symmetric discretizations are well understood, but many high-order finite-difference schemes yield nonsymmetric matrices whose rigorous spectral analysis has been overlooked.

Method: Rigorous mathematical proof analyzing the spectra of nonsymmetric discrete Laplacians arising from two fourth-order finite-difference schemes with Dirichlet boundary conditions, supplemented by computer-assisted computations for higher-order extensions.

Result: The two fourth-order schemes yield nonsymmetric discrete Laplacians with purely real and strictly positive spectra. Computer-assisted computations show this property does not generally hold for higher-order extensions of these schemes.

Conclusion: Despite nonsymmetry, the fourth-order discrete Laplacians retain favorable spectral properties (real, positive eigenvalues), but this desirable behavior is not preserved at higher orders.

Abstract: Finite-difference discretizations of Laplace operators yield discrete Laplacians whose spectral properties are closely tied to the stability, convergence, and physical fidelity of numerical solvers. While symmetric discretizations are well understood, many high-order finite-difference schemes produce nonsymmetric matrices for which rigorous spectral analysis is overlooked. Surprisingly, we prove that two fourth-order schemes with Dirichlet boundary conditions yield nonsymmetric discrete Laplacians whose spectra are purely real and strictly positive. Computer-assisted computations show that this property does not hold for higher-order extensions of these schemes in general.

</details>


### [16] [Fast Differentiable SVD on GPU via Polar Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32505)
*Uliana Parkina,Askar Tsyganov,Sergei Kudriashov,Sergey Samsonov,Maxim Rakhuba*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A GPU-oriented SVD pipeline via polar decomposition using matrix-multiplication-only iterations (e.g., Newton-Schulz), with a stable backward pass for a fully differentiable SVD.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Iterative methods relying solely on matrix multiplications (like Newton-Schulz) are well-suited to GPUs, but standard SVD implementations are not, creating a performance mismatch.

Method: Build an SVD pipeline around polar decomposition, driven entirely by matrix multiplications; derive a numerically stable backward pass through the polar decomposition.

Result: Up to 2x speedup over standard implementations; a fully differentiable SVD; open-source PyTorch and JAX releases.

Conclusion: Matrix-multiplication-based polar decomposition offers a fast, GPU-friendly, and differentiable route to SVD.

Abstract: We present a fully GPU-oriented SVD pipeline based on polar decomposition, motivated by iterative methods that rely solely on matrix multiplications, such as the Newton-Schulz iteration. We show that this approach enables up to a $2\times$ speedup compared to standard implementations. Furthermore, we derive a numerically stable backward pass for the polar decomposition and leverage it to obtain a fully differentiable SVD. Our methods are released as open-source implementations in both PyTorch and JAX: https://github.com/fallnlove/cans_svd.

</details>


### [17] [Deep learning methods for stochastic Galerkin approximations of random domain problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32508)
*Fabio Musco,Andrea Barth*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Comparing physics-informed neural network and Deep Ritz approaches for strong and weak stochastic Galerkin approximations of elliptic PDEs on random domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Random domain problems for elliptic PDEs lead to high-dimensional coupled stochastic Galerkin systems that are computationally expensive for traditional numerical solvers.

Method: Random domain mapping transforms the problem to a deterministic reference domain. Neural networks act as surrogates for deterministic spectral coefficients of the stochastic Galerkin solution. PINN is based on the strong stochastic Galerkin residual; Deep Ritz is based on the weak stochastic Galerkin system reformulated as Ritz energy minimization. Stochastic parameters are not NN inputs, and stochastic domain is not sampled during training.

Result: Efficiency demonstrated on a randomly stretched interval and a randomly deformed annulus in two spatial dimensions.

Conclusion: Deep learning techniques offer an effective replacement for traditional numerical methods in solving high-dimensional stochastic Galerkin systems for random domain elliptic PDE problems.

Abstract: This work considers strong and weak stochastic Galerkin approximations of random domain problems for the elliptic random partial differential equation (PDE). The random domain problems are realized by sufficiently regular random domain mapping, allowing a transformation on deterministic reference domain. A traditional numerical method for solving the resulting high-dimensional coupled stochastic Galerkin systems is replaced by deep learning techniques. We compare a physics-informed neural network approach, based on the strong stochastic Galerkin residual, with a Deep Ritz approach, based on the weak stochastic Galerkin system, reformulated as Ritz energy minimization. The neural networks serve as surrogates for the deterministic spectral coefficients of the respective stochastic Galerkin solution. The stochastic parameters are not used as neural network input parameters and the stochastic domain is not sampled during training. The efficiency of the methods is demonstrated on a randomly stretched interval and a randomly deformed annulus in two spatial dimensions.

</details>


### [18] [Monte Carlo pricing under fast mean-reverting stochastic volatility: the multi-scale limit ${ε\to 0}$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32765)
*Laurent Mertz,Olivier Pironneau*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A conditional (mixing) Monte Carlo estimator with a Black–Scholes control variate efficiently prices European options under fast mean-reverting stochastic volatility, reducing variance dramatically when driven by the true volatility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct Monte Carlo estimation of $(S_T-K)^+$ for a stochastic-volatility model with a fast mean-reverting factor suffers from infinite variance, and efficient variance reduction is needed to price options and calibrate the model accurately.

Method: Use a conditional (mixing) estimator with finite variance and simulate the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck volatility factor with its exact transition. Test a martingale control variate based on the Black–Scholes delta with averaged volatility, comparing drivers by true volatility vs. constant averaged volatility. Calibrate the model with correlation to S&P 500 implied volatilities by full simulation, including a four-index basket (state dimension 8).

Result: As ε→0 the price converges at rate O(ε) to the Black–Scholes price with averaged volatility and the implied-volatility smile flattens. The control variate driven by true volatility reduces variance by a factor growing like 1/ε (≈160 at ε=10⁻³), while constant volatility gives almost no reduction. In calibration it reduces variance by 1.7–16× (median 5.6) and improves parameter accuracy; for the basket the gain is larger (median 8), making it ~4–5× more efficient than plain Monte Carlo at equal CPU time.

Conclusion: The conditional estimator plus true-volatility-driven martingale control variate is highly effective, especially in higher dimensions, and improves calibration accuracy; benefits at equal CPU time require rebalancing the delta on a coarser grid than the time step.

Abstract: We compute $\E[(S_T-K)^+]$ by Monte Carlo for a scalar stochastic-volatility model with a fast mean-reverting factor of time scale $\eps$, for $\eps$ ranging from $1$ down to $10^{-3}$. A conditional (mixing) estimator gives finite variance, whereas the direct estimator has infinite variance for this model. The volatility factor is simulated with its exact Ornstein--Uhlenbeck transition. As $\eps\to0$ the price converges, at rate $O(\eps)$, to the Black--Scholes price with the averaged volatility $\barσ$, and the implied-volatility smile flattens to $\barσ$. Finally, we test a martingale control variate built on the Black--Scholes delta with volatility $\barσ$. If the martingale is driven by the true volatility $σ(Y_t)$, the variance is reduced by a factor that grows like $1/\eps$, about $160$ at $\eps=10^{-3}$. If it is driven by the constant $\barσ$, the variance is essentially not reduced. Last, we calibrate the model with $ρ\neq0$ to S\&P~500 implied volatilities by full simulation of $S$. At the same number of paths the control variate reduces the variance by a factor $1.7$--$16$ (median $5.6$) and gives more accurate calibrated parameters; at equal CPU time it pays off only if the delta is rebalanced on a coarser grid than the time step. For a basket of four indices (state dimension $8$) the gain is larger (median $8$) and the relative cost smaller, so that the control variate is about $4$--$5$ times more efficient than plain Monte Carlo at equal CPU time.

</details>


### [19] [Tolerance Proportionality and Computational Stability in Adaptive Parallel-in-Time Runge-Kutta Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32881)
*Imre Fekete,Ferenc Izsák,Vendel P. Kupás,Gustaf Söderlind*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper studies combining adaptive time-integration with parallel-in-time methods (MGRIT) for ODEs, focusing on tolerance proportionality and showing that a simple refinement-factor adjustment improves stability and reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adaptive time-integration strategies are essential for ODE solvers, but their interaction with parallel-in-time methods—especially regarding tolerance proportionality—is not well understood. The authors aim to clarify how grid-refinement strategies within parallel-in-time frameworks affect accuracy, stability, and reliability.

Method: They analyze various grid-refinement strategies within the multigrid reduction-in-time (MGRIT) framework and propose a simple adjustment to the original refinement factor. Numerical experiments are conducted on standard test problems using the XBraid library.

Result: A simple adjustment to the original refinement factor substantially improves computational stability and reliability. Parallel-in-time solutions closely match sequential counterparts, and using multiple processors significantly reduces computing time.

Conclusion: Adaptive time-integration can be effectively combined with parallel-in-time methods, and a minor modification to the refinement factor yields notable gains in stability and reliability, while parallel execution preserves accuracy and speeds up computation.

Abstract: In this paper, we investigate how adaptive time-integration strategies can be effectively combined with parallel-in-time numerical methods for solving systems of ordinary differential equations. Our focus is particularly on their influence on tolerance proportionality. We examine various grid-refinement strategies within the multigrid reduction-in-time (MGRIT) framework. Our results show that a simple adjustment to the original refinement factor can substantially improve computational stability and reliability. Through numerical experiments on standard test problems using the XBraid library, we demonstrate that parallel-in-time solutions closely match their sequential counterparts. Moreover, with the use of multiple processors, computing time can be significantly reduced.

</details>


### [20] [Neural Network-Assisted Refinement of Traditional Schemes for One-Dimensional Scalar Conservation Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32887)
*Imre Fekete,Ferenc Izsák,Vendel P. Kupás*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper connects numerical methods and neural network approximations for 1D scalar conservation laws, building minimal-parameter networks that rediscover Godunov's method and emulate a second-order slope-limiter, which merge into improved second-order reconstruction schemes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve classical schemes for one-dimensional scalar conservation laws by constructing appropriate flux terms for convex flux functions, while reducing the complexity of prior neural-network approaches.

Method: Construct two neural networks with a minimal number of parameters: one rediscovers Godunov's method, the other emulates a second-order slope-limiter function. Merging them yields second-order reconstruction-based schemes; networks can be chained consecutively into a deep network for multiple time steps, trained with an appropriate loss.

Result: Training with an appropriate loss leads to stable schemes. The resulting schemes improve upon classical methods without increasing their complexity, and use significantly fewer parameters than previous approaches.

Conclusion: A clear link between conventional numerical methods and neural network approximations is established, enabling stable, lower-complexity schemes that can improve classical methods for conservation laws.

Abstract: A clear link is established between conventional numerical methods and neural network approximations for solving one-dimensional scalar conservation laws. The focus is on the construction of an appropriate flux term in the case of convex flux functions for improving the classical schemes. The first neural network developed here is able to rediscover Godunov's method, while the second one emulates the behavior of a second-order slope-limiter function. In this way, by merging them, second-order reconstruction-based schemes can be developed. The networks presented here employ a minimal number of parameters, significantly reducing the complexity compared to previous approaches. These networks can also be linked consecutively to get a deep one corresponding to multiple time steps. Training them with an appropriate loss leads to stable schemes, improving even the classical methods without increasing their complexity.

</details>


### [21] [Amortized Generative Modeling of Invariant Measures across A Family of Kuramoto-Sivashinsky PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32914)
*Arnab Roy,Tobin A. Driscoll*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A single conditional diffusion model can generate invariant measures across a family of chaotic dissipative PDEs, but agreement is limited by the low-dimensional support of weakly chaotic members.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Invariant measures of a parametrized chaotic PDE form a family that can vary in kind (steady states to spatiotemporal chaos). The paper asks whether one generative model can cover such a whole family.

Method: Train one conditional diffusion model on Kuramoto-Sivashinsky states across 160 hyperviscosity values; evaluate on interleaved held-out parameters via a classifier two-sample test with spectral, geometric, and tail statistics.

Result: Agreement improves as chaos increases. Weakly chaotic measures concentrate on low-dimensional sets that a full-support output cannot represent; target dimensionality grows with chaos. Restricting training to strongly chaotic members cuts classifier excess over chance ~40% and matches or beats single-parameter models, reproducing energy spectra within a resampling floor (small deficit at largest scales) and matching energy balance.

Conclusion: A single conditional diffusion model can cover most of the parameter family, with key limitation tied to low-dimensional invariant measures; focusing on strongly chaotic regime yields high-quality, competitive generation.

Abstract: The long-run statistics of chaotic dissipative partial differential equations are described by invariant measures, and when the equation carries a parameter these measures form a family whose members can differ in kind, from steady states to sustained spatiotemporal chaos. We ask whether a single generative model can cover such a family. We train a single conditional diffusion model once, on states of the Kuramoto-Sivashinsky equation at 160 values of its hyperviscosity, and evaluate it against held-out data at interleaved parameter values not seen in training, using a classifier two-sample test supported by spectral, geometric and tail statistics. Over most of the family, agreement improves as the dynamics become more chaotic. We identify a structural cause: the weakly chaotic members have invariant measures concentrated on low-dimensional sets, which a model with full support output cannot represent, and the target approaches full dimensionality as chaos increases. Restricting training to the strongly chaotic members reduces the classifier's excess over chance on those members by roughly 40%, and this restricted conditional model matches or exceeds models trained at single parameter values. On those members, generated samples reproduce energy spectra to within a resampling floor, with a small systematic deficit at the largest scales, and match the energy balance of held-out data.

</details>


### [22] [Finite Element Analysis of Maxwell's Equations with Time-Varying Coefficients and Nonlinear E-H Coupling: Convergence and Exponential Decay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32967)
*Jan Renner,Irwin Yousept*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proves uniform convergence and exponential stability for fully discrete finite element approximations of Maxwell's equations with time-dependent coefficients and nonlinear E-H coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlinear coupling and time-varying material parameters block standard analytical tools (discrete compactness, Minty-Browder), making numerical analysis hard.

Method: Uses a Cauchy-type argument in a time-dependent Hilbert space to avoid Minty's trick and discrete compactness; analyzes exponential stability via a nonlinear sup-max problem on consecutive discrete energy ratios.

Result: Obtains uniform convergence on every finite interval [0,T]; proves fully discrete unconditional exponential decay under a sufficient condition, and gives a counterexample showing decay fails otherwise. Numerical experiments validate the theory.

Conclusion: The framework delivers convergence and stability results beyond the reach of standard techniques for nonlinear, time-dependent Maxwell problems.

Abstract: This paper analyzes finite element approximations for Maxwell's equations with time-dependent coefficients and nonlinear E-H coupling in both the total current density and the Silver-Müller-type boundary condition. The nonlinear coupling and the time-varying material parameters prevent the use of standard analytical tools, including the discrete compactness property and the Minty-Browder argument, making the numerical analysis particularly challenging. As the main novelty, we establish two primary contributions: Uniform convergence on every finite time interval $[0,T]$ and exponential stability on the infinite time horizon $[0,\infty)$. The uniform convergence is established via a Cauchy-type argument within a time-dependent Hilbert space framework that entirely bypasses Minty's trick and the discrete compactness argument. Furthermore, under a positive homogeneity assumption on the nonlinearities, the exponential stability is analyzed by means of a nonlinear sup-max problem arising from ratios of consecutive discrete energies. With this strategy, we ultimately prove a fully discrete unconditional exponential decay result under a specific sufficient condition. In the absence of this condition, a concrete counterexample shows that the energy decay fails to hold. Numerical experiments are provided to validate the theoretical findings.

</details>


### [23] [A Spectral Model-Informed Neural Network for Inverse Source Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33062)
*Dinh-Liem Nguyen,Nhung H. Nguyen,Aravinth Ravi*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: An unsupervised two-step model-informed deep learning framework solves the inverse source problem by transforming boundary data into an imaging function, deriving a Fourier-based model equation, then embedding it into a model-informed neural network for fast, accurate, noise-robust source reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving the inverse source problem typically requires reconstructing source functions from boundary measurements, which is challenging. Traditional methods like least-squares can be computationally expensive and less efficient, motivating a fast, accurate, and noise-robust approach.

Method: Two-step unsupervised model-informed deep learning. Step 1: transform boundary measurement data into an imaging function encoding source shape/location, then derive a Fourier-based model equation in the spectral domain relating the imaging function to the source's Fourier coefficients. Step 2: incorporate this model equation into training a model-informed neural network to recover parameters of interest.

Result: Numerical experiments in 2D and 3D demonstrate effectiveness. In 2D, comparison with the traditional least-squares method validates computational efficiency and reconstruction accuracy.

Conclusion: The proposed framework enables fast and accurate source function reconstruction while maintaining robustness to noise, showing promise for inverse source problems.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose an unsupervised, two-step model-informed deep learning framework for solving the inverse source problem. In the first step, boundary measurement data are transformed into an imaging function that encodes information about the shape and location of the unknown source. Leveraging this information, we derive a Fourier-based model equation in the spectral domain that relates the imaging function to the Fourier coefficients of the source function. In the second step, this model equation is incorporated into the training of a model-informed neural network to recover the parameters of interest. The proposed approach enables fast and accurate reconstruction of the source function while maintaining robustness to noise. The effectiveness of the method is demonstrated through numerical experiments in both two- and three-dimensional settings. For the two-dimensional case, we further compare our approach with the traditional least-squares method to validate its computational efficiency and reconstruction accuracy.

</details>


### [24] [A Model-Informed Deep Learning Algorithm for Solving the Phaseless Inverse Scattering Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33070)
*Dinh-Liem Nguyen,Nhung H. Nguyen,Aravinth Ravi*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: An unsupervised two-step model-informed deep learning framework solves the phaseless inverse scattering problem by combining a physics-based Lippmann-Schwinger equation with a spectral imaging equation, using two jointly trained neural networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Phaseless inverse scattering is challenging because only the modulus of the total wave is measured (phase information is lost), and multiple incident waves add complexity. A method is needed that is accurate, efficient, robust to noise, and avoids reliance on labeled data.

Method: A two-step unsupervised approach. Step 1 transforms phaseless data into an imaging function related to the scatterer and derives a coupled system of model equations: the Lippmann-Schwinger integral equation (enforcing physics) plus a spectral-based equation (encoding shape/location). Step 2 parameterizes the scatterer and contrast source each as independent feedforward neural networks, trained simultaneously per iteration using the derived model equations.

Result: The framework achieves accurate and efficient reconstructions with robustness to measurement noise. Transforming parts of the problem to the spectral domain reduces computational cost, enabling fast recovery of the unknown scatterer, as demonstrated in 2D and 3D numerical experiments.

Conclusion: The proposed model-informed deep learning method effectively and stably solves the phaseless inverse scattering problem, offering practical potential for fast, noise-robust reconstruction.

Abstract: We study in this paper an unsupervised, two-step, model-informed deep learning framework for solving the phaseless inverse scattering problem. The objective is to reconstruct a compactly supported function that characterizes a scatterer from boundary measurements of the modulus of the total wave corresponding to multiple incident waves. In the first step, the phaseless data are transformed into an imaging function that is directly related to the underlying scatterer. Motivated by the contrast source method, we derive a coupled system of model equations using the Lippmann-Schwinger integral equation and a spectral-based equation involving the imaging function. In the second step, the unknown scatterer and contrast source function are each parameterized as independent feedforward neural networks, which are trained simultaneously at each iteration using the derived system of model equations. The Lippmann-Schwinger integral equation enforces the underlying physics as a model constraint, while the spectral-based equation incorporating the imaging function supplies geometric information on the shape and location of the unknown scatterer. The resulting framework achieves accurate and efficient reconstructions while maintaining robustness to measurement noise. Furthermore, transforming some aspects of the problem to the spectral domain enables an effective reduction in the computational cost, allowing us to recover the unknown scatterer quickly. Numerical experiments in both two- and three-dimensional settings demonstrate the effectiveness, stability, and practical potential of the proposed approach.

</details>


### [25] [The stability barrier of a class of Hermite-type discretizations of advection equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33201)
*Xianyi Zeng*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper fully categorizes hybrid-variable (HV) discretizations of linear advection equations by stability, establishing a stability barrier analogous to classical finite difference schemes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and characterize the stability properties of hybrid-variable discretization methods for linear advection equations, which evolve cell-averaged and nodal solutions simultaneously.

Method: Categorize all HV discretizations of linear advection equations by their stability property and derive a stability barrier condition based on the stencil's upwind vs. downwind unknown counts.

Result: The semi-discretized HV scheme is stable if and only if the upwind-direction unknowns equal the downwind-direction unknowns plus one or two, except when the counts are three and zero; all central HV schemes are neutrally stable with uniformly bounded L2-norm.

Conclusion: HV methods share a similar stability barrier to classical finite difference schemes, and theoretical predictions are confirmed by extensive numerical tests.

Abstract: In this paper we fully categorize all hybrid-variable (HV) discretizations of linear advection equations according to their stability property and establish the stability barrier of these methods. In the HV discretization framework, we find numerical approximations to both cell-averaged solutions and nodal solutions and evolve them in time simultaneously. We prove that the HV methods have a similar stability barrier as the classical finite difference schemes, that the semi-discretized HV scheme is stable if and only if it uses a stencil such that the number of unknowns in the upwind direction is precisely that in the downwind direction plus one or two, with only one exception when the two numbers are three and zero, respectively. It is also proved that all central HV schemes are neutrally stable, in the sense that the L2-norm of the solution remain uniformly bounded at all times. The theoretical predictions are verify by extensive numerical tests.

</details>


### [26] [Finance-Informed Operator Learning for Option Pricing with Quantum-Compatible Realizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33213)
*Jiarui Feng,Bingyang Hu,Jiang Yu,Changhong Mou,Yeyu Zhang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: FI-DeepONet prices European options under local volatility by learning only a correction on top of a Black-Scholes carrier with strike-integrated variance, and enforces no-arbitrage bounds via a monotone admissibility layer; it cuts in-distribution price error by ~10x over DeepONet baselines and supports sensitivities and a quantum-compatible realization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Repeatedly solving the local-volatility PDE is costly, and neural surrogates struggle near expiry where solutions lose regularity, causing poor curvature learning and no-arbitrage violations. Practitioners also need both prices and sensitivities across spot-time surfaces.

Method: A finance-informed DeepONet decomposes the pricing operator into an input-dependent Black-Scholes carrier plus a learned correction. The carrier uses strike-line integrated variance to capture the leading near-expiry curvature singularity; a smooth monotone Financial Admissibility Layer enforces pointwise price bounds. The authors derive a short-maturity estimate, differentiated asymptotics for the exact correction, and error identities linking correction error to price, sensitivity, and PDE-residual errors. A quantum-compatible variant compiles or approximates selected linear maps via a restricted diagonal-orthogonal family before supervised adaptation.

Result: In-distribution, global relative price error drops nearly an order of magnitude versus vanilla and physics-informed DeepONet baselines. On parameter-shift OOD tests within the same local-volatility family, generalization is moderate. On index-option data, the frozen model stays accurate without market-specific retraining but does not beat the same-input analytic formula.

Conclusion: Embedding financial structure (carrier decomposition plus admissibility constraints) yields a more accurate and regularity-aware operator surrogate for local-volatility option pricing, with sensitivities and a quantum-compatible realization, though OOD generalization and out-performance of analytic formulas remain limited.

Abstract: Pricing European options under local volatility requires repeatedly solving a PDE whose coefficients change with recalibration, while practitioners need both prices and sensitivities across spot-time surfaces. Neural surrogates can amortize these solves, but near expiry the solution loses regularity, making curvature difficult to learn and allowing violations of no-arbitrage bounds. We propose a finance-informed Deep Operator Network (FI-DeepONet) that decomposes the pricing operator into an input-dependent Black-Scholes carrier and a learned correction. The carrier uses strike-line integrated variance, capturing the leading near-expiry curvature singularity, while a smooth monotone Financial Admissibility Layer enforces pointwise price bounds. We derive a short-maturity estimate and differentiated asymptotics for the exact correction, together with identities linking correction error to price, sensitivity, and PDE-residual errors. On in-distribution tests, FI-DeepONet reduces global relative price error by nearly an order of magnitude versus vanilla and physics-informed DeepONet baselines. On parameter-shift OOD tests within the same local-volatility family, generalization is moderate. On index-option data, the frozen model remains accurate without market-specific retraining, though it does not outperform the same-input analytic formula. We also give a quantum-compatible realization in which selected linear maps are exactly compiled or approximated by a restricted diagonal-orthogonal family before supervised adaptation.

</details>


### [27] [Hierarchical low-rank compression exploiting symmetric boundary integral equations in transmission scattering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33216)
*Yasuhiro Matsumoto,Taizo Maruyama,Qianxiang Ma,Rio Yokota*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A matrix-symmetry approach accelerates hierarchical low-rank compression for Helmholtz and elastic wave transmission scattering by halving proxy low-rank work and enabling faster direct solvers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct solvers for wave transmission scattering are expensive; exploiting matrix symmetry can reduce the cost of hierarchical low-rank compression, but existing formulations often lack usable symmetry.

Method: Introduces a symmetric Müller-like boundary integral formulation for Helmholtz transmission (not second kind but direct-solver friendly), discretized via a Nyström scheme with one-point corrected quadrature preserving complex symmetry, integrated into a hierarchical semiseparable direct solver that halves proxy low-rank cost via left/right symmetry; extends to elastic problems via a symmetric Galerkin BEM.

Result: On a multi-node cluster, the symmetric Müller-like Helmholtz formulation gives an average 1.41x speedup over the non-symmetric baseline while maintaining third-order convergence; the symmetry-aware elastic solver is over 1.5x faster.

Conclusion: Exploiting matrix symmetry in hierarchical low-rank direct solvers yields substantial speedups for both Helmholtz and elastic transmission scattering while preserving accuracy, offering an effective acceleration strategy.

Abstract: We propose an approach using matrix symmetry to accelerate hierarchical low-rank compression for wave transmission scattering. For Helmholtz transmission problems, we introduce a symmetric Müller-like boundary integral equation formulation. Although not of the second kind, this formulation creates no difficulties for direct solvers. A one-point corrected quadrature in a Nyström discretization preserves the complex symmetry of the discretized system. This formulation is integrated into a variant of a hierarchical semiseparable direct solver. Within this framework, the computational workload of the low-rank approximation in the proxy method is halved by exploiting the symmetric relationship between the left and right coefficients.
