Furthermore, we extend this symmetry-exploiting approach to elastic transmission problems by using a symmetric Galerkin boundary element method. Numerical experiments on a multi-node computing cluster demonstrate that the symmetric Müller-like formulation for Helmholtz scattering achieves on average a 1.41-fold speedup compared to the non-symmetric baseline while maintaining third-order convergence. Furthermore, the symmetry-aware solver for elastic scattering is more than 1.5 times faster.

</details>


### [28] [Some new numerical schemes for delay sub-diffusion equations via the idea of symmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33406)
*Dakang Cen,Caixia Ou,Seakweng Vong,Zhibo Wang*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes novel numerical schemes combining the L1 method with the symmetric fractional-order reduction (SFOR) method for time-delay fractional sub-diffusion equations, achieving an improved temporal convergence order of 2 − α/2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fractional sub-diffusion equations with time delay are challenging to solve numerically. Classical L1-type schemes achieve only 2 − α temporal accuracy under standard smoothness conditions, motivating the need for higher-order, more efficient schemes.

Method: Synergistic integration of the L1 method and the symmetric fractional-order reduction (SFOR) method. A weighted alternating direction implicit (ADI) scheme is developed for the 2D case, coupled with compact spatial discretization. A new error analysis framework for L1-type discretization on graded temporal meshes is introduced.

Result: Established optimal temporal convergence order of 2 − α/2, improving on the classical 2 − α under identical smoothness conditions. High-order spatial accuracy achieved via compact ADI coupling. Numerical experiments validate theoretical accuracy and efficiency.

Conclusion: The proposed schemes deliver improved accuracy and computational efficiency for time-delay fractional sub-diffusion equations, with a relaxed mesh grading requirement that still guarantees optimal convergence at continuous time points.

Abstract: In this work, we propose several novel numerical schemes that synergistically integrates the $L1$ method and symmetric fractional-order reduction (SFOR) method, specifically designed for fractional sub-diffusion equations with time delay. Crucially, we rigorously establish an optimal convergence order of $2 - α/2$ in temporal direction, which improves upon the classical rate of $2-α$ under identical smoothness conditions. To further enhance computational efficiency, we develop a weighted alternating direction implicit (ADI) scheme for the two-dimensional case and analyze its coupling with compact spatial discretization---thereby achieving high-order accuracy in space. Moreover, we introduce a new framework for analyzing the error of $L1$-type discretization on graded temporal meshes, which relaxes the mesh grading requirement and still guarantees optimal convergence rate at continuous time points. Some numerical experiments comprehensively validate both the theoretical accuracy and computational efficiency of the resulting schemes.

</details>


### [29] [Optimal Transport Bounds for Latent Density Smoothing on MTW($K>0$) Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33629)
*Wonjun Lee,Wenyan Luo*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the tradeoff of smoothing an empirical distribution through Wasserstein error bounds for intrinsic heat smoothing, ambient Gaussian smoothing, and encoder-decoder latent space smoothing, and derives bandwidth-selection rules favoring latent smoothing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Smoothing an empirical distribution can fill gaps between observations, but noise added in the surrounding space also moves probability mass away from the manifold supporting the data. The paper aims to study this tradeoff formally.

Method: Wasserstein error bounds for intrinsic heat smoothing, ambient Gaussian smoothing, and latent space smoothing. Assumptions include the Ma-Trudinger-Wang condition with positive cross-curvature and transport-regularity assumptions. A quadratic approximation to the bound yields bandwidth-selection rules.

Result: Sufficiently small intrinsic smoothing improves upon the unsmoothed empirical measure. The ambient bound quantifies how increasing surrounding dimension restricts the smoothing scale. For latent smoothing, benefit depends on reconstruction accuracy and how the encoder-decoder preserves movement along the manifold and responds to noise in other directions.

Conclusion: The quadratic approximation yields bandwidth-selection rules and conditions favoring latent smoothing. Controlled synthetic experiments confirm predicted bandwidth and geometric trends, and an exploratory MNIST study illustrates latent smoothing relative to pixel-space perturbations.

Abstract: Smoothing an empirical distribution can fill gaps between observations, but noise added in the surrounding space also moves probability mass away from the manifold supporting the data. We study this tradeoff through Wasserstein error bounds for intrinsic heat smoothing, ambient Gaussian smoothing, and smoothing in an encoder--decoder's latent space. Under the Ma--Trudinger--Wang condition with positive cross-curvature and the stated transport-regularity assumptions, we prove that sufficiently small intrinsic smoothing improves upon the unsmoothed empirical measure. Our ambient bound quantifies how increasing the surrounding dimension restricts the smoothing scale suggested by the bound. For latent smoothing, the analysis shows how the benefit depends on reconstruction accuracy and on how the encoder--decoder pair preserves movement along the manifold and responds to noise in other directions. A quadratic approximation to the bound yields bandwidth-selection rules and conditions favoring latent smoothing in this comparison. Controlled synthetic experiments examine the predicted bandwidth and geometric trends. An exploratory MNIST study illustrates latent smoothing relative to pixel-space perturbations.

</details>


### [30] [Mathematical modeling, simulation and optimization via (port-Hamiltonian) differential-algebraic equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33954)
*Volker Mehrmann*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This survey addresses modeling with differential-algebraic equations (DAEs), a ubiquitous model class in simulation, control, and optimization of complex systems like digital twins. It highlights numerical challenges—especially coupling systems across physical domains with differing scales or regularity—and proposes the port-Hamiltonian DAE (pHDAE) framework combined with space-time-model adaptive methods to overcome them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DAEs combined with space/time discretization are work-horses in engineering, but they pose challenges for numerical methods, particularly when coupling systems from different physical domains with different scales or regularity requirements.

Method: A survey of DAE-based modeling using the port-Hamiltonian differential-algebraic systems (pHDAEs) framework, along with space-time-model adaptive simulation and optimization methods.

Result: The paper surveys DAE modeling and presents pHDAEs and adaptive methods as a means to overcome coupling and numerical challenges in multisystem settings.

Conclusion: The pHDAE framework together with space-time-model adaptive methods offers a viable path to addressing key numerical challenges in DAE-based modeling, simulation, and optimization of complex multiphysics systems.

Abstract: Differential-algebraic equations (DAEs) present a ubiquitous model class in the automated modeling, simulation, control and optimization of complex finite or infinite dimensional dynamical systems, in particular in the construction of digital twins. When combined with adequate space and time discretization methods they present the work-horse of developments in many engineering domains. However, this approach comes with certain challenges for the numerical methods and in particular in the coupling of systems from different physical domains with different scales or regularity requirements. We survey the modeling with DAEs and address how to overcome some of the challenges via the framework of port-Hamiltonian differential-algebraic systems (pHDAEs) and their simulation and optimization with space-time-model adaptive methods.

</details>


### [31] [Immersogeometric fluid-structure interaction modeling of the human mitral valve and transcatheter edge-to-edge repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34209)
*Keon Ho Kim,Ashton M. Corpuz,Monu Jaiswal,Michael S. Sacks,Ming-Chen Hsu*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper presents an immersogeometric FSI framework to simulate transcatheter edge-to-edge repair (TEER) of the mitral valve, capturing leaflet-chordae deformation and contact without conforming meshes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Predicting TEER outcome requires resolving blood flow interaction with the full mitral valve apparatus, where highly deformable leaflets and chordae undergo large deformation and complex contact. Conforming discretizations are difficult for such moving, contacting components.

Method: An immersogeometric FSI formulation weakly couples valve components, flow, and repair without conforming discretizations. A parameterized reconstruction pipeline converts segmented images to isogeometric meshes. Leaflets and chordae use Kirchhoff-Love shells and cables with an in-vivo prestrain tensor. A subcell quadrature approach for the interfacial constraint eliminates over-refinement and unphysical leakage. Repair is modeled via a penalty enforcing coaptation over a prescribed region (clip number/placement variable). A lumped-parameter outflow model imposes flow-dependent pressure.

Result: Reconstruction validated against segmented imaging data; mesh convergence demonstrated for the proposed formulations; framework applied to a prolapsed MV with single- and double-clip repairs, quantifying pre- and post-operative changes in valve dynamics and left-heart hemodynamics.

Conclusion: The immersogeometric FSI framework enables realistic, remesh-free simulation of TEER, providing a tool to quantify repair effects on valve dynamics and hemodynamics.

Abstract: Transcatheter edge-to-edge repair (TEER) treats mitral regurgitation with a clip that grasps and coapts the mitral valve (MV) leaflets. Predicting its outcome requires resolving the interaction between the blood flow and the complete MV apparatus, in which highly deformable leaflets and chordae tendineae undergo large deformation and complex contact. In this work, we introduce an immersogeometric fluid-structure interaction (FSI) formulation in which the coupling among the valvular components, the surrounding flow, and the repair is enforced weakly, without requiring conforming discretizations. A fully parameterized reconstruction pipeline is developed to convert segmented images into analysis-ready isogeometric meshes. The leaflets and chordae are modeled as Kirchhoff-Love shells and cables, and the shell formulation is modified to include an in-vivo prestrain tensor that accounts for the loading already present in the imaged geometry. We propose a subcell approach that improves the quadrature for the interfacial constraint and eliminates the over-refinement otherwise needed to prevent unphysical leakage through the leaflets. The MV repair is modeled by a penalty formulation that enforces leaflet coaptation over a prescribed region, allowing clip number and placement to be varied without remeshing or modeling the device. A lumped-parameter model at the outflow tract of the left heart imposes a flow-dependent pressure rather than a prescribed waveform, so that the split between forward and regurgitant flow matches physiological behavior. We validate the reconstruction against the segmented imaging data, demonstrate mesh convergence of the proposed formulations, and apply the framework to a prolapsed MV and its single- and double-clip repairs to quantify pre- and post-operative changes in valve dynamics and left-heart hemodynamics.

</details>


### [32] [The paxotopy method for finding isolated roots of a system of nonlinear equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34583)
*Sándor Bozóki*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Paxotopy is a general framework for finding isolated roots of nonlinear systems containing non-polynomial (transcendental) equations, combining polynomial approximation, homotopy continuation, and Newton refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is no general framework for solving nonlinear systems with non-polynomial equations, despite major advances in polynomial system solving and only ad-hoc extensions to specific transcendental cases.

Method: Paxotopy: (1) polynomial approximation of transcendental functions, (2) solving the approximating polynomial system via homotopy continuation, (3) using those polynomial roots as starting points for Newton's iteration to reach solutions of the original system.

Result: The paper proposes the paxotopy framework for finding isolated roots, combining local and global methods. (Abstract reports no numerical results.)

Conclusion: Paxotopy unifies global homotopy-based polynomial solving with local Newton refinement, offering a general approach to non-polynomial nonlinear systems.

Abstract: We focus on systems of nonlinear equations, where some equations are non-polynomial. Building on the remarkable developments in solving polynomial systems during the recent decades, and on a few extensions to specific examples of the transcendental case, a general framework for finding isolated roots is proposed. The name paxotopy is proposed after (1) Polynomial ApproXimation of the transcendental functions, then (2) solving the approximating polynomial system by the homOTOPY continuation method, and finally (3) the roots of the polynomial systems are used as starting points of a Newton's iteration to arrive at the solutions of the original system. These steps combine the advantages of local and global methods.

</details>


### [33] [Spectral analysis of a class of IRK stage preconditioners for linear wave equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34620)
*Michal Outrata,Aman Rani*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the "Kronecker preconditioners" previously used for linear wave equations, providing the theoretical justification for claims made in a prior work [24, Section 4.2] and elaborating the numerical background behind observed phenomena.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The preconditioners adopted in [24] for linear wave equations were claimed to be effective, but lacked a rigorous theoretical justification. This paper supplies the missing analysis and necessary numerical background.

Method: The authors carry out an analysis of the Kronecker preconditioners, unpacking the relevant numerical background to fully explain the observed phenomena.

Result: The analysis justifies the claims made in [24, Section 4.2] about the Kronecker preconditioners.

Conclusion: The paper confirms and rigorously supports the previously observed behavior of Kronecker preconditioners for linear wave equations.

Abstract: We lay out the analysis of the ``Kronecker preconditioners'' adopted in [24] for the linear wave equations. Importantly, we deliver the analysis justifying the claims in~[24, Section 4.2] about these preconditioners and we unpack all of the necessary numerical background for a full understanding of the observed phenomena.

</details>


### [34] [A weighted polygonal discontinuous Galerkin method for hierarchically coupled reaction-diffusion systems with cubic interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34625)
*Mattia Corti*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper develops and analyzes a symmetric weighted interior penalty polytopal discontinuous Galerkin (SWIP-PolyDG) method for multi-species reaction-diffusion systems with nonlinear reaction terms up to cubic order, establishing stability and error estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Higher-order interactions in population models produce nonlinear reaction terms (up to cubic order) with non-additive effects among multiple species, requiring a flexible numerical framework that handles heterogeneous/anisotropic diffusion on general polygonal meshes.

Method: A SWIP-PolyDG method on general polygonal meshes supporting heterogeneous and anisotropic diffusion tensors. Nonlinear coupling is controlled via a hierarchical block structure in the reaction operator, where each population block depends only on its own and preceding blocks' variables, and cubic self-interactions are assumed dissipative diagonal. Stability and error estimates are derived recursively over the hierarchy.

Result: Local-in-time stability estimates in 2D for the semi-discrete formulation in both L2 and dG norms, plus an a priori error estimate in a combined L2-dG energy norm for sufficiently regular solutions. Numerical experiments confirm predicted convergence and robustness under heterogeneous diffusion.

Conclusion: The SWIP-PolyDG framework provides a stable and convergent discretization for multi-species reaction-diffusion systems with cubic nonlinearities on polygonal meshes, with theoretical guarantees validated numerically.

Abstract: We develop and analyse a symmetric weighted interior penalty polytopal discontinuous Galerkin (SWIP-PolyDG) method for multi-species reaction--diffusion systems with nonlinear reaction terms of up to cubic order. Such terms arise naturally when higher-order interactions are incorporated into population models, allowing non-additive effects among multiple species to influence local growth and conversion mechanisms. The proposed framework accommodates heterogeneous and possibly anisotropic diffusion tensors on general polygonal meshes. To control the nonlinear coupling, we consider a hierarchical block structure in the reaction operator, whereby each population block depends only on its own variables and on those associated with preceding blocks. In addition, the cubic self-interactions within each block are assumed to have a dissipative diagonal structure. Under these hypotheses and proceeding recursively over the hierarchy of population blocks, we derive local-in-time stability estimates in two spatial dimensions for the semi-discrete formulation in both the $L^2(Ω)$-and dG-norms. We further establish an a priori error estimate in a combined $L^2(Ω)$-dG energy norm for sufficiently regular solutions for the semi-discrete formulation. Finally, numerical experiments confirm the predicted convergence behaviour and illustrate the robustness of the method under heterogeneous diffusion.

</details>


### [35] [The Numerical Onsager-Machlup action functional for Euler discretized SDEs driven by fractional Brownian motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34626)
*Shanqi Liu,Qiqi Lian,Jinqiao Duan,Hongjun Gao*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Derives an explicit numerical Onsager-Machlup action functional for Euler-discretized SDEs driven by fractional Brownian motion, obtains the corresponding numerical fractional Euler-Lagrange equations, and validates them with numerical experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous works lack an explicit Onsager-Machlup action functional for numerically discretized SDEs driven by fractional Brownian motion, leaving a gap between continuous and numerical analysis of most probable paths.

Method: Under suitable conditions on the drift coefficient and numerical reference path, the authors derive an explicit expression for the numerical Onsager-Machlup action functional of Euler-discretized SDEs driven by fractional Brownian motion, then derive the numerical fractional Euler-Lagrange equations from it.

Result: Explicit numerical Onsager-Machlup action functional and numerical fractional Euler-Lagrange equations are obtained for Euler-discretized fractional-Brownian-motion SDEs, supported by numerical experiments.

Conclusion: The work extends Onsager-Machlup theory to the numerical (Euler discretization) setting for fractional Brownian motion SDEs, providing both theoretical expressions and experimental verification.

Abstract: In this work, different from previous results, the explicit expression of numerical Onsager-Machlup action functional for Euler discretized SDEs driven by fractional Brownian motion is derived provided the drift coefficient and numerical reference path satisfy some suitable conditions. Then numerical fractional Euler-Lagrange equations for numerical Onsager-Machlup action functional are also obtained. Finally, numerical experiments to illustrate and support the theoretical findings.

</details>


### [36] [Fully Discrete Active Flux Methods for the Euler Equations: Exploring Different Reconstructions and New Multi-Dimensional Evolution Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34669)
*Christiane Helzel,Mária Lukáčová-Medvid'ová,Amelie Porfetye,Zhuyan Tang*

TL;DR: New fully discrete Cartesian grid Active Flux methods for 2D Euler equations, featuring third-order multidimensional evolution operators derived via bicharacteristics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Active Flux methods need an effective evolution operator for point-value degrees of freedom; existing operators are insufficient in multidimensional settings.

Method: Derive several new third-order accurate multidimensional evolution operators for point-value updates of linearized Euler equations using the method of bicharacteristics; investigate reconstruction and approximate evolution operator choice effects on accuracy and stability.

Result: Several new third-order accurate multidimensional evolution operators are derived, and the impact of reconstruction and evolution operator choice on accuracy/stability is analyzed.

Conclusion: The choice of reconstruction and approximate evolution operator critically affects accuracy and stability of the resulting Active Flux methods.

Abstract: We present new fully discrete Cartesian grid Active Flux methods for the two-dimensional Euler equations of gas dynamics. A key component of these methods is the evolution operator used to evolve point-value degrees of freedom. Therefore, we derive several new, third-order accurate, multidimensional evolution operators for point-value updates of the linearized Euler equations using the method of bicharacteristics. We also investigate how the reconstruction and choice of approximate evolution operator affect the accuracy and stability of the resulting methods.

</details>


### [37] [Addressing Data Engineering Challenges in the Cold-Chain Sector to Reduce its Environmental Impact Through Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34762)
*Mathilde Marcy,Camille Fertel,Thomas Suquet,G{é}rald Cavalier*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper argues that conventional data-science approaches are poorly suited to the cold-chain industry and proposes an alternative, system-oriented data-ecosystem approach to enable sustainable analytics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cold-chain organizations see data analytics as a path to sustainability and reduced environmental footprint, but struggle with data quality and streamlined full-scale ecosystems. Existing data techniques and tools come from the general data-science community and are not suited to cold-chain specificities—especially because almost all data-quality frameworks consider entities individually, while reducing environmental impact in industrial cold-chain settings requires modelling entire systems of interdependent entities.

Method: Conceptual critique and proposal: the paper challenges the application of conventional data-science and engineering concepts in cold-chain data ecosystems and outlines an alternative approach aimed at addressing prevalent challenges and optimizing analytical processes sustainably.

Result: As a position/conceptual paper, it identifies the limitation of entity-centric data-quality frameworks and offers an alternative system-level approach rather than reporting empirical results.

Conclusion: Adapting data ecosystems to cold-chain domain specificities—modeling interdependent systems rather than isolated entities—is necessary to enable sustainable analytics and environmental impact reduction.

Abstract: Data analytics offer a great opportunity for organizations within the cold-chain industry to become more sustainable and reduce their environmental footprint. Yet, despite recent progress in data sciences, they still face challenges to ensure data quality and streamline data processes through adapted full-scale ecosystems, limiting their ability to perform sustainable analytics.  Most data techniques and tools stem from the data-science community and are not always suitably adapted to domain specificities. For instance, nearly all data-quality frameworks are designed to individually consider entities, i.e. representations of real-world elements. However, achieving environmental impact reduction in industrial settings, particularly within the cold-chain sector, often requires modelling and studying entire systems comprised of multiple interdependent entities. This publication challenges the application of conventional data science and engineering concepts in data ecosystems within the cold-chain sector and proposes an alternative approach to sustainably address prevalent challenges and thus optimize analytical processes.

</details>


### [38] [A priori error analysis for one-parameter Lagrange and POD reduced order methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34865)
*Antti Hannukainen,Vigdis Toresen*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes reduced order models (POD and Lagrange subspace methods) for parametric linear elliptic PDEs, proving error estimates as a function of the snapshot set, achieving sub-exponential and exponential convergence under mild and structured assumptions respectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Parametric linear elliptic PDEs require efficient reduced order models (ROMs) to handle parameter dependency, but existing error analysis of Lagrange subspace methods relies on interpolation or perturbation arguments that are limiting. A cleaner error analysis based on the snapshot point set is needed.

Method: The authors use the finite element method for spatial discretization, combined with a proper orthogonal decomposition (POD) or Lagrange subspace method for the ROM. They study a simple one-parameter model problem and introduce a new approximation operator defined from the snapshot subspace, for which they prove error and stability estimates.

Result: Sub-exponential convergence with respect to the number of snapshot points is established under very mild assumptions on the point set, and exponential convergence is proven for structured point sets. These results hold for both POD and Lagrange subspace ROMs.

Conclusion: The proposed error analysis avoids interpolation or perturbation arguments, offering a more general framework for ROM error estimates. The new approximation operator enables rigorous convergence results tied directly to the snapshot point set.

Abstract: This work deals with the numerical solution of parametric linear elliptic partial differential equations using the finite element method for spatial discretization and a proper orthogonal decomposition or Lagrange subspace method based ROM for treating the parameter dependency. We study a simple one-parameter model problem and prove an error estimate for these ROMs as a function of the snapshot point set. Sub-exponential convergence with respect to the number of snapshot points is established under very mild assumptions on the point set. Exponential convergence is proven for structured point sets. In contrast to existing error analysis of Lagrange subspace methods, our estimates are not based on interpolation or perturbation arguments. Rather, we define a new approximation operator from the snapshot subspace and prove error and stability estimates for it.

</details>


### [39] [Refined Gauss--Lobatto bounds for odd-order convexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34918)
*Tomasz Kania,Szymon Wąsowicz*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: For (2n-1)-convex functions, the integral lies between the n-point Gauss-Legendre rule and its midpoint with the (n+1)-point Gauss-Lobatto rule, giving a sharp certified estimator Q_n = 3/4 G_n + 1/4 L_{n+1} with error bound 1/4 |L_{n+1}-G_n|. An even-order obstruction is also characterized, and numerical certification is distinguished from high-order approximation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical results bracket the integral of higher-order convex functions between Gaussian (lower) and Gauss-Lobatto (upper) quadrature, but this bracket is loose. The paper tightens this bracket in odd orders and clarifies a complementary even-order limitation, aiming for certified estimators that remain meaningful when smoothness-based error constants fail or are pessimistic.

Method: Use higher-order convexity (divided differences/peano kernel analysis) to show that for (2n-1)-convex f, I[f] lies between G_n[f] and the midpoint (G_n+L_{n+1})/2. Construct the convex combination Q_n = 3/4 G_n + 1/4 L_{n+1} and prove the sharp error bound 1/4|L_{n+1}-G_n|. Analyze even order via Gauss-Radau endpoint rules and an odd, sign-changing Radau quadrature kernel to explain the obstruction.

Result: Sharp estimator Q_n[f] = 3/4 G_n[f] + 1/4 L_{n+1}[f] with |I[f]-Q_n[f]| <= 1/4|L_{n+1}[f]-G_n[f]|, constant 1/4 sharp. In even order, Gauss-Radau endpoint rules still bracket the integral, but the midpoint of the two Radau rules cannot give one-sided refinement (obstruction from odd, sign-changing kernel). Experiments on truncated powers and near-pole Stieltjes kernels show the shape certificate remains useful where smoothness-based constants are unavailable or pessimistic.

Conclusion: Higher-order convexity yields a sharp, certified quadrature estimator that refines classical Gaussian bounds, with a fundamental even-order limitation. This shape-based certification differs from ordinary high-order approximation and is effective in spline, moment, and statistical settings, especially for truncated powers and near-pole Stieltjes kernels.

Abstract: For functions that are convex of higher order, classical extremalities identify Gaussian quadrature as a lower bound and Gauss--Lobatto quadrature as an upper bound for the integral. We sharpen this bracket in every odd order: if $f$ is $(2n-1)$-convex on $[-1,1]$, then the exact integral $I[f]:=\int_{-1}^{1}f(x)\,dx$ lies not merely between the $n$-point Gauss--Legendre rule $G_n[f]$ and the $(n+1)$-point Gauss--Lobatto rule $L_{n+1}[f]$, but already between $G_n[f]$ and their midpoint. This yields the certified estimator $Q_n[f]:=\frac{3}{4}G_n[f]+\frac{1}{4}L_{n+1}[f]$, with $|I[f]-Q_n[f]|\leqslant\frac{1}{4}|L_{n+1}[f]-G_n[f]|$, and the constant $\frac{1}{4}$ is sharp. We also record a complementary phenomenon in even order: for $2n$-convex functions the Gauss--Radau endpoint rules still bracket the integral, but the midpoint of the two Radau rules cannot yield a one-sided refinement. The obstruction is explained by an odd, sign-changing Radau quadrature kernel. Finally, we distinguish numerical certification from ordinary high-order approximation: we include concrete examples from spline, moment and statistical models and report experiments for truncated powers and near-pole Stieltjes kernels, where the shape certificate remains meaningful when classical smoothness-based error constants are unavailable or severely pessimistic.

</details>


### [40] [GAC-PINN: Geometry-Adaptive and Constraint-Enhanced Physics-Informed Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35196)
*Yanxin Zhang,Yong Zhang,Houbiao Li*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: GAC-PINN is a geometry-adaptive, constraint-enhanced PINN framework that overcomes spectral bias, geometric inflexibility, and boundary constraint conflicts via adaptive grid mapping, hard-constraint ansatz, Fourier features, and operator-aware routing, achieving superior accuracy on sharp-feature benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard PINNs suffer from spectral bias, geometric inflexibility, and boundary constraint conflicts when handling steep gradients, sharp interfaces, or severe spatio-temporal coupling, undermining accuracy and convergence.

Method: Four components: (1) gradient-driven adaptive grid mapping (AGM) with Jacobian regularization for diffeomorphic point concentration; (2) adaptive bandwidth hard-constraint ansatz with spatially-varying boundary transition widths; (3) Gaussian Fourier feature mapping as spectral preconditioner; (4) operator-aware router selecting hard-constraint construction based on presence of temporal derivatives. Plus an AGM callback and three-stage training strategy.

Result: On viscous Burgers, sharp-peaked 2D Poisson, and Allen-Cahn equations, GAC-PINN achieves relative L2 errors of (1.747±0.450)e-4, (2.868±0.947)e-5, and (1.756±0.712)e-3, consistently beating baselines. Ablations show AGM alone outperforms RAR, while RAR helps only with FFM, indicating context-dependent module interaction. Convergence analysis confirms rapid error reduction and saturation with resolution.

Conclusion: GAC-PINN provides a practical adaptive framework for high-fidelity simulation of problems with localized sharp features in applied mechanics and computational physics.

Abstract: For systems with steep gradients, sharp interfaces, or severe spatio-temporal coupling, Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) suffer from spectral bias, geometric inflexibility, and boundary constraint conflicts, which undermine accuracy and convergence. To overcome these issues, we propose a geometry-adaptive and constraint-enhanced PINN (GAC-PINN). The framework comprises four components: a gradient-driven adaptive grid mapping (AGM) for diffeomorphic point concentration with Jacobian regularization, an adaptive bandwidth hard-constraint ansatz with spatially-varying boundary transition widths, a Gaussian Fourier feature mapping as a spectral preconditioner to further enhance high-wavenumber representation, and an operator-aware router that automatically selects the appropriate hard-constraint construction based on whether the governing PDE contains temporal derivatives. An AGM callback mechanism and a three-stage training strategy ensure stable coordination. Benchmarks including the viscous Burgers equation, a sharp-peaked 2D Poisson problem, and the Allen-Cahn phase-transition equation show that GAC-PINN attains relative (L^2) errors of ((1.747\pm 0.450)\times 10^{-4}), ((2.868\pm 0.947)\times 10^{-5}), and ((1.756 \pm 0.712)\times 10^{-3}), respectively, consistently outperforming the baselines. Ablation studies further reveal that AGM alone yields a substantially lower error than residual-based adaptive refinement (RAR), while RAR becomes beneficial only when combined with FFM, demonstrating a context-dependent module interaction. Convergence analysis verifies rapid error reduction and saturation with increasing resolution, establishing a practical adaptive framework for high-fidelity simulation of problems with localized sharp features in applied mechanics and computational physics.

</details>


### [41] [Finite Element Approximation of a Hemivariational Inequality for Steady-State Heat Conduction: Double-Limit Convergence of Penalization and Discretization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35538)
*Piotr Bartman-Szwarc,Anna Ochal,Domingo A. Tarzia*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: Studies finite element approximation and convergence for a steady-state heat conduction problem with mixed boundary conditions, governed by a hemivariational inequality with penalization parameter α, proving double-limit convergence and optimal error estimates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the numerical approximation of a physical heat conduction model with mixed boundary conditions, where a hemivariational inequality and a heat transfer parameter α>0 complicate the analysis. The goal is to rigorously justify finite element methods for both the penalized problem and its limit (prescribed temperature) counterpart.

Method: Finite element approximation of the penalized hemivariational inequality problem and its limit problem with prescribed constant boundary temperature. Convergence analysis is performed via a double-limit approach, letting the mesh size h→0 and penalization parameter α→∞, both independently and simultaneously. An optimal-order error estimate for the discrete limit problem is derived, and the absence of a uniform-in-α estimate is explained. Numerical simulations on four examples use the method of manufactured solutions for verification.

Result: Strong convergence of discrete solutions is proved, including double-limit convergence of finite element approximations to the limit solution as h→0 and α→∞ (independently and simultaneously). An optimal-order error estimate is established for the discrete limit problem. It is shown that a uniform-in-α error estimate cannot be expected. Numerical experiments on four examples verify the theory.

Conclusion: The finite element method is validated for the steady-state heat conduction problem with mixed boundary conditions and hemivariational inequalities. The double-limit convergence framework and optimal error estimate provide a solid theoretical foundation, with the caveat that uniform-in-α estimates are unattainable, confirmed by numerical simulations.

Abstract: In this paper we study the numerical approximation and convergence analysis of a steady-state heat conduction problem with mixed boundary conditions. The physical model is governed by a hemivariational inequality depending on a heat transfer parameter $α> 0$. We consider the finite element approximation of this penalized problem, as well as its corresponding limit problem with a prescribed constant temperature on a part of the boundary. The main theoretical result establishes the strong convergence of the discrete solutions. Specifically, we prove the double-limit convergence of the finite element approximations to the limit solution as the mesh size $h$ tends to zero and the penalization parameter $α$ tends to infinity, independently and simultaneously. We further complement the convergence analysis with an error estimate of optimal order for the discrete limit problem, and we explain why an estimate uniform in the penalization parameter cannot be expected. The theoretical results are illustrated by numerical simulations on four examples verified by the method of manufactured solutions.

</details>


### [42] [High-order energy diminishing ALE-SAV finite element methods for two-phase Navier--Stokes flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35590)
*Shu Ma*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: A high-order fully discrete ALE-SAV finite element method for sharp-interface two-phase Navier-Stokes flow that provably satisfies a discrete energy dissipation law.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulating sharp-interface two-phase Navier-Stokes flow with moving interfaces is challenging; ensuring discrete energy stability across moving meshes while retaining high-order accuracy is difficult.

Method: Combines an SAV reformulation for stable treatment of surface and gravitational potential energies with an energy-stable ALE transfer operator that controls density-weighted kinetic energy across moving meshes. The resulting scheme is linear.

Result: The scheme satisfies a modified energy-dissipation inequality and is linear. Numerical experiments, including rising-bubble benchmarks, show robustness and confirm second-order convergence in time and arbitrarily high-order convergence in space.

Conclusion: The proposed high-order ALE-SAV finite element method provides a robust, energy-stable, and high-order accurate approach for sharp-interface two-phase Navier-Stokes flow.

Abstract: We develop a high-order fully discrete ALE-SAV finite element method for sharp-interface two-phase Navier--Stokes flow that satisfies a discrete energy dissipation law. We introduce an SAV reformulation to ensure the stable treatment of the surface and gravitational potential energies, together with an energy-stable ALE transfer operator that controls the density-weighted kinetic energy across moving meshes. The resulting scheme is linear and satisfies a modified energy-dissipation inequality. Numerical experiments, including rising-bubble benchmarks, demonstrate the robustness of the method and confirm second-order convergence in time and arbitrarily high-order convergence in space.

</details>


### [43] [Isogeometric multi-patch preconditioner for time-dependent electromagnetic problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35636)
*Bernard Kapidani,Gabriele Loli,Giancarlo Sangalli,Mattia Tani,Rafael Vázquez*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper extends high-order spline-based geometric methods for 3D Maxwell's equations from single- to multi-patch geometries by constructing a spectrally equivalent preconditioner for 1-forms mass matrices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous high-order spline-based geometric schemes for the 3D Maxwell initial boundary value problem were limited to single-patch geometries, and inverting mass matrices in multi-patch settings poses computational challenges.

Method: The authors extend existing techniques for 0-forms mass and stiffness matrices to 1-forms mass matrices, leveraging the tensor-product structure of spline spaces to build a spectrally equivalent preconditioner. For single-patch domains, the preconditioner is robust with respect to mesh size and spline degree. In the multi-patch case, the single-patch preconditioner is combined with an additive Schwarz approach to ensure robustness and scalability with respect to the number of patches.

Result: The computational complexity of the proposed preconditioner is analyzed, and its robustness and efficiency are validated through comprehensive numerical experiments.

Conclusion: The proposed spectral-equivalent preconditioner, combined with additive Schwarz, enables efficient and scalable solution of Maxwell's equations on multi-patch spline geometries.

Abstract: We build upon recent developments in high-order spline-based geometric methods for the three-dimensional initial boundary value problem of Maxwell's equations. Previous schemes were limited to single-patch geometries. To address the computational challenges associated with inverting mass matrices in multi-patch settings, we extend existing techniques for 0-forms mass and stiffness matrices to 1-forms mass matrices. Our approach leverages the tensor-product structure of spline spaces to construct a spectrally equivalent preconditioner for the 1-forms mass matrices. Specifically, we design an efficient preconditioner for single-patch domains that is robust with respect to both mesh size and spline degree. In the multi-patch case, this methodology is extended by integrating the single-patch preconditioner with an additive Schwarz approach, ensuring robustness and scalability with respect to the number of patches. We analyze the computational complexity of the proposed preconditioner and validate its robustness and efficiency through comprehensive numerical experiments.

</details>


### [44] [Symplectic filtering of Hamiltonian dynamics with moving sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35647)
*Olga Mula,Cecilia Pagliantini,Federico Vismara*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: The paper proposes an online structure-preserving filtering algorithm for reconstructing an unknown solution of a parametric Hamiltonian PDE from finite measurements, using time-evolving low-dimensional symplectic approximation spaces, with rigorous error bounds and optional dynamic sensor placement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structure-preserving methods for forward Hamiltonian simulation are well developed, but their data assimilation and filtering counterparts lack rigorous accuracy guarantees and mathematical foundations. This paper aims to fill that gap.

Method: An online filtering algorithm reconstructs an unknown function u† from finitely many measurements, assuming u† solves a parametric Hamiltonian PDE with unknown inputs. It employs low-dimensional symplectic approximation spaces that evolve in time according to the PDE model and are informed by measurement data. The method can also be coupled with a dynamical sensor-placement strategy to optimize reconstruction stability.

Result: The authors derive error bounds in terms of stability and best approximation. They show the reconstruction remains in a symplectic space and preserves energy up to the approximation error. Numerical experiments demonstrate the method's effectiveness and the benefits of dynamic over static sensor placement.

Conclusion: The proposed online symplectic filtering method provides rigorous accuracy guarantees for data assimilation of Hamiltonian PDEs, preserves key geometric structure (symplecticity and energy), and benefits from dynamic sensor placement.

Abstract: While structure-preserving methods for the forward simulation of Hamiltonian systems are well established, the mathematical foundations of their data assimilation and filtering counterparts remain comparatively less developed, particularly regarding rigorous accuracy guarantees. To address this gap, we propose an online filtering algorithm to reconstruct an unknown function $u^\dagger$ from finitely many measurements, assuming that $u^\dagger$ solves a parametric Hamiltonian PDE with unknown inputs. The method uses low-dimensional symplectic approximation spaces that evolve in time according to the PDE model and informed by the measurement data. The method can also be coupled with a dynamical sensor-placement strategy designed to optimize reconstruction stability. We derive error bounds in terms of stability and best approximation, and show that the reconstruction remains in a symplectic space and preserves energy up to the approximation error. Numerical experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the method and the benefits of dynamic over static sensor placement.

</details>


### [45] [Boris-type exponential integrators for charged-particle dynamics in strong magnetic fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35679)
*Marlis Hochbruck,Sebastian Merk*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper develops a family of exponential integrators (via matrix-valued filter functions) for charged-particle motion in a strong constant magnetic field, achieving error bounds independent of field strength with bounded filters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard Boris-type methods degrade in accuracy as field strength grows, and filtered Boris methods suffer from singularities and resonance-induced error blow-up. A systematic framework is needed for unconditionally well-behaved integrators in the unresolved-gyration regime.

Method: Starting from the variation-of-constants formula, the authors derive a broad family of one-step exponential integrators expressed through matrix-valued filter functions. This generalizes existing filtered Boris schemes and allows construction of uniformly bounded filters. Error analysis uses a discrete variation-of-constants representation with summation-by-parts arguments.

Result: Error bounds with constants independent of magnetic field strength are established, along with filter conditions for first- and second-order accuracy. Bounded filters avoid resonance blow-up and permit arbitrary time steps, at the cost of controlled order reduction near resonant frequencies. Numerical experiments confirm convergence behavior, differing position/velocity accuracy, and leading error term effects.

Conclusion: A unified exponential-integrator framework with bounded filters yields robust, field-strength-independent time integration for strongly magnetized particle motion, trading minor order reduction at resonances for stability and uniform accuracy.

Abstract: We study numerical time integration for the motion of a charged particle in a strong magnetic field, focusing on the regime in which the fast gyration is not resolved by the time step. Standard Boris-type methods are structure preserving and accurate when the gyration is resolved, but their error constants deteriorate with increasing field strength; filtered Boris methods improve this behavior but may suffer from singularities and resonance-induced error blow-up. In this work we consider the case of a constant strong magnetic field and derive a broad family of one-step exponential integrators from the variation-of-constants formula, formulated in terms of matrix-valued filter functions. This framework includes existing filtered Boris variants and permits the construction of schemes with uniformly bounded filters. Using a discrete variation-of-constants representation together with summation-by-parts arguments, we establish error bounds whose constants are independent of the magnetic field strength and characterize the filter conditions required for first- and second-order accuracy. In particular, we show that bounded filters avoid resonance blow-up and allow arbitrary time steps, at the cost of a controlled order reduction near resonant frequencies. Numerical experiments confirm the predicted convergence behavior, the different accuracy of position and velocity components, and the role of the leading error terms across a wide range of step-size-field-strength products.

</details>


### [46] [When does least-squares residual minimization solve a differential equation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35716)
*Carlos Esteve-Yagüe,Richard Tsai,Stanley Osher*

Main category: math.NA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes when least-squares residual minimization actually recovers the exact solution of a differential equation, showing that stationary points and minimizing sequences often fail to solve the target equation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Least-squares residual minimization is widely used to solve differential equations by turning them into optimization problems, yet it is unclear what stationarity of the residual functional and its sampled gradients actually imply about the residual and the intended solution.

Method: Starting from the orthogonality relation between residual and the range of the linearized operator, the authors study how boundary conditions and parameterized variations constrain the residual at stationary points; they examine linear equations via a compatibility/projection characterization, a scalar conservation law with transonic boundary data, a Hamilton-Jacobi example, a viscous model, uniformly convex Monge-Ampere critical points, and piecewise-smooth trial families.

Result: Smooth spurious critical points can occur even for well-posed problems; for a scalar conservation law every minimizing sequence converges to a continuous non-solution even when a stationary entropy shock exists, with parameter norms diverging. A Hamilton-Jacobi function can have zero residual without being the viscosity solution. For piecewise-smooth trial families, the regularity defining the residual does not justify the sampled gradient used in training; moving residual jumps add terms absent from it, with an example having vanishing sampled gradients but nonzero continuous derivative.

Conclusion: Stationarity and sampled gradients generally do not guarantee the exact solution; only under specific conditions (e.g., compatibility, viscous models, uniformly convex Monge-Ampere critical points) are the intended implications restored, clarifying when minimizing sequences lead to the intended solution.

Abstract: Least-squares residual minimization approximates a differential equation through an optimization problem. This paper investigates the conditions required to transition from stationary points and minimizing sequences of the residual functional to the exact solution. Starting from the classical orthogonality relation between the residual and the range of the linearized operator, we study how boundary conditions and parameterized variations determine what can be inferred about the residual at a stationary point. Smooth spurious critical points can occur even for well-posed problems; for linear equations, a compatibility condition yields an exact projection characterization. For a scalar conservation law with transonic boundary data, every minimizing sequence converges to a continuous function that does not solve the equation, even when a stationary entropy shock exists. We give conditions under which approaching this limit forces the parameter norms to diverge. A Hamilton--Jacobi example shows that a function can have zero residual without being the viscosity solution. Favorable results for a viscous model and uniformly convex Monge--Ampère critical points identify conditions that restore the corresponding implications. For piecewise-smooth trial families, the regularity needed to define the residual functional does not by itself justify the sampled gradient used in training. Moving residual jumps can contribute terms absent from that gradient; an explicit example has identically vanishing sampled gradients and a nonzero continuous derivative. Together, these results clarify what stationarity and sampled gradients imply about the residual, and under which conditions minimizing sequences lead to the intended solution.

</details>

### [47] [Eigenfunction asymptotics and nodal domain estimates for dumbbell domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31856)
*Thomas Beck,Yaiza Canzani,Jeremy L. Marzuola*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This paper studies nodal structure of Neumann eigenfunctions on planar dumbbell domains as the neck width shrinks to zero, showing thin necks force more nodal domains and that the third eigenfunction can be Courant sharp.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how domain geometry (a shrinking neck) governs the number of nodal domains of Neumann eigenfunctions, and whether Courant sharpness holds in such singular limits.

Method: Asymptotic analysis of the vanishing neck limit via two-dimensional matched asymptotics with logarithmic terms, deriving refined asymptotics for first nonzero profiles on vanishing regions expressed through Neumann Green's functions with poles at neck attachment points.

Result: Sufficiently thin necks force an increasing number of nodal domains as the limiting eigenvalue grows; the third Neumann eigenfunction has exactly three nodal domains and is Courant sharp, while nodal deficiency on an end domain can persist on the full dumbbell.

Conclusion: Thin necks strongly influence nodal domain counts of Neumann eigenfunctions, yielding both Courant-sharp behavior and persistent nodal deficiency, depending on the end-domain structure.

Abstract: We study the nodal structure of Neumann eigenfunctions on planar dumbbell domains as the width of the neck joining two fixed end domains tends to zero. Under simplicity and non-degeneracy assumptions, we show that sufficiently thin necks force an increasing number of nodal domains as the limiting eigenvalue grows. At the bottom of the spectrum this forcing can attain Courant's upper bound: in particular, the third Neumann eigenfunction has exactly three nodal domains and is Courant sharp. At the same time, nodal deficiency of an eigenfunction on an end domain can persist on the full dumbbell, giving eigenfunctions that are not Courant sharp. The main analytic difficulty is that the singular limit may vanish on one or both end domains even though these regions contribute nodal domains for every positive neck width. We overcome this by deriving refined asymptotics for the first nonzero profiles on the vanishing regions, expressed through Neumann Green's functions with poles at the neck attachment points and obtained by a two-dimensional matched asymptotic analysis with logarithmic terms.

</details>


### [48] [Homogenization and Long Time Behavior of Wasserstein gradient flow for McKean-Vlasov Equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31945)
*Yuan Gao,Nung Kwan Yip*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper studies a McKean-Vlasov gradient flow with fast spatial oscillations in both the confining potential and the underlying metric, identifies the homogenized limiting dynamics as a gradient flow in an effective medium, and proves long-time exponential convergence to the invariant measure with a Log-Sobolev constant uniform in the oscillation scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fast oscillatory behavior appears in both the energetic component (confining potential) and the kinematic component (metric) of the model, and existing homogenization results were limited to linear Fokker-Planck equations. The authors aim to extend their previous linear work to a nonlinear, nonlocal McKean-Vlasov setting.

Method: The equation is formulated as a gradient flow in the space of probability measures with a Wasserstein metric. The analysis relies on variational methods and functional inequalities to pass to the homogenized limit and to obtain uniform Logarithmic Sobolev constants.

Result: The limiting dynamics is identified as a gradient flow in an effective medium. Long-time exponential convergence of solutions to the invariant measure is proved, with a rate expressed via a Logarithmic Sobolev constant that is uniform in the oscillatory length scale.

Conclusion: The homogenization result extends the authors' previous work on a linear Fokker-Planck equation to a nonlinear and nonlocal McKean-Vlasov setting.

Abstract: We consider a McKean-Vlasov equation incorporating spatial fast oscillatory behavior into both the energetic and kinematic components of the model. The former is given in terms of a confining potential while the latter by an underlying metric. The equation is formulated as a gradient flow in the space of probability measures endowed with a Wasserstein metric. We identify the limiting dynamics which is also described as a gradient flow in an effective media. We further prove the long time exponential convergence of solutions to the invariant measure. The rate of convergence is expressed in terms of a Logarithmic Sobolev constant which is uniform in terms of the oscillatory length scale. Our approach is based on variational and functional inequalities. The homogenization result of the current paper extends the authors' previous work [GaoYip] on a linear Fokker-Planck equation to a nonlinear and nonlocal setting.

</details>


### [49] [A Fourier approach to the sharp stability of Heisenberg Uncertainty Principles of higher and fractional orders: a new perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31949)
*Anh Do,Nguyen Lam,Guozhen Lu,Van Hoang Nguyen*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The sharp second-order Heisenberg Uncertainty Principle and its stability are shown to be equivalent (via Fourier transform) to the first-order L2 Caffarelli-Kohn-Nirenberg inequality and its stability. This yields short proofs and two families of sharp fractional HUPs with optimal constants, optimizers, and stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors previously proved a sharp stability estimate for the second-order HUP through a long argument. They seek a simpler, unifying explanation and a broader framework covering fractional versions and deeper remainder structures.

Method: Fourier transform identifies the second-order HUP and its stability with the first-order L2 Caffarelli-Kohn-Nirenberg inequality and its stability. This observation is extended to fractional powers of the Laplacian (including negative orders/Riesz potentials), computing sharp constants and optimizers, analyzing stability constants (equal to 1 for the first family in every dimension and nonnegative order), and decomposing the deficit into chains of remainder terms governed by spectral gaps of an explicit operator.

Result: Short proofs of the sharp stability estimate; two one-parameter families of sharp fractional HUPs with computed sharp constants, characterized optimizers, and sharp stability. For classical HUP, three remainder terms with optimal explicit constants; for second-order HUP, a chain of four remainder terms via confluent hypergeometric profiles. Also stability chains for curl-free vector fields.

Conclusion: The Fourier-side equivalence unifies and simplifies sharp HUP stability, extends to fractional and negative orders, and reveals that the deficit encodes richer information than distance to optimizers, controlling explicit remainder chains with optimal constants.

Abstract: We show that, on the Fourier side, the sharp second order Heisenberg Uncertainty Principle (HUP) and its sharp stability for functions are nothing but the first order $L^{2}$-Caffarelli--Kohn--Nirenberg inequality and its sharp stability, applied to their Fourier transforms. This gives a proof in a few lines of the sharp stability estimate that we established recently by a much longer argument. The same observation then produces two one-parameter families of sharp fractional Heisenberg Uncertainty Principles, in which the gradient is replaced by a fractional power of the Laplacian: for each of them we compute the sharp constants, characterize all the optimizers, and establish the sharp stability estimates. The stability constant of the first family is equal to $1$ for every nonnegative order and every dimension. Negative orders, where the fractional Laplacian becomes a Riesz potential, are treated as well. Finally, we show that the deficit carries much more information than the distance to the optimizers alone: it controls an explicit chain of successive remainder terms whose constants are the successive spectral gaps of an explicit operator. For the classical Heisenberg Uncertainty Principle this gives three remainder terms with optimal explicit constants, and for the second order HUP a chain of four remainder terms measured against explicit confluent hypergeometric profiles and coupled through one single set of parameters. As applications, we also establish the chain of stability of the HUP for curl-free vector fields.

</details>


### [50] [Large time behaviour in the multidimensional nonlocal Fisher-KPP equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32051)
*Jean-Michel Roquejoffre,Mingmin Zhang*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper studies level-set expansion for Fisher-KPP reaction-diffusion equations with non-spherically-symmetric, compactly supported, even nonlocal kernels, pushing the asymptotic expansion beyond the leading Freidlin-Gärtner speed to include vanishing (logarithmic delay plus a direction-dependent constant) terms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlocal diffusion kernels need not be spherically symmetric, so the level sets of solutions advance anisotropically, governed by a Freidlin-Gärtner type asymptotic speed rather than a simple radial one. Understanding finer corrections to this expansion is the goal.

Method: Analysis of a Fisher-KPP type reaction-diffusion equation where the diffusion is given by a nonnegative, compactly supported, even kernel; the asymptotic expansion of level sets is derived, extending beyond the leading-order Freidlin-Gärtner formula.

Result: The level sets advance asymptotically at a Freidlin-Gärtner type speed, and the expansion is refined to include terms that vanish as time tends to infinity: an asymptotically logarithmic delay corrected by a time-constant, direction-dependent function.

Conclusion: The main contribution is extending the level-set expansion up to vanishing terms, revealing an asymptotically logarithmic delay with a constant-in-time correction depending on the direction of propagation.

Abstract: This work studies the expansion of the level sets of the solutions of a Fisher-KPP type reaction- diffusion equation, the diffusion being given by a nonnegative, compactly supported, even kernel. Because the latter is not assumed to be spherically symmetric, the level sets advance at an asymptotic speed given by a Freidlin-Gärtner type formula. The main contribution of this paper is to push the expansion up to terms that vanish as time goes to infinity, consisting in an asymptotically logarithmic delay corrected by an function that is constant in time, but depends on the direction of propagation.

</details>


### [51] [Isoperimetric clusters with a liquid phase](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32145)
*Jake Wellington*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper studies an isoperimetric problem for wet foams where the liquid chamber may collapse to zero-thickness films; it identifies the relaxed energy and proves regularity of generalized minimizers, connecting wet and dry foam limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Minimizers of the wet foam isoperimetric problem need not exist because the liquid chamber can collapse onto films of zero thickness, so a relaxation framework is required.

Method: The authors identify the relaxed energy that accounts for collapsed films, then prove compactness and energy representation theorems for limits of minimizing sequences, using these to establish regularity of generalized minimizers.

Result: Limits of minimizing sequences are minimizers of the relaxed energy; generalized minimizers exhibit regularity including an equilibrium law for pressures across collapsed films; as liquid volume vanishes, minimal energies converge to those of the classical dry foam.

Conclusion: A complete relaxed variational description of wet foams is obtained, with a pressure equilibrium law for collapsed films and a rigorous link to dry foam in the vanishing-liquid limit.

Abstract: A wet foam is a cluster of air chambers whose interfaces are coated by a single liquid chamber of prescribed volume. In the corresponding isoperimetric problem the liquid chamber may collapse onto films of zero thickness, so that minimizers need not exist. We identify the relaxed energy accounting for such collapsed films and prove that limits of minimizing sequences are its minimizers. The resulting compactness and energy representation theorems lead to the regularity of these generalized minimizers, including an equilibrium law for the pressures across collapsed films. Finally, as the liquid volume vanishes, the minimal energies converge to those of the classical dry foam.

</details>


### [52] [On elastic scattering behavior of corner domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32148)
*Pu-Zhao Kow,Mikko Salo,Henrik Shahgholian*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper proves that corners and edge singularities always scatter elastic waves in anisotropic inhomogeneous media for any fixed wave number.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extends a previous scalar-equation scattering result to systems of equations, generalizing the "corners always scatter" principle to anisotropic media with variable coefficients.

Method: Blowup analysis and involved computations, treating penetrable obstacles with piecewise C^1 boundaries and corner (2D) or edge (higher-D) singularities.

Result: Proves that in 2D every penetrable obstacle with piecewise C^1 boundary and corner singularities, and in higher dimensions every obstacle with edge singularities, scatters every incident wave satisfying suitable assumptions.

Conclusion: Corners always scatter for general anisotropic media with variable coefficients, extending the principle from scalar to elastic systems.

Abstract: We investigate the scattering of elastic waves by anisotropic inhomogeneous media at a fixed wave number. We prove that, in two dimensions, every penetrable obstacle with piecewise $C^{1}$ boundary and corner singularities, and, in higher dimensions, every obstacle with edge singularities, scatters every incident wave satisfying suitable assumptions. This work extends our previous result [J. Math. Anal. Appl. 2026, doi:10.1016/j.jmaa.2026.130630] from scalar equations to systems of equations, a generalization that requires a more delicate blowup analysis and more involved computations. Roughly speaking, our results establish the principle that \emph{corners always scatter} for general anisotropic media with variable coefficients.

</details>


### [53] [Global-in-time inviscid limit of the Navier-Stokes equations for vortex-wave data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32233)
*Trinh T. Nguyen*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper justifies the vortex-wave system as the vanishing-viscosity limit of the 2D Navier-Stokes equations on any finite time interval for a point vortex plus smooth background initial vorticity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The vortex-wave system was introduced by Marchioro and Pulvirenti in the 1990s, but a global-in-time justification as the vanishing-viscosity limit of Navier-Stokes was lacking for this class of initial data.

Method: Consider 2D Navier-Stokes with initial vorticity given by a point vortex plus a smooth, compactly supported background vanishing near the vortex; derive quantitative estimates for the regular and concentrated components.

Result: The regular component converges strongly to the background vorticity governed by the vortex-wave system, while the concentrated component is approximated by a Lamb-Oseen vortex centered on the limiting point-vortex trajectory. Quantitative estimates are obtained with constants and a viscosity threshold depending on the time interval.

Conclusion: Provides a global-in-time justification of the vortex-wave system as the vanishing-viscosity limit of 2D Navier-Stokes for this class of initial data.

Abstract: We justify the vortex-wave system introduced by Marchioro and Pulvirenti in the 1990s as the vanishing-viscosity limit of the two-dimensional Navier-Stokes equations on every prescribed finite time interval. The initial vorticity consists of a point vortex and a smooth, compactly supported background that vanishes near the vortex. The regular component converges strongly to the background vorticity governed by the vortex-wave system, while the concentrated component is approximated by a Lamb-Oseen vortex centered on the limiting point-vortex trajectory. We obtain quantitative estimates for both components, with constants and a viscosity threshold depending on the chosen time interval, thereby providing a global-in-time justification of the vortex-wave system for this class of initial data.

</details>


### [54] [Scattering for the quadratic NLS system in $\mathbb{R}^{5}$ without mass resonance or radial symmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32364)
*Takahisa Inui,Kuranosuke Nishimura*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: Proves scattering below the ground state for the quadratic NLS system in 5D when κ≠1/2, removing the radial symmetry assumption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior results: scattering below the ground state was known for κ=1/2 (mass resonance, Hamano) and for radial solutions when κ≠1/2 (Hamano–Inui–Nishimura 2021). This paper extends scattering to non-radial solutions in the κ≠1/2 case.

Method: Concentration compactness and rigidity method of Kenig–Merle (2006). For the rigidity argument, following Pausader (2010), a localized virial argument in the direction orthogonal to the momentum is used.

Result: Scattering below the ground state for κ≠1/2 is established without radial symmetry.

Conclusion: The radial assumption in the κ≠1/2 scattering result is removed, giving scattering below the ground state for general (non-radial) solutions.

Abstract: We consider the quadratic NLS system \begin{equation*}
  \begin{cases}
  i\partial_tu+Δu=v\bar{u},\newline
  i\partial_tv+κΔv=u^2,
  \end{cases}
  \qquad (t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^5, \end{equation*} where $κ>0$. If $κ=1/2$, which is called mass resonance condition, then scattering below the ground state is shown by Hamano. Moreover, when $κ\neq 1/2$, scattering of radial solutions is proved in Hamano--Inui--Nishimura (2021). In the present paper, we prove scattering below the ground state for $κ\neq1/2$ without radial symmetry. Our proof is based on the concentration compactness and rigidity method by Kenig--Merle (2006). For the rigidity argument, following Pausader (2010), we use a localized virial argument in the direction orthogonal to the momentum.

</details>


### [55] [A singular minimizer of a smooth strongly polyconvex functional in two dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32404)
*André Guerra,Riccardo Tione*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The authors construct a Lipschitz but non-C^1 map u: R^2 -> R^6 that is the unique global minimizer of a smooth, strongly polyconvex functional, a necessarily non-homogeneous example.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is interest in whether minimizers of smooth, strongly polyconvex functionals can be non-smooth (non-C^1). Prior examples faced regularity expectations, prompting a need for a genuine counterexample.

Method: Construct a Lipschitz, non-C^1 map u: R^2 -> R^6 and show it is the unique global minimizer of a smooth, strongly polyconvex functional. The construction is necessarily non-homogeneous.

Result: A Lipschitz, non-C^1 map u: R^2 -> R^6 is obtained that uniquely globally minimizes a smooth, strongly polyconvex functional.

Conclusion: Smooth, strongly polyconvex functionals can have unique global minimizers that are Lipschitz but not C^1, showing regularity fails in this setting; such examples must be non-homogeneous.

Abstract: We construct a Lipschitz, non-$C^1$ map $u\colon \mathbb{R}^2\to \mathbb{R}^6$ which is the unique global minimizer of a smooth, strongly polyconvex functional. Such an example is necessarily non-homogeneous.

</details>


### [56] [Optimal mixing and enhanced dissipation for a smooth velocity field in any spatial dimension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32535)
*Luca Melzi*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: This note constructs an explicit smooth, divergence-free random velocity field on the d-dimensional torus that is a universal exponential mixer and, with small positive diffusivity, dissipation-enhancing at optimal rate, using the Random Dynamical Systems viewpoint and ergodicity of the two-point Markov process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how random incompressible velocity fields can drive mixing and enhance dissipation is central to fluid dynamics and the theory of advection-diffusion equations. The paper seeks an explicit example achieving universal exponential mixing and optimal dissipation enhancement in arbitrary dimension.

Method: The construction is based on the Random Dynamical Systems viewpoint, relying on the uniform ergodicity of the two-point Markov process associated with the two-dimensional Pierrehumbert model. An explicit space-time smooth, divergence-free random velocity field is built and then analyzed for the associated transport and advection-diffusion equations.

Result: The velocity field is shown to be a universal exponential mixer for the transport equation and, when molecular diffusivity is set to a small positive value, to be dissipation enhancing with optimal rate, for arbitrary dimension d on the torus.

Conclusion: The paper provides an explicit example demonstrating that a single smooth divergence-free random velocity field can simultaneously achieve universal exponential mixing and optimal dissipation enhancement in any dimension.

Abstract: In this note, we consider the advection-diffusion equation on the $d$-dimensional torus, where $d$ is arbitrary. First, for the associated transport equation, we provide an explicit example of a space-time smooth, divergence-free, random velocity field that is a universal exponential mixer. Second, we set the molecular diffusivity to a (small) positive value, and we show that the same velocity field is dissipation enhancing with optimal rate. Our construction is based on the Random Dynamical Systems viewpoint, and relies on the uniform ergodicity of the two-point Markov process associated with the two-dimensional Pierrehumbert model.

</details>


### [57] [Near-cancellation of enstrophy and strain requires shear or a balanced vortex](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32543)
*Jonathan Massey*

Main category: math.AP

TL;DR: The paper studies where the near-cancellation between fluctuating enstrophy and strain in the pressure Poisson source is "complete" (silent), proving a universal inequality m ≤ 1−f ≤ 3m linking shear fraction and distance from the silent origin, and characterizing the escape mechanism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The fluctuating enstrophy and strain in the nonlinear source of the pressure Poisson equation nearly cancel, so understanding where cancellation is complete (and how the source escapes it) is key to modeling the source. The authors seek a pointwise, geometry-based characterization of this cancellation.

Method: The source is expressed pointwise via eigenvalues of the velocity gradient. Pure shear (silent, zero eigenvalues) defines the origin of a normalized invariant plane, with normalized source as second-invariant coordinate. Using shear fraction f and distance m from the origin, the authors prove m ≤ 1−f ≤ 3m for all incompressible flows. At no-slip walls the silent object is exact (rank-one wall shear-stress fluctuation sheet); away from walls they assume the same sheet plus a small residual and derive the escape route through the along-sheet variation of sheet-normal velocity.

Result: Proved m ≤ 1−f ≤ 3m at every point of every incompressible flow. Where the source nearly vanishes and the third invariant is small, the gradient is nearly pure shear; otherwise the cancelling gradient is a balanced vortex (swirl cancelling its own strain at any strength). The escape amplitude equals the geometric mean of sheet and residual, and its sign selects swirl or strain. A rank-two silent object shares the same source and gates but has balanced eigenvalues, so the assumption fixes escape topology, not its source.

Conclusion: The shear fraction is a pointwise proxy for cancellation by shear, and any model of the pressure Poisson source must be built on what escapes the silent (cancelling) structure rather than on the source itself.

Abstract: In the nonlinear source of the pressure Poisson equation the fluctuating enstrophy and strain nearly cancel. We ask where the cancellation is complete, which we call silent, and how the source escapes it. Pointwise the source depends only on the eigenvalues of the velocity gradient. Pure shear has none, so it is silent. It is the origin of the normalised invariant plane, whose second-invariant coordinate is the normalised source. Let $f$ be the shear fraction of the gradient and $m$ its distance from that origin. We prove that $m\le1-f\le3m$ at every point of every incompressible flow. Wherever the source nearly vanishes and the third invariant is small, the gradient is nearly pure shear. Otherwise the cancelling gradient is a balanced vortex, a swirl its own strain cancels at any strength. At a no-slip wall the silent object is exact: the wall shear-stress fluctuation, a rank-one sheet. Away from the wall we assume the same sheet, dressed with a small residual, and derive how it escapes. The escape passes through one element of the residual, the along-sheet variation of the sheet-normal velocity.
