### [149] [Accelerating massive ensembles of ordinary differential equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35661)
*Lawrence Berry,Will Handley,Oliver Hahn,Nils Schöneberg*

Main category: astro-ph.IM

TL;DR: modax is a Python library of GPU-accelerated ODE solvers designed for large ensembles of low-dimensional independent ODEs, using a thread-based paradigm compatible with JAX, achieving large speed-ups over existing solvers and applied to cosmology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving large ensembles of small, independent ODEs is common in computational science (Bayesian estimation, Monte Carlo UQ, uncoupled physical systems like cosmological Einstein-Boltzmann equations). GPUs suit these parallel workloads but require careful handling of architectural constraints, and existing pure-JAX solvers are ill-suited for highly divergent trajectories.

Method: modax provides GPU-accelerated ODE solvers compatible with the JAX ecosystem but written in a low-level, thread-based programming paradigm. It includes an explicit Tsit5 solver and an implicit Rodas5P (Rosenbrock-Wanner) solver, the latter using sparsity-exploiting techniques and data structures to scale to higher dimensions.

Result: Benchmarks on Lorenz, Robertson, and van der Pol lattice systems (across ensemble size, dimensionality, and trajectory divergence) compare modax against diffrax, DiffEqGPU.jl, and torchdiffeq. Tsit5 achieves 30x speed-up over diffrax on Lorenz; Rodas5P achieves over 700x speed-up on Robertson, showing the superiority of linearly implicit Rosenbrock-Wanner methods on GPUs.

Conclusion: modax demonstrates significant efficiency improvements for modeling phenomena described by low-dimensional (<200D) ODEs, validated on three cosmology examples: Bayesian parameter estimation from primordial abundances, Monte Carlo UQ of the global 21cm signal, and uncoupled Fourier modes in the primordial power spectrum.

Abstract: Solving large ensembles of small, independent ordinary differential equations (ODEs) is a common task in computational science and engineering, arising for example in Bayesian parameter estimation, Monte Carlo uncertainty quantification, and the integration of uncoupled physical systems such as the linearised Einstein-Boltzmann equations of cosmology. Modern graphics processing units (GPUs) with thousands of cores are well suited to such parallel workloads, but realising this potential requires careful attention to GPU-specific architectural constraints. We present modax, a Python library of GPU-accelerated ODE solvers specifically optimised for solving large ensembles of low-dimensional, independent problems. Our solvers are compatible with the JAX ecosystem but are written in a low-level, thread-based programming paradigm better suited to ensembles of highly divergent trajectories than their pure-JAX counterparts. Benchmarks on the Lorenz, Robertson and van der Pol lattice systems compare modax against diffrax, DiffEqGPU$.$jl and torchdiffeq across ensemble size, dimensionality, and trajectory divergence. Our explicit Tsit5 solver achieves 30x speed-up over diffrax on Lorenz systems, and our implicit Rodas5P solver achieves over 700x speed-up on Robertson systems, demonstrating the superiority of linearly implicit Rosenbrock-Wanner methods on GPUs. Our implicit solver scales well to higher dimensions thanks to the use of sparsity-exploiting techniques and data structures. We conclude by applying modax to three examples from the field of cosmology - Bayesian parameter estimation from primordial abundances, Monte Carlo uncertainty quantification of the global 21cm signal, and the computation of uncoupled Fourier modes in the primordial power spectrum - and demonstrate significant efficiency improvements in our ability to model physical phenomena described by low-dimensional (<200D) ODEs.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.soft'></div>

# cond-mat.soft [[Back]](#toc)

### [150] [Quorum sensing with density-enhanced motility and size regulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35085)
*Itay Azizi*

Main category: cond-mat.soft

TL;DR: A model of density-enhanced size and motility shows that sensing radius controls pattern length scale, while size ratio governs particle area coverage, producing spots, holes, bands, and labyrinths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivated by biological systems and prior quorum-sensing studies with density-enhanced motility, to understand how particle size and motility jointly link microscopic regulation to large-scale pattern formation.

Method: A model where both particle size and motility increase when local density (sensed over a radius larger than particle diameter) exceeds global density. Structures are studied across different size ratios.

Result: Emergent structures include spots, holes, bands, and labyrinths. Pattern length scale is set by sensing radius and is roughly independent of size ratio; total particle-occupied area decreases with increasing size ratio; the active fraction is nearly independent of size ratio.

Conclusion: Particle size and motility couple microscopic regulation to large-scale pattern formation. The paper ends by discussing possible extensions of the model.

Abstract: Motivated by biological systems and my previous studies of quorum sensing with density-enhanced motility, I study a model of density-enhanced size and motility, in which both particle size and motility increase when the local density (over a lengthscale larger than particle diameter) exceeds the global density. The emergent structures along different size ratios are spots, holes, bands and labyrinths controlled by several distinct observations. First, the characteristic pattern length scale is controlled by the sensing radius and remains approximately independent of the size ratio. Second, the total area occupied by the particles decreases with increasing size ratio. Third, the active fraction barely depends on the size ratio. These results demonstrate how both particle size and motility can couple microscopic regulation to large-scale pattern formation. I conclude by discussing possible extensions of the model.

</details>

### [151] [Magnetizing nonlinear plasma wakefields for positron acceleration: mechanism and operating limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33175)
*Yung-Kun Liu,Pisin Chen,Ching-En Lin,Spencer Gessner,Bernhard Hidding*

Main category: physics.acc-ph

TL;DR: An axial magnetic field reorganizes nonlinear electron return in plasma wakes, creating a gyro-image structure that sustains positron acceleration over a much wider interval than unmagnetized blowout.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Positron acceleration in linear plasma wakes is possible, but the nonlinear electron-driven blowout regime confines both acceleration and focusing to narrow regions of returning plasma electrons, limiting sustained positron acceleration.

Method: Quasi-3D and full-3D particle-in-cell simulations examine how an axial magnetic field reshapes the nonlinear electron return, forming finite-radius electron columns (gyro images). The authors derive a positioning law from the quasi-static invariant and canonical angular momentum, modified by the orbit-sampled wake potential, and carry out beam loading, alignment, and driver-energy scaling tests with independent codes.

Result: Image spacing is set by the quasi-static invariant while canonical angular momentum limits the return radius; the derived law predicts density peaks across a wide range of magnetic fields. At 10^16 cm^-3 and 35 T, the usable accelerating/focusing interval expands over fourfold versus the unmagnetized case. A witness bunch at the settled phase achieves 92% capture over 60 mm with normalized emittance saturating at 83 mm mrad; energy gains of 99–150 MeV are confirmed, though exact convergence stays open.

Conclusion: Magnetic control of the nonlinear electron return provides a route to single-stage positron acceleration. Next steps require beam quality preservation, formation-transient control, and solenoid integration.

Abstract: Positron acceleration is possible in linear plasma wakes, but the nonlinear electron-driven blowout regime confines simultaneous acceleration and focusing to narrow regions of returning plasma electrons. Here we investigate how an axial magnetic field reorganizes this nonlinear return to open an operating regime for sustained positron acceleration in a uniform plasma. Using quasi-3D and full-3D particle-in-cell simulations, we connect the formation of finite-radius electron columns, or gyro images, to witness transport and its operating limits. The image spacing is governed by the quasi-static invariant, while canonical angular momentum limits the return radius. Modified by the orbit-sampled wake potential, this mechanism yields a positioning law that accurately predicts the density peaks across a wide range of magnetic field strengths. At a plasma density of $10^{16} cm^-3$ and a 35 T axial field, the reorganized structure expands the usable accelerating and focusing interval by more than a factor of four compared to the unmagnetized case. A positron witness bunch placed at the settled phase of this band achieves 92% capture over a 60 mm stage, with the normalized emittance saturating at 83 mm mrad. Independent codes confirm sustained transport, yielding energy gains between 99 and 150 MeV, though numerical convergence on the exact energy gain remains an open challenge. Together with beam loading, alignment, and driver-energy scaling tests, these results establish magnetic control of the nonlinear electron return as a route to single-stage positron acceleration. Beam quality preservation, formation-transient control, and solenoid integration define the necessary next development steps.

</details>

### [152] [Negative and Positive Cascade Rates of Slow Alfvénic Turbulence in Switchback and Non-switchback Intervals: \emph{Wind} Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31886)
*Sofiane Bourouaine*

Main category: astro-ph.SR

TL;DR: This study estimates turbulence cascade rates in imbalanced Alfvénic slow solar wind using a non-isotropic Politano--Pouquet approach on Wind data, finding forward cascade in non-switchback intervals but inverse cascade in switchback intervals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous reports of negative cascade rates in imbalanced Alfvénic solar-wind turbulence remain unexplained; this work investigates whether large magnetic-field rotations (switchbacks) modify the direction and magnitude of scale-to-scale energy transfer.

Method: A Politano--Pouquet-based approach without isotropic assumption is applied to selected 8-hour Wind intervals characterized by near-homogeneity, weak velocity shear, and imbalanced Alfvénic fluctuations, classified as switchback or non-switchback based on the angular spread of the magnetic field relative to the mean field.

Result: Non-switchback intervals show a positive total cascade rate and spectra consistent with -3/2 scaling (forward cascade). Switchback intervals show a negative total cascade rate (inverse cascade) and steeper spectra with index ~-1.6.

Conclusion: Large magnetic-field rotations substantially modify scale-to-scale energy transfer in Alfvénic slow solar wind; switchbacks may involve additional processes (local instabilities, reconnection-related structures) that alter cascade direction and magnitude, potentially explaining prior reports of negative cascade rates.

Abstract: We estimate the turbulent cascade rate in imbalanced Alfvénic turbulence in the slow solar wind using a Politano--Pouquet-based approach that does not require an isotropic assumption. We analyze selected 8-hour intervals provided by \emph{Wind} spacecraft. These intervals are characterized by near-homogeneity, weak velocity shear, and imbalanced Alfvénic fluctuations, and are classified as switchback or non-switchback depending on the angular spread of the magnetic field relative to the mean field. Switchback intervals are identified by large magnetic-field rotations, whereas non-switchback intervals exhibit only small angular deviations. Our results show that non-switchback intervals exhibit a positive total cascade rate and power spectra consistent with a $-3/2$ scaling, indicative of a forward energy cascade. In contrast, switchback intervals are characterized by a negative total cascade rate (inverse cascade), and by steeper power spectra with a spectral index of approximately $-1.6$. This indicates that large magnetic-field rotations can substantially modify the scale-to-scale energy transfer in Alfvénic slow solar wind. The results suggest that switchbacks may be associated with additional physical processes, such as local instabilities, or reconnection-related structures, that alter the direction and magnitude of the turbulent cascade. These findings may help explain previous reports of negative cascade rates in imbalanced Alfvénic solar-wind turbulence.

</details>


### [153] [Transport of Magnetic Fields by Thermohaline Convection in Crystallizing White Dwarfs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31943)
*Nicolas Frazao Fernandes,J. R. Fuentes,Jim Fuller*

Main category: astro-ph.SR

TL;DR: Thermohaline convection in crystallizing white dwarfs likely cannot generate the observed strong magnetic fields itself, but it may advect pre-existing interior fields to the surface.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Origins of strong magnetic fields in many white dwarfs are uncertain, and they appear more common for white dwarfs with crystallized cores. Crystallization drives a destabilizing composition gradient that triggers thermohaline convection, which may play a key role in magnetic field emergence.

Method: 3D magnetohydrodynamical simulations of thermohaline convection with magnetic fields of various strengths and orientations, using the Boussinesq approximation in periodic Cartesian boxes.

Result: Thermohaline convection can generate magnetic fields when the magnetic Prandtl number is large (low magnetic diffusivity), but they are likely too weak to explain observed magnetic white dwarfs. Strong magnetic fields can nonetheless be advected outwards by the convection without being destroyed, depending on the net magnetic flux.

Conclusion: Crystallization-induced thermohaline mixing may transport magnetic fields initially trapped in white dwarf interiors to their outer layers, making them observable. Additional simulations with more realistic geometry and magnetic field configurations are needed to confirm this possibility.

Abstract: The origins of strong magnetic fields observed in many white dwarfs are uncertain. Recent observations show that such fields are more common for white dwarfs with crystallized cores. Crystallization creates a destabilizing composition gradient that drives thermohaline convection in the star's fluid envelope, which may play an important role in magnetic field emergence. We perform 3D magnetohydrodynamical simulations of thermohaline convection with magnetic fields of various strengths and orientations, using the Boussinesq approximation in periodic Cartesian boxes. Our simulations suggest that thermohaline convection can generate magnetic fields when the magnetic Prandtl number is large (i.e., low magnetic diffusivity), but they are likely too weak to account for those observed in magnetic white dwarfs. However, the simulations indicate that strong magnetic fields can be advected outwards by the thermohaline convection without being destroyed, depending on the net magnetic flux. Hence, crystallization-induced thermohaline mixing may transport magnetic fields initially trapped in white dwarf interiors to their outer layers, allowing them to be observed. However, additional simulations using more realistic geometry and magnetic field configurations will be needed to confirm this possibility.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.mtrl-sci'></div>

# cond-mat.mtrl-sci [[Back]](#toc)

### [154] [Let CSP Be Your ANCHOR: Adaptive Crystal Search over Frozen Structure Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33407)
*Emma Lei Hovmand,Jonas Elsborg,Melih Kandemir,Arghya Bhowmik*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: ANCHOR separates crystal discovery into a frozen CSP physical prior and a GRPO composition search policy with adaptive novelty rewards, raising MSUN from 11.4% to 47.6% and SUN from 1.1% to 22.1%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: De novo crystal generation (DNG) models conflate two distinct tasks: searching composition space and generating structures with one set of weights. The authors argue discovery is better served by separating these, so a physical prior (CSP model) guides structure generation while rewards (including novelty) act on a composition search policy.

Method: ANCHOR: a GRPO composition policy trained with multi-objective rewards around a frozen CSP model. CAN (Continuous Adaptive Novelty): a graded novelty score measured against known structures and a growing discovery history. Experiments replace DNG compositions with ANCHOR's policy on the same CSP backbone, compare fine-tuning DNG directly on rewards, test KL-regularized vs unregularized DNG fine-tuning, and route a stability-only reward into ANCHOR's CSP backbone.

Result: ANCHOR raises MSUN from 11.4% to 47.6% and SUN from 1.1% to 22.1% at 99.9% formula uniqueness. Fine-tuning DNG directly on the same rewards only moves the composition marginal without raising on-hull fraction. KL-regularized DNG fine-tuning can only reweight existing chemistry by a bounded factor; unregularized fine-tuning moves toward known or less stable chemistry. Stability-only reward into ANCHOR's backbone roughly halves SUN. Under MatterGen's pipeline, ANCHOR raises SOTA MSUN from 29.2% to 41.3%, transfers without retraining to two further CSP backbones, and reaches 47.1% after distillation into Crystalite-CSP.

Conclusion: Separating composition search from structure generation is key to improving crystal discovery. Adaptation should act on the composition policy rather than the CSP prior, and likelihood training on structures found during search can improve a CSP backbone. On-hull rates depend on the potential used, as with any model optimized against it.

Abstract: De novo crystal generation (DNG) models decide where to search in composition space and how to generate structures with one set of weights. We argue that discovery is better served by separating the two. A crystal structure prediction (CSP) model is a physical prior that should be improved by likelihood training, while rewards, including novelty measured against the search's own history, should act on a search over compositions. We introduce ANCHOR, a GRPO composition policy trained with multi-objective rewards around a frozen CSP model, and continuous adaptive novelty (CAN), a graded novelty score against known structures and a growing discovery history. Using the frozen CSP model as a fixed ruler under one evaluator, we test where adaptation should act. Replacing DNG compositions with ANCHOR's policy on the same CSP backbone raises MSUN from 11.4% to 47.6% and SUN from 1.1% to 22.1% at 99.9% formula uniqueness. Fine-tuning DNG models directly on the same rewards instead moves their composition marginal without raising their on-hull fraction. We show that KL-regularized fine-tuning of a DNG model can only reweight chemistry the pretrained model already supports by a bounded factor, while unregularized DNG fine-tunes move toward known or less stable chemistry. Even a stability-only reward routed into ANCHOR's CSP backbone roughly halves SUN relative to the frozen backbone, whereas likelihood training on structures found during search can improve a CSP backbone. Under MatterGen's evaluation pipeline, ANCHOR raises state-of-the-art MSUN from 29.2% to 41.3%, transfers without retraining to two further CSP backbones, and reaches 47.1% after distillation into Crystalite-CSP. As with any model optimised against a potential, its on-hull rate depends on that potential.

</details>


### [155] [Native implementation of the machine-learned Skala exchange-correlation functional in CP2K: Unified one-centre reconstruction for molecular and condensed-phase calculations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34055)
*Johann Pototschnig,Franz Pöschel,Jürg Hutter,Thomas D. Kühne*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The machine-learned Skala exchange-correlation functional is implemented natively in CP2K (GPW/GAPW) with periodic boundary conditions, achieving molecular-level accuracy and good band gaps for condensed-phase systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge molecular Skala accuracy with condensed-phase electronic structure calculations, enabling periodic many-body reference data development.

Method: Native CP2K implementation of Skala XC via GPW/GAPW; joint one-centre reconstruction of density, gradients, and kinetic-energy density; XC-specific GAPW representation on atom-centred grids; includes Brillouin-zone sampling and point-group symmetry reduction; tested with all-electron and pseudopotential/mixed-core setups.

Result: All-electron Skala-D3(BJ): MAE 1.54 kJ/mol vs DMC for CO2, NH3, urea; DMC-ICE13 MAEs 1.16 kJ/mol (absolute) and 0.82 kJ/mol (relative to ice Ih); band-gap MAE 0.43 eV on 15 materials; LC10 shows systematically underestimated lattice constants (structural overbinding).

Conclusion: The framework connects molecular Skala to condensed-phase electronic structure, matches earlier GauXC molecular accuracy, and enables future development with periodic many-body data—though structural overbinding remains to be addressed.

Abstract: We implement the machine-learned Skala exchange-correlation (XC) functional natively in CP2K using its Gaussian and plane-wave (GPW) and Gaussian and augmented-plane-wave (GAPW) methods. A joint one-centre reconstruction of density, density gradients, and kinetic-energy density before functional evaluation preserves mixed gradient terms and nonlocal couplings between smooth and atom-local contributions in all-electron and pseudopotential calculations. An XC-specific GAPW representation resolves rapidly varying local contributions on atom-centred grids, reducing plane-wave requirements for pseudopotential calculations. The implementation includes Brillouin-zone sampling and point-group symmetry reduction. With sufficiently flexible orbital bases, it retains the molecular benchmark accuracy of our earlier GauXC formulation. All-electron Skala-D3(BJ) calculations yield a mean absolute error (MAE) of 1.54 kJ mol$^{-1}$ against diffusion Monte Carlo for crystalline CO$_2$, NH$_3$, and urea, spanning dispersion, quadrupolar electrostatics, and hydrogen bonding. For all thirteen DMC-ICE13 phases, the MAEs are 1.16 kJ mol$^{-1}$ for absolute lattice energies and 0.82 kJ mol$^{-1}$ for the twelve relative energies to ice Ih. At fixed experimental geometries, all-electron/mixed-core calculations give a band-gap MAE of 0.43 eV on a 15-material set, substantially below commonly used semilocal functionals and comparable to widely used hybrid functionals. The LC10 benchmark nevertheless reveals systematically underestimated equilibrium lattice constants, indicating structural overbinding. This framework connects molecular Skala to condensed-phase electronic structure and enables future development using periodic many-body reference data.

</details>


### [156] [Chemical site bases and average-atom potentials for the atomic cluster expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34869)
*Lorenzo Piersante,Anirudh Raju Natarajan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper generalizes the atomic cluster expansion (ACE) to an arbitrary chemical site basis and derives an exact mapping to average-atom potentials. The occupational basis is most data-efficient and gives the best average-atom description of disordered alloys, though all bases converge in the large-data limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interatomic potentials, including ACE, conventionally use a one-hot chemical representation producing chemically stratified models. Alternative chemical bases from on-lattice cluster expansions have not been tested for interatomic potentials, leaving the role of chemical basis choice unexplored.

Method: The authors revisit multicomponent ACE for an arbitrary chemical site basis, establish an exact analytical mapping between a fitted linear ACE and the average-atom potential of a perfectly random alloy, and benchmark occupational, Chebyshev, and conventional bases.

Result: Benchmarks on Mg-Nd (solute binding, vacancy formation), and Mo-Nb and Cr-W (mixing enthalpies) show that with scarce data the occupational basis converges fastest and best controls targeted properties, while conventional and Chebyshev bases struggle. The occupational basis also gives the most reliable average-atom thermodynamics. All three bases perform identically with large data.

Conclusion: The chemical basis is a design choice governing data efficiency, and explicitly treating it enables average-atom potentials for thermodynamic, mechanical, and kinetic properties of concentrated alloys.

Abstract: Interatomic potentials are central tools in the atomistic modeling of materials. The atomic cluster expansion (ACE) parameterizes such potentials from ab initio data, conventionally encoding the chemical degrees of freedom with a one-hot representation that yields chemically stratified models. The alternative chemical representations used in on-lattice configurational cluster expansions have not been assessed for interatomic potentials. Here we revisit the multicomponent ACE for an arbitrary chemical site basis. We then establish an exact analytical mapping between a fitted linear ACE and the average-atom potential that describes a perfectly random alloy. We benchmark potentials built on the occupational, Chebyshev, and conventional ACE bases against solute binding and vacancy formation energies in Mg-Nd, and against the mixing enthalpies of the Mo-Nb and Cr-W solid solutions. When training data are scarce, the occupational basis converges fastest and offers the best control over targeted material properties, while the conventional and Chebyshev bases face challenges in reproducing these properties. The occupational basis likewise yields the most reliable average-atom description of disordered alloy thermodynamics. In the large-data limit the three bases perform identically. The chemical basis is therefore a design choice that governs data efficiency. Its explicit treatment opens a route to average-atom potentials for the thermodynamic, mechanical, and kinetic properties of concentrated alloys.

</details>


### [157] [Density functional perturbation theory of meta-generalized gradient approximations using algorithmic differentiation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35572)
*Bruno Ploumhans,Niklas Frederik Schmitz,Michael F. Herbst*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A density functional perturbation theory (DFPT) implementation for exchange-correlation functionals depending on both density and kinetic energy density (meta-GGAs), using algorithmic differentiation to avoid deriving cumbersome second-derivative expressions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DFPT is an established framework for computing derivatives in plane-wave DFT, but extending it to meta-GGAs and broader nonlocal functionals such as $E_\mathrm{xc}(\rho,\tau)$ is difficult because it requires tedious derivation of XC second energy derivative expressions.

Method: The authors implement DFPT for XC functionals with explicit dependence on density $\rho$ and kinetic energy density $\tau$. They avoid deriving cumbersome XC second derivative expressions by recasting these derivatives as a Jacobian-vector product of the XC potentials, evaluated via algorithmic differentiation (AD). This integrates with their previously developed AD-DFPT framework to enable derivatives of arbitrary ground-state quantities with respect to arbitrary perturbations.

Result: Using AD-DFPT, they compute response properties for ZnO and BaTiO3, finding that r2SCAN01 meta-GGA generally outperforms LDA and PBE. They also optimize a neural-network meta-GGA to self-consistently reproduce hybrid-DFT reference densities of bulk silicon using AD-DFPT gradients.

Conclusion: AD-DFPT is established as a versatile route for computing DFT derivatives at the meta-GGA level, applicable to both common response properties and the unusual derivatives needed for gradient-based training of novel XC functionals.

Abstract: Density functional perturbation theory (DFPT) is an established framework for the computation of derivatives in plane-wave density functional theory. We present an implementation of DFPT for exchange-correlation (XC) functionals $E_\mathrm{xc}(ρ,τ)$ that incorporate an explicit dependence on both the density $ρ$ and the kinetic energy density $τ$. This covers the popular class of semilocal meta-generalized gradient approximations (meta-GGAs) as well as broader nonlocal parametrizations. We sidestep the derivation of cumbersome XC second energy derivative expressions by recasting these derivatives as a Jacobian-vector product of the XC potentials, which we evaluate with algorithmic differentiation (AD) techniques. Integration with our previously developed AD-DFPT framework [N. F. Schmitz et al., npj Comput. Mater. 12, 6 (2026)] provides access to derivatives of arbitrary ground state quantities with respect to arbitrary perturbations. We employ AD-DFPT to compute a range of response properties for ZnO and BaTiO3, and find that the recent r2SCAN01 meta-GGA functional generally outperforms LDA and PBE. Finally, we showcase the optimization of a neural-network meta-GGA to self-consistently reproduce hybrid-DFT reference densities of bulk silicon, using AD-DFPT gradients. Overall, these results establish AD-DFPT as a versatile route for computing DFT derivatives at the meta-GGA level, be they common response properties or the unusual derivatives required for the gradient-based training of novel XC functionals.

</details>


<div id='math.DS'></div>

# math.DS [[Back]](#toc)

### [158] [An Analytical Framework for a Multi-Particle Oja Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33541)
*Sixu Li*

Main category: math.DS

TL;DR: The paper builds a unified analytical framework for the multi-particle Oja flow, a family of interacting particle systems, covering well-posedness, mean-field convergence, and low-dimensional invariant structures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The same interaction structure appears in higher-order synchronization, opinion dynamics, and Transformer self-attention, so a unified mathematical framework is needed to analyze these seemingly disparate systems.

Method: Establish well-posedness of finite-particle and mean-field systems; prove finite-to-mean-field convergence as particle number grows; derive a time-dependent normalized linear transformation representation of the mean-field solution via a matrix ODE; identify invariant parametric families; reduce dynamics to family parameters, exemplified by the angular central Gaussian family.

Result: The mean-field solution is represented by a normalized linear transformation of the initial distribution obeying a matrix ODE, and broad parametric families are shown to be invariant, allowing reduced descriptions; this is concretely derived for the angular central Gaussian family.

Conclusion: The framework provides a unified treatment of multi-particle Oja flows and yields reduced parameter dynamics that describe several representative systems.

Abstract: We develop an analytical framework for studying a family of interacting particle systems that we refer to as the multi-particle Oja flow. The common interaction structure underlying this family arises in a variety of applications, including higher-order synchronization models, opinion dynamics, and self-attention dynamics derived from Transformer architectures, motivating a unified mathematical framework for their analysis. We first establish well-posedness for both the finite-particle and the mean-field systems. We also prove convergence of the finite-particle dynamics to the mean-field equation as the number of particles tends to infinity. We then investigate low-dimensional structures underlying the dynamics. In particular, we show that the mean-field solution admits a representation through a time-dependent normalized linear transformation acting on the initial distribution, with the transformation governed by a matrix ODE. Building on this representation, we identify a broad class of parametric families that are invariant under the mean-field dynamics. When the initial distribution belongs to one of these families, the evolution remains within the same family for all time and can therefore be characterized through the dynamics of the corresponding family parameters. As a concrete example, we develop this reduction within the angular central Gaussian family and illustrate how the resulting parameter dynamics provide reduced descriptions of the mean-field evolution for several representative systems.

</details>


<div id='astro-ph.HE'></div>

# astro-ph.HE [[Back]](#toc)

### [159] [Jet Forecast: Timing of Discrete Jet Ejections of Stellar-Mass Black Holes Anticipated via X-ray Properties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34947)
*Toshihiro Kawaguchi,Kazutaka Yamaoka*

Main category: astro-ph.HE

TL;DR: Radio–soft γ-ray data of XTE J1859+226 suggest discrete jets launch when the disk inner radius (R_in) rapidly approaches the ISCO; this behavior is tested across XTE J1550-564, GX 339-4, and MAXI J1820+070.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior phenomenological findings link jet ejections to the rapid shrinkage of R_in to the ISCO, but it was unclear whether this holds for other black hole binaries. Clarifying this could enable predicting jet ejection timing.

Method: The authors investigated X-ray and radio data of XTE J1550-564, GX 339-4, and MAXI J1820+070, comparing R_in, X-ray rms variability, hardness, and accretion rate behavior relative to jet activity.

Result: All four sources show drops in R_in and rms 1–10 days before jet activity, with no huge jumps in accretion rate before ejections. XTE J1859 and MAXI J1820 show jets under sub-Eddington accretion. A curved rms–R_in correlation is common across multiple sources.

Conclusion: Models requiring enhanced accretion rate or super-Eddington accretion for jet production are unlikely. Monitoring R_in can predict jet ejection timing; when R_in and rms decrease continuously and R_in nears the ISCO, a "jet forecast" can trigger Target-of-Opportunity observations.

Abstract: The radio -- soft $γ$-ray data of the Galactic black hole binary XTE J1859+226 showed that discrete jets are launched when the inner radius of the accretion disk ($R_{\rm in}$) rapidly approaches the innermost stable circular orbit (ISCO). Given the correlations among the X-ray fractional variability amplitude (rms), $R_{\rm in}$, and the X-ray hardness, previous phenomenological findings related to jet ejections (``jet line'' and sharp drops of rms) also indicate that the rapid shrinkage of $R_{\rm in}$ down to the ISCO turns on the jet formation. In order to make clear how these properties appear in other objects, we investigated the X-ray and radio data of XTE J1550-564, GX 339-4, and MAXI J1820+070. We found that all the four sources show drops of $R_{\rm in}$ and rms 1-10 days ahead of a jet activity, and exhibit no huge jumps of the accretion rates prior to jet ejections. XTE J1859 and MAXI J1820 show the jet activities under sub-Eddington accretion. Models for the jet production that require an enhancement of $\dot{M}$ or a super-Eddington accretion rate are unlikely. Another behavior common to multiple sources is the curved shape of the rms--$R_{\rm in}$ correlation. Therefore, monitoring of $R_{\rm in}$ seems to be suitable to predict the timing of jet ejections. When both $R_{\rm in}$ and rms continuously decrease and when $R_{\rm in}$ is about to reach the ISCO, alerts can be circulated as a ``jet forecast'' for triggering Target-of-Opportunity observations.

</details>


<div id='quant-ph'></div>

# quant-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [160] [Conserved and relaxing modes enable polynomial-success coherent Carleman lattice Boltzmann evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32310)
*Hua-Dong Yao,Sauro Succi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A quantum Carleman lattice Boltzmann scheme using a weighted Hermite moment basis enables a norm-attaining block encoding with near-unity single-step success probability and polynomial (not exponential) overhead across steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing population-space Carleman encodings for nonlinear lattice Boltzmann collision dynamics suffer from low postselection probabilities, making coherent multi-step evolution exponentially unlikely to succeed. A new encoding with favorable success scaling is needed.

Method: Carleman linearization is applied to the multi-relaxation-time collision operator in a weighted Hermite moment basis. In this basis the lifted collision operator decomposes into small coupled blocks whose norm exceeds unity due to conserved-relaxing mode coupling, enabling a norm-attaining block encoding. The pair sector is scaled for a prescribed evolution horizon.

Result: Single-step success probability is close to unity. Combined encoding and sector-selection overhead grows polynomially rather than exponentially with step count. For 100-step evolution, complete-run success probability exceeds population-space encodings by orders of magnitude. Over tens of steps, truncated dynamics reproduces nonlinearly generated modes of a benchmark flow within a few percent.

Conclusion: The combined preparation, evolution, and readout protocol is verified as a single unitary circuit with full gate-level resource estimates for extracting a nonlinear observable. The block structure extends to three-dimensional lattices, offering a constructive route to norm-attaining encodings for lattice Boltzmann models with quadratic equilibria.

Abstract: Carleman linearization represents nonlinear lattice Boltzmann collision dynamics in a form amenable to quantum block encoding, but low postselection probabilities in existing population-space encodings make coherent multi-step evolution exponentially unlikely to succeed. We present a quantum Carleman lattice Boltzmann scheme based on the multi-relaxation-time collision operator in a weighted Hermite moment basis. In this basis, the lifted collision operator separates into small coupled blocks whose norm exceeds unity because of the coupling between conserved and relaxing modes. This block structure enables a norm-attaining block encoding with a single-step success probability close to unity. Crucially, scaling the pair sector for a prescribed evolution horizon makes the combined encoding and sector-selection overhead grow polynomially rather than exponentially with the number of steps. For a hundred-step evolution, the resulting complete-run success probability exceeds that of population-space encodings by orders of magnitude. Over horizons of several tens of steps, the truncated dynamics reproduces the nonlinearly generated modes of a benchmark flow to within a few percent. We verify the combined preparation, evolution and readout protocol as a single unitary circuit and give a complete gate-level resource estimate for extracting a nonlinear observable of the benchmark flow from a coherent Carleman evolution. The block structure of conserved and relaxing modes holds equally on three-dimensional lattices and provides a constructive route to norm-attaining encodings for lattice Boltzmann models with quadratic equilibria.

</details>


### [161] [Quantum-Computing Self-Consistent Kohn-Sham DFT: Plane-Wave-Orthonormalized Orbitals with a Dual-Basis Quantum Eigensolver](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33934)
*Lazaro Calderin*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: A first-quantization, all-electron, full-potential Kohn-Sham DFT method for quantum computers using a dual-basis quantum-circuit eigensolver, validated on H8 chains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum computers could enable all-electron, full-potential Kohn-Sham DFT, but existing quantum approaches struggle with large basis sets and self-consistent field eigensolving.

Method: Combines plane-wave-orthonormalized-orbital (PWOO) basis (plane waves plus orthonormalized atomic orbitals) with a classically optimized quantum-circuit eigensolver having a dual-basis form: bootstrap in full basis, then solve each SCF step in a contracted basis of K functions (ceil(log2 K) qubits), updating in full basis when residual exceeds a threshold.

Result: For H8 (SVWN, K=8), state-vector-emulated SCF matches classical results within 0.02 mHa for 32768 plane waves and PWOO bases up to cc-pVTZ; contraction shifts energies by at most 5.7 μHa. With emulated shot noise, STO-3G PWOO SCF converges near noise-free fixed point. On IonQ Forte-1, three-qubit-register readout reproduces noise-free PWOO-AO energy difference within one standard deviation.

Conclusion: The method provides an accurate, hardware-validated path to quantum-computed all-electron Kohn-Sham DFT with compact qubit requirements.

Abstract: We present a first-quantization, all-electron, full-potential Kohn-Sham density functional theory method for quantum computers, based on a classically optimized quantum-circuit eigensolver for the self-consistent field (SCF) eigenproblems in any orthonormal basis. The plane-wave-orthonormalized-orbital (PWOO) basis combines plane waves with atomic orbitals (AOs) orthonormalized against them and one another. The eigensolver has a dual-basis form: after full-basis bootstrap steps, it solves each SCF step in a contracted basis of $K$ functions ($\lceil\log_2 K\rceil$ qubits), updated in the full basis when the residual outside it exceeds a threshold. For an H$_8$ chain (SVWN, $K=8$), the state-vector-emulated SCF agrees with classical calculations within 0.02 mHa for 32768 plane waves and PWOO bases up to cc-pVTZ; contraction changes these PWOO energies by at most 5.7 $μ$Ha. With emulated shot noise, the STO-3G PWOO SCF converges near the noise-free fixed point. On IonQ Forte-1, three-qubit-register readout reproduces the noise-free PWOO-AO energy difference within one standard deviation.

</details>


### [162] [Breaking the Curse of Dimensionality in Quantum PDE Solvers via Gevrey Regularity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07893)
*Pooya Ronagh,Mariia Sobchuk,Xiaoran Li,Grecia Castelazo,Ala Shayeghi*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper links smoothness of periodic functions to qubit requirements for high-precision Fourier-basis amplitude encoding, using the Gevrey hierarchy to build and analyze quantum PDE solvers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-precision Fourier-basis amplitude encodings of real-valued periodic functions depend critically on their smoothness, and understanding this relationship is key to designing efficient high-precision quantum algorithms. The space between smooth and analytic functions lacked a natural organizing class for this purpose.

Method: The authors connect degrees of function smoothness to qubit counts for high-precision Fourier encoding via the Gevrey hierarchy. They use Gevrey regularity to analyze a quantum algorithm for general linear PDEs with periodic boundary conditions, then extend it to Dirichlet boundary conditions using thin Gevrey collars and a regularized inverse.

Result: The approach yields resource bounds that specify precision-dependent Fourier cutoffs and account for restricted discrete stability, elementary gate costs, and restriction to the Dirichlet domain. It is applied to isotropic and anisotropic Poisson equations with periodic and Dirichlet boundary conditions.

Conclusion: Gevrey regularity provides a natural and effective class for high-precision quantum algorithms, enabling analysis and extension of quantum PDE solvers across periodic and Dirichlet boundary conditions.

Abstract: We connect different degrees of smoothness of real-valued periodic functions to the number of qubits required for their high-precision Fourier-basis amplitude encodings. Our central observation is that the Gevrey hierarchy, which stratifies the space between smooth and analytic functions, provides a natural class for high-precision quantum algorithms. We use Gevrey regularity to analyze a quantum algorithm for solving general linear partial differential equations (PDEs) with periodic boundary conditions. We then extend the solver to PDEs with Dirichlet boundary conditions using thin Gevrey collars and a regularized inverse. Our resource bounds specify precision-dependent Fourier cutoffs and account for restricted discrete stability, elementary gate costs, and restriction to the Dirichlet domain. Finally, we apply our approach to isotropic and anisotropic Poisson equations with periodic and Dirichlet boundary conditions.

</details>


### [163] [Contour-integral and Fourier transform based multivariable quantum eigenvalue transformation for commuting matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32262)
*Shan Jiang,Dong An*

Main category: quant-ph

TL;DR: This paper studies implementing multivariable matrix-valued functions on quantum computers, proposing two algorithms for matrix eigenvalue transformations on tuples of pairwise commuting matrices, with complexity analyses and hybrid quantum-classical variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by the need to implement multivariable matrix-valued functions on quantum computers, generalizing single-variable matrix function techniques to the multivariable setting for tuples of pairwise commuting matrices.

Method: Two quantum algorithms: (1) based on multivariable contour integrals, applicable to arbitrary holomorphic functions on C^n; (2) based on high-dimensional Fourier transforms, designed for smooth functions of commuting Hermitian matrices. Both leverage quantum singular value transformation, compression gadgets, and linear combination of unitaries. Hybrid quantum-classical variants are also studied.

Result: Complexity of quantum implementations is discussed and analyzed. Hybrid variants reduce ancilla qubit requirements to logarithmic dependence on the number of variables, at the cost of increased query complexity. Multivariable matrix polynomials can be implemented with no additional explicit degree dependence, depending only on global properties of the polynomials.

Conclusion: The work provides quantum algorithms for multivariable matrix eigenvalue transformations with analyzed complexities and trade-offs, and demonstrates an application to multivariable matrix polynomials.

Abstract: We study the problem of implementing multivariable matrix-valued functions on a quantum computer and propose two quantum algorithms for multivariable matrix eigenvalue transformations acting on tuples of pairwise commuting matrices. The first algorithm is based on multivariable contour integrals and applies to arbitrary holomorphic functions on $\mathbb{C}^n$. The second algorithm is based on high-dimensional Fourier transforms and designed for smooth functions of commuting Hermitian matrices. For both algorithms, we discuss and analyze the complexity of their quantum implementation leveraging quantum singular value transformation, compression gadgets, and linear combination of unitaries. We additionally study their hybrid quantum--classical variants that reduce the number of required ancilla qubits to logarithmic dependence on the number of variables, at the cost of increased query complexity. As an application, we show that multivariable matrix polynomials can be implemented with no additional explicit degree dependence, depending only on global properties of the polynomials.

</details>


<div id='stat.ML'></div>

# stat.ML [[Back]](#toc)

### [164] [AECSF: Adaptive Ensemble Conditional Score Filtering for High-Dimensional Nonlinear Data Assimilation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32411)
*Yangwen Zhang,Shiwei Ni,Xiaoping Zhang,Xiaofei Guan,Lili Ju*

Main category: stat.ML

TL;DR: AECSF is a training-free adaptive ensemble conditional score filter for high-dimensional nonlinear Bayesian state estimation, using the conditional Tweedie identity and an adaptive weighted proposal ensemble to improve posterior sampling accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Particle filters suffer weight collapse, Gaussian ensemble updates miss non-Gaussian structure, and existing training-free score filters rely on heuristic likelihood corrections, compromising posterior accuracy by neglecting uncertainty of the system state for each noisy reverse particle.

Method: AECSF builds an analytically tractable score estimator from the conditional Tweedie identity, recasting noisy posterior score estimation as estimating the conditional mean of the system state given a noisy reverse particle and observation. It uses a shared adaptive weighted proposal ensemble with particle-specific conditional weights, updated within the same reverse-diffusion run, avoiding separate proposal sampling.

Result: Theoretically, the paper characterizes when a fixed weighted proposal measure yields the exact noisy posterior score, and derives a bound relating conditional-mean estimation errors to reverse-sampling endpoint error. Numerical experiments show AECSF improves posterior sampling and nonlinear filtering accuracy in high-dimensional problems with limited forecast ensembles.

Conclusion: AECSF offers an accurate, training-free score-based filtering approach for high-dimensional nonlinear systems with limited ensembles, bridging statistical fidelity and computational tractability.

Abstract: Bayesian state estimation for high-dimensional nonlinear dynamical systems entails a fundamental tension between statistical fidelity and computational tractability, as particle weights can collapse, while Gaussian ensemble updates can miss non-Gaussian posterior structure. Score-based diffusion filters offer a sampling-based alternative, but existing training-free score filters often rely on heuristic likelihood corrections, which can compromise posterior accuracy by neglecting uncertainty about the system state associated with each noisy reverse particle. To address these issues, we propose AECSF, a training-free adaptive ensemble conditional score filter. AECSF constructs an analytically tractable score estimator from the conditional Tweedie identity, which recasts noisy posterior score estimation as estimating the conditional mean of the system state given a noisy reverse particle and the observation. To estimate these conditional means efficiently, AECSF employs a shared adaptive weighted proposal ensemble, while particle-specific conditional weights yield an estimate for each noisy reverse particle without separate proposal sampling. The proposal ensemble is updated using reverse-particle information within the same reverse-diffusion run to improve conditional-mean estimation. Theoretically, we characterize when a fixed weighted proposal measure yields the exact noisy posterior score. Under stated assumptions, we establish a bound relating conditional-mean estimation errors to reverse-sampling endpoint error. Numerical experiments demonstrate that AECSF improves the accuracy of posterior sampling and nonlinear filtering in high-dimensional problems with limited forecast ensembles.

</details>


### [165] [Calibrated Derivative-Process Sensitivity for Gaussian-Process Variable Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33549)
*Jia Cai*

Main category: stat.ML

TL;DR: This paper proposes a calibrated input-selection rule for GP regression based on derivative sensitivity, using a studentized multiplier bootstrap to fix anti-conservative cutoffs at null inputs, achieving asymptotic FWER/FDR control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ARD lengthscales rank inputs by function variability, not predictive contribution, and give no calibrated rule for which inputs to keep. The derivative-sensitivity alternative is available in closed form but suffers from degenerate quadratic-form estimators at null inputs, making standard cutoffs anti-conservative and residual bootstrap mis-scaled.

Method: A studentized multiplier bootstrap of the GP derivative process is introduced, with validity proved via an invariance principle for quadratic forms, yielding asymptotic family-wise and FDR control across inputs; a block-averaged variant achieves linear-in-n cost.

Result: Over 100 replications the rule controls FDR at null inputs, while uncalibrated rankings breach it by up to 2x and Bernstein-von Mises by 2.2x; matched-FDR power is preserved; validity holds under Matérn kernels and input correlation up to 0.99; on real data it admits 5-12x fewer spurious inputs, costing 5-18% of the GP fit.

Conclusion: A studentized multiplier bootstrap provides a principled, calibrated, computationally affordable input-selection rule that dominates ARD-style and uncalibrated derivative approaches.

Abstract: Automatic relevance determination (ARD), the default tool for variable selection in Gaussian-process (GP) regression, ranks inputs by inverse lengthscales -- which measure how fast a function varies, not how much an input contributes to prediction -- and offers no calibrated rule for deciding which inputs to keep. The prediction-centred alternative, the derivative sensitivity $ν_j = \mathbb{E}[(\partial f/\partial x_j)^2]$, is available in closed form from a fitted GP, but turning it into a selection rule is harder than it looks: at a null input the estimator is a degenerate quadratic form, so Wald and Bernstein-von Mises cutoffs are anti-conservative, and the natural residual bootstrap is mis-scaled. We show that a studentized multiplier bootstrap of the GP derivative process repairs both, prove its validity through an invariance principle for quadratic forms, and obtain asymptotic family-wise and false-discovery-rate control across inputs. Over 100 replications the rule controls FDR wherever inputs are truly null, while uncalibrated derivative rankings breach the target by up to 2x and a Bernstein-von Mises cutoff by 2.2x; at matched FDR it loses no power; it holds under a Matérn kernel and input correlation up to 0.99; on real data with planted and authentic null inputs it admits 5-12x fewer spurious inputs; it costs 5-18% of the GP fit; and a block-averaged variant retains validity at cost linear in $n$.

</details>


<div id='math.RA'></div>

# math.RA [[Back]](#toc)

### [166] [Algebras with Variable Structural Constants and Their Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31627)
*Vitalii Shpakivskyi*

Main category: math.RA

TL;DR: Introduces a new class of algebras with variable structural constants and develops monogenic function theory within them, linking such functions to solutions of second-order PDEs with variable coefficients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extends classical monogenic (hypercomplex-analytic) function theory, which traditionally relies on algebras with constant structural constants, to more general algebras with variable structural constants.

Method: Defines the new algebra class, establishes compatibility conditions for the structural functions, and derives the correspondence between monogenic functions and second-order PDEs with variable coefficients (including converse results).

Result: Shows that components of monogenic functions satisfy certain second-order PDEs with variable coefficients, proves the converse, and provides a constructive method for explicit solutions.

Conclusion: The approach offers a constructive tool for solving variable-coefficient PDEs, illustrated by several examples.

Abstract: We introduce a new class of algebras with variable structural constants and develop elements of the theory of monogenic functions in such algebras. Compatibility conditions for the structural functions are established. It is shown that the components of monogenic functions satisfy certain second-order partial differential equations with variable coefficients, and converse results are proved. This approach provides a constructive method for obtaining explicit solutions of such equations. Several illustrative examples are presented.

</details>


### [167] [Algebras with Variable Operator-Valued Structural Constants and Their Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31628)
*Vitalii Shpakivskyi*

Main category: math.RA

TL;DR: Introduces algebras with variable operator-valued structural constants and a theory of monogenic functions, linking them to operator-differential equations and providing constructive solution methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extends classical monogenic function theory, traditionally based on algebras with constant structural constants, to a more general setting with variable operator-valued structural constants.

Method: Defines a new class of algebras with variable operator-valued structural constants; establishes compatibility conditions for the structural operators; derives second-order operator-differential equations with variable coefficients satisfied by monogenic function components and proves converse results.

Result: Monogenic function components satisfy second-order operator-differential equations with variable coefficients, with converse results proven; the approach yields a constructive method for solving differential, integro-differential, and functional-differential equations, illustrated by examples.

Conclusion: The proposed framework generalizes monogenic function theory and offers a constructive tool for obtaining solutions to various classes of equations.

Abstract: We introduce a new class of algebras with variable operator-valued structural constants and develop elements of the theory of monogenic functions in such algebras. Compatibility conditions for the structural operators are established. It is shown that the components of monogenic functions satisfy second-order operator-differential equations with variable coefficients, and converse results are proved. The proposed approach provides a constructive method for obtaining solutions of various differential, integro-differential, and functional-differential equations. Several illustrative examples are presented.

</details>


<div id='cs.DC'></div>

# cs.DC [[Back]](#toc)

### [168] [Performance vs Portability in Heterogeneous HPC Environments: Why Pre-execution Benchmarking is Required](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33847)
*Mindaugas Macernis*

Main category: cs.DC

TL;DR: This study benchmarks quantum chemistry software (precompiled executables) using Podman containerization versus on-system execution on HPC compute nodes, finding that performance hinges on compilation, startup overhead, and execution configuration rather than networking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cloud computing (Kubernetes-orchestrated) and HPC (Slurm batch schedulers) follow different paradigms, but growing demand for shared compute resources drives a need for interoperability between them. Users need practical tools to run containerized scientific applications on HPC systems.

Method: Podman is used as a user-accessible containerization tool to benchmark quantum chemistry software distributed as precompiled executables. Containerized execution is compared against on-system execution, across CPU-optimized builds and multiple compute nodes, without additional network optimizations.

Result: Performance depends on software compilation, startup overhead, and execution configuration. Container preparation can dominate short-task workflows, while long-running calculations require monitoring of computational progress.

Conclusion: Workload-specific benchmarking and user-level monitoring are important when deploying scientific applications in containers on HPC systems.

Abstract: Cloud computing and high-performance computing (HPC) typically follow different paradigms: cloud services are often orchestrated using Kubernetes, whereas HPC workloads are managed through batch schedulers such as Slurm. Growing demand for shared computational resources increases the need for interoperability between these environments. This study uses Podman as a user-accessible tool to benchmark quantum chemistry software distributed as precompiled executables. Containerized and on-system execution are compared across CPU-optimized builds and multiple compute nodes, without additional network optimizations. The results demonstrate that performance depends on software compilation, startup overhead, and execution configuration. Container preparation can dominate short-task workflows, while long-running calculations require monitoring of computational progress. These findings highlight the importance of workload-specific benchmarking and user-level monitoring when deploying scientific applications in containers on HPC systems.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [169] [Enhancing generalization in endwall film cooling prediction: Incorporating the superposition principle into transformer-based neural operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31633)
*Qineng Wang,Liming Song,Tianyuan Liu,Zhendong Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A physics-enhanced neural operator framework (SDNO) predicts turbine endwall film cooling temperature fields with a variable number of film holes, generalizing from 1–5 holes in training to 10–20 unseen holes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enhancing the generalization prediction ability of turbine endwall cooling layouts when the number of film holes varies, which conventional models struggle to handle.

Method: Inspired by the film cooling superposition principle, SDNO splits prediction into two stages: Calculate Net (a Transformer-based neural operator) predicts each sub-part temperature field, and Super Net combines them into the superposed full field. A signed distance function (SDF) encodes variable cooling hole locations instead of raw pixel contours.

Result: Trained on samples with 1–5 film holes at variable locations, SDNO accurately predicts film effectiveness for 10–20 holes unseen in training and outperforms the fully supervised baseline in accuracy.

Conclusion: The proposed physics-enhanced SDNO framework demonstrates strong generalization and improved accuracy, validating its effectiveness for turbine endwall film cooling prediction.

Abstract: In this study, a physics-enhanced neural operator framework is proposed to enhance the generalization prediction ability of the cooling layout of a turbine endwall with variable number of film holes. Specifically, inspired by the film cooling superposition principle, we propose a film cooling prediction model, namely superposition-based deep neural operator (SDNO), that divides the endwall temperature field prediction into two stages. In the first stage, the cooling layout of a turbine endwall is divided into several sub-parts with randomly assigned film holes, and a Transformer-based neural operator network, namely Calculate Net, is designed to predict the temperature field of each sub-part. Then, in the second stage, another neural operator network, i.e., Super Net, is trained to combine the temperature fields predicted by Calculate Net for each sub-part and obtain the superposed temperature field of the full cooling layout. Additionally, instead of directly taking the film cooling contours as pixel plots, a signed distance function (SDF) which is sensitive to the variable locations of cooling holes, is designed to encode the location information of cooling holes. Furthermore, the proposed endwall film cooling prediction model is trained with the samples that changing the number of film holes from 1-5 with variable locations. Then, the trained prediction shows excellent generalization prediction ability, which can accurately predict the film effectiveness of the cooling layout with 10-20 film cooling holes that are unseen in the training samples. The proposed SDNO also improves prediction accuracy relative to the fully supervised baseline. With the above, the effectiveness of our proposed prediction model has been well demonstrated.

</details>


### [170] [MinkowskiPE: Minkowski Positional Encoding for Spatiotemporal Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33804)
*Yuhao Li,Louie Hong Yao,Tianyi Shi,Hanqun Cao,Hongxia Hao,Zhen Zhao,Shengchao Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MinkowskiPE encodes joint spacetime geometry via Lorentz transformations parameterized by relative displacement, adding an explicit geometric bias to attention for spatiotemporal modeling across scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modeling spatiotemporal coupling is central to physical intelligence. Physics-motivated dynamical models offer strong priors but limited flexibility; learning-based architectures are flexible but treat spatiotemporal coupling implicitly.

Method: Minkowski Positional Encoding (MinkowskiPE) uses joint temporal and spatial coordinates to parameterize Lorentz transformations applied to query and key features. Attention scores thus depend on position only through relative spacetime displacement, giving global translation invariance, while keeping the standard dot-product interface compatible with efficient attention.

Result: Achieves best results on all nine multi-trajectory molecular dynamics evaluations and reduces KTH video-prediction MSE by 9.9% vs. the best baseline with roughly one-tenth the parameters.

Conclusion: MinkowskiPE combines flexible learning with an explicit geometric spacetime bias, improving microscopic and macroscopic spatiotemporal prediction efficiently.

Abstract: Modeling spatiotemporal coupling is a key challenge in building physical intelligence across scales, from microscopic to macroscopic. Existing models capture such structure broadly through physics-motivated dynamical formulations or learning-motivated architectures. The former provide stronger priors but may constrain flexibility, whereas the latter are more flexible but leave the spatiotemporal coupling largely implicit. We therefore seek an approach that combines flexible learning with an explicit geometric bias for jointly modeling time and space. To this end, we propose Minkowski Positional Encoding (MinkowskiPE), which uses joint temporal and spatial coordinates to parameterize Lorentz transformations applied to query and key features. With MinkowskiPE, the query-key attention score depends on position only through the relative spacetime displacement between the two tokens and is therefore invariant to global translation of the coordinates. This paradigm retains the standard dot-product attention interface and remains compatible with efficient attention implementations. We evaluate MinkowskiPE on microscopic molecular dynamics and macroscopic video prediction tasks, achieving the best results on all nine multi-trajectory molecular evaluations and reducing KTH video-prediction MSE by 9.9% relative to the best baseline while using roughly one-tenth as many parameters.

</details>


### [171] [E3J: An Efficient and Open-Source Backend for Euclidean Equivariant Operations on GPU and TPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35099)
*Olivier Peltre,Armand Picard,Adrien Pichard,Miguel Bragança,Luca Giacomoni,Valentin Heyraud,Zachary Weller-Davies,Christoph Brunken,Jules Tilly*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: e3j is an open-source Euclid-equivariance backend for geometric deep learning with JAX bindings for GPU/TPU, achieving state-of-the-art throughput via optimized CUDA and Pallas kernels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Equivariant geometric deep learning lacks fast, open-source backends, and running large-scale equivariant workloads on TPU architectures has been difficult.

Method: The library combines optimized CUDA and Pallas kernels with algorithmic improvements to accelerate both forward and backward paths, with JAX bindings for GPU and TPU.

Result: Up to 34% speed-up over cuEquivariance on water box NPT simulation with MACE; >80% efficiency over H100 max memory bandwidth on tensor products; >2x throughput on message-passing convolution forward vs. prior backends; >80% of TPUv6e memory bandwidth, up to 10x more than e3nn-jax.

Conclusion: e3j enables large-scale equivariant deep learning on TPU and delivers leading GPU performance, released open source under Apache 2.0 on GitHub and PyPI.

Abstract: We present e3j, a fast Euclid-equivariance backend for geometric deep learning applications with JAX bindings for GPU and TPU. Leveraging both optimized CUDA and Pallas kernels and algorithmic improvements, the library achieves state-of-the-art throughput and runtime on both forward and backward paths. On a machine learning interatomic potential (MLIP) use case, it outperforms established backends, measuring up to 34% speed-up over cuEquivariance on water box NPT simulation using MACE, while remaining fully open source. E3j achieves over 80% efficiency over the H100 maximum memory bandwidth on tensor product operations, and in many cases more than doubles throughput of message passing convolutions forward compared to previously available backends. In addition, with the release of dedicated Pallas TPU kernel, e3j opens the possibility of large scale equivariant deep learning workloads on TPU architectures, which has so far been difficult to achieve. Our benchmarks show that e3j also achieves over 80% of a TPUv6e memory bandwidth, up to one order of magnitude more than e3nn-jax. The library is available on GitHub, PyPI and is released under an open source Apache 2.0 license.

</details>


### [172] [Optimal Transport Dropout for Structured Predictive Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33377)
*Giacomo Lorenzon,Francesco Regazzoni*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OTD jointly learns a neural network's predictive mapping and the law of its latent perturbations by transporting perturbations from a simple reference distribution through a learnable flow, providing structured predictive uncertainty without posterior inference or ensembles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deterministic neural networks and neural operators give only point predictions with no reliability measure, yet predictive uncertainty stems from outcome variability, finite data, or model-class limitations. Monte Carlo dropout is convenient but its perturbation law is prescribed a priori and typically factorised across latent coordinates, limiting its structure.

Method: Optimal Transport Dropout (OTD) jointly learns the predictive mapping and the law of its latent perturbations. It starts from an independent reference distribution and transports latent perturbations through a learnable flow, propagating them through the predictive network to induce a structured predictive law. Training uses the strictly proper Energy Score, with a kinetic-action term geometrically regularising the transport.

Result: On synthetic benchmarks, OTD captures multimodal predictive distributions, gives meaningful dispersion under model misspecification, and shows contracting dispersion as data or model capacity grows. For a field-valued PDE surrogate, predictive dispersion aligns with spatial error patterns, yielding more accurate predictions and better-calibrated, substantially narrower intervals than MC dropout. On real-world regression benchmarks, it shows competitive accuracy and better probabilistic predictions than established baselines.

Conclusion: OTD offers a way to learn structured predictive uncertainty without explicit posterior inference or ensembles of independently trained predictors.

Abstract: Deterministic neural networks and neural operators provide point predictions with no intrinsic measure of reliability. Yet, predictive uncertainty may stem from irreducible outcome variability, finite data, or limitations of the chosen model class. Monte Carlo dropout offers a computationally convenient way to construct a predictive distribution through stochastic feature masking, without training multiple independent networks or explicitly inferring a posterior over model parameters. However, its perturbation law is largely prescribed a priori and typically factorised across latent coordinates. We introduce Optimal Transport Dropout (OTD), which instead learns the predictive mapping and the law of its latent perturbations jointly. Starting from a simple independent reference distribution, OTD transports latent perturbations through a learnable flow and propagates them through the predictive neural network, thereby inducing a structured predictive law. Training uses the strictly proper Energy Score, while a kinetic-action term geometrically regularises the transport. Synthetic benchmarks show that OTD captures multimodal predictive distributions, generates meaningful dispersion when the model is misspecified, and exhibits contracting dispersion as more training data or greater model capacity are provided. For a field-valued partial differential equation surrogate, predictive dispersion strongly aligns with the spatial pattern of prediction errors. On this task, compared with Monte Carlo dropout, OTD yields more accurate predictions and better-calibrated, substantially narrower intervals. On real-world regression benchmarks, it further shows competitive accuracy and better probabilistic predictions compared to established baselines. OTD therefore offers a way to learn structured predictive uncertainty without explicit posterior inference or ensembles of independently trained predictors.

</details>


### [173] [Structure-Adaptive Tree Field Integrators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34025)
*Millend Roy,Soham Samal,Ivan Zelich,Krzysztof Marcin Choromanski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: STAD-TFIs are near-linear algorithms that integrate tensor fields on trees with distance-dependent kernels using path backbones, vertex separators, and 2D FFTs, outperforming prior tree field integrators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient integration of tensor fields on trees with distance-dependent kernels is computationally costly; existing efficient tree field integrators (TFIs) do not fully exploit tree structure.

Method: Exploit tree structure via decompositions around path backbones and single vertex separators, and use 2D fast Fourier transforms to compute interactions jointly.

Result: More computationally efficient than regular TFI counterparts, validated by theoretical analysis and empirical evaluations: speed tests on synthetic trees, accelerated Sinkhorn-based Optimal Transport relaxations on real meshes, and Topological Attention Transformers for vision.

Conclusion: Among the first results showing fast TFI methods yield efficient and accurate relaxations of geodesic Sinkhorn-based Optimal Transport solutions.

Abstract: We present a new class of near-linear algorithms for efficiently integrating general tensor fields defined on trees with distance dependent kernels, the Structure-Adaptive Tree Field Integrators (STAD-TFIs). STAD-TFIs exploit the tree's underlying structure through decompositions built around path backbones and single vertex separators, and use two-dimensional fast Fourier transforms to compute interactions jointly. By exploiting this structural information, STAD-TFIs achieve more computationally efficient integration than their regular efficient tree field integrators (TFI) counterparts. We provide a detailed theoretical analysis of our proposed approach and complement it with an exhaustive empirical evaluation, ranging from speed tests on synthetic trees, through accelerated Sinkhorn-based relaxations of the Optimal Transport algorithms on real meshes, to Topological Attention Transformers for vision tasks. To the best of our knowledge, we provide some of the first results showing that efficient to compute and accurate relaxations of the geodesic Sinkhorn-based solutions of the Optimal Transport problem can be derived by applying fast TFI methods.

</details>


### [174] [Broken Symmetry in BF16 Attention: Why FlashAttention Gradients Blow Up Late in Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34272)
*Junlin Chen,Daize Dong,Huanwei Di,Haolong Jia,Jiawei Wu,Haotian Xie,Mingkai Zheng,Yang Li,Leshang Chen,Huishu Wang,Eric P. Xing,Hongyi Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BF16 FlashAttention-3 silently degrades pretraining via a broken softmax conservation law; GProj restores the zero-sum constraint with rank-one corrections, matching FP32 accuracy for 4.7% overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BF16 fused attention kernels like FlashAttention-3 are widely trusted, but they caused a 450M-parameter transformer trained on 50B tokens to suffer a thousandfold gradient norm blow-up and excess loss (0.2 nats above FP32), despite no NaNs. The authors investigate why this trusted kernel silently destabilizes training.

Method: The authors identify two causes: a fused multiply-add in the forward softmax (previously dismissed as an extreme-input NaN case, never fixed), and a broken conservation law—softmax score gradients should sum to zero per row, but BF16 rounding of the query gradient leaves a small nonzero sum that leaks the mean key into the gradient. They introduce GProj (gauge projection), which restores the zero-sum property after the cast using two rank-one corrections per row.

Result: Repairing the FMA stops the blow-up, but the query gradient remains wrong by more than its own size due to the conservation-law violation. GProj cuts median query/key gradient errors from 219%/13% to 0.34%/0.37%—on par with FP32 attention—at 4.7% more time per training step. In matched from-scratch runs, GProj trains to the same loss as FP32 attention, while FlashAttention-3 and key smoothing both destabilize.

Conclusion: BF16 fused attention's instability stems not just from precision loss but from a violated zero-sum conservation law that grows with late-training key magnitudes and sharper attention. GProj cheaply restores this law, matching FP32 training stability and loss at modest cost, offering a practical fix for BF16 attention kernels.

Abstract: BF16 is now standard in large-scale pretraining, including in fused attention kernels such as FlashAttention, and these kernels are widely trusted. When we used FlashAttention-3 to pretrain a 450M-parameter transformer on 50B tokens, however, we ran into a problem: training was healthy for 25B tokens, then the gradient norm grew a thousandfold and the loss ended 0.2 nats above FP32 attention, without a single NaN. Recomputing the attention backward of just two layers in FP32 removes almost all of the excess gradient. Part of the cause is known: a fused multiply-add in the forward softmax, so far treated as an extreme-input NaN case and never fixed in FlashAttention-3. Repairing it stops the blow-up, but the query gradient is still wrong by more than its own size, and training still drives attention logits to thousands of times their size under accurate gradients. The remaining error comes from a broken conservation law. The softmax score gradient sums to zero along every row, which makes the query gradient blind to where the keys sit as a group; rounding it to BF16 leaves a small nonzero sum that leaks the mean key into the gradient, and the leak grows exactly as late training makes keys large and attention sharp.
