We introduce GProj (gauge projection), which restores the zero sum after the cast with two rank-one corrections per row. It cuts the remaining median query/key gradient errors from 219%/13% to 0.34%/0.37%, on par with FP32 attention, for 4.7% more time per training step. In matched from-scratch runs it trains to the same loss as FP32 attention, while FlashAttention-3 and key smoothing both destabilize.

</details>


### [175] [Quasi Linear Kernel Attention with Infinite Capacity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35349)
*Nicolaj Rux,Johannes Hertrich,Sebastian Neumayer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a "capacity" metric to measure attention kernel expressivity, proves common quasi-linear kernels have finite capacity, and proposes additive kernels (spline/polynomial exponential) that achieve infinite capacity with quasi-linear computation via sorting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Softmax attention scales quadratically with sequence length. Kernel attention replaces softmax with general kernels to reduce cost, but existing quasi-linear kernels may sacrifice expressivity. The paper seeks kernels that retain attention's expressivity while enabling quasi-linear computation.

Method: Define a capacity for each kernel as the maximum sequence length for which the attention matrix can approximate the identity (higher = more expressive). Analyze softmax, Gauss, Laplace, and finite-dimensional feature map kernels. Propose additive kernels built from univariate spline and polynomial exponential kernels, computed via sorting.

Result: Expressive kernels (softmax, Gauss, Laplace) have infinite capacity. Common quasi-linear kernels from finite-dimensional feature maps have finite capacity. The proposed additive spline/polynomial exponential kernels maintain infinite capacity while allowing quasi-linear computation. Efficient implementation shows advantages over modern softmax backends for long sequences.

Conclusion: Additive kernels constructed from spline and polynomial exponential kernels achieve both infinite capacity (full expressivity) and quasi-linear computation, offering practical gains for long-sequence attention.

Abstract: The evaluation cost of transformers with softmax attention scales quadratically with sequence length. Kernel attention addresses this by replacing softmax with a more general kernel function. In this paper, we aim to identify kernels that retain the expressivity of attention while enabling quasi linear computation. To quantify expressivity, we introduce a capacity for each kernel, measuring the maximum sequence length for which the attention matrix can approximate the identity. A higher capacity thus indicates greater expressivity. We show that expressive kernels like softmax, Gauss, and Laplace have infinite capacity. In contrast, common quasi linear kernels, such as those derived from finite dimensional feature maps, exhibit finite capacity. As a solution, we propose additive kernels constructed from univariate spline and polynomial exponential kernels. We prove that these maintain infinite capacity while allowing quasi linear computation via sorting. Finally, we implement additive sorting kernels efficiently and benchmark them against modern softmax backends, demonstrating advantages for long sequences.

</details>


### [176] [Universal Approximation of Measure-to-Measure Operators by Pushforwards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35483)
*Takashi Furuya,Nicholas H. Nelsen,Frank Cole*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies when measure-dependent pushforward models can universally approximate continuous operators between spaces of probability measures, showing failure for atomic inputs but success on uniform level set families, with applications to measure-theoretic transformers and cross-attention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many learning tasks map input distributions to output distributions, naturally modeled by transforming each sample via a continuous function depending on the entire input distribution (a measure-dependent pushforward). Despite the rise of measure-theoretic transformer formulations, it was unclear when such models can approximate arbitrary continuous measure-to-measure operators.

Method: The authors first exhibit counterexamples where universal approximation fails when atomic inputs are allowed, since operators splitting or redistributing atomic mass cannot be approximated by deterministic pushforwards. They then introduce the uniform level set condition: a continuous measure-dependent scalarization whose shrinking level set neighborhoods carry uniformly vanishing mass. They prove uniform approximation in p-Wasserstein distance on compact families satisfying this condition, and extend the framework to continuously-varying source measures.

Result: Universal approximation fails for atomic inputs. On every compact family satisfying the uniform level set condition (including compact families of absolutely continuous measures), any continuous measure-to-measure operator with finite p-th moment outputs can be uniformly approximated in p-Wasserstein distance by continuous measure-dependent pushforwards. Combined with existing in-context map approximation results, this yields universal approximation by measure-theoretic transformers; extension gives universality for cross-attention-aligned pushforward models.

Conclusion: Measure-dependent pushforward approximation depends critically on the input family: atomic measures break universality, while the uniform level set condition restores it. This characterizes when measure-theoretic transformers and related attention architectures are universal approximators of continuous measure-to-measure operators.

Abstract: Many learning tasks map an input distribution to an output distribution. A natural way to model such an operator is to transform each input sample using a continuous function that may depend on the entire input distribution, and then take the distribution of the transformed samples. This defines a measure-dependent pushforward model and includes measure-theoretic formulations of transformers. We ask when such models can approximate arbitrary continuous operators between spaces of probability measures. We first show that universal approximation fails when atomic inputs are allowed: some continuous measure-to-measure operators that split or redistribute atomic mass cannot be approximated arbitrarily well by deterministic pushforward models. We then introduce the uniform level set condition, which requires a continuous measure-dependent scalarization whose shrinking level set neighborhoods carry uniformly vanishing mass over the input family. This condition is satisfied, in particular, by compact families of absolutely continuous measures. On every compact family satisfying this condition, we prove that any continuous measure-to-measure operator with outputs of finite $p$-th moment can be uniformly approximated, in the $p$-Wasserstein distance, by continuous measure-dependent pushforwards. Combining our theorem with existing approximation results for measure-dependent in-context maps yields universal approximation by measure-theoretic transformers. We also extend the framework to continuously-varying source measures, yielding a corresponding universality result for a class of pushforward models that are closely aligned with cross-attention architectures.

</details>


### [177] [Neural Harmonic Measure Operator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35752)
*Jinjin He,Sinan Wang,Yuchen Sun,Bo Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NHMO is a neural solver that parameterizes the harmonic measure density as a transformer-based boundary kernel, supervised by Walk-on-Spheres exit samples, to solve Laplace and Poisson PDEs on variable-shape domains without retraining for new boundary values or sources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Elliptic PDE solvers typically need retraining or re-evaluation when boundary values, shapes, or sources change. The harmonic measure depends only on geometry, not boundary data, so learning it once could enable reuse across many boundary conditions—but existing neural-operator approaches do not exploit this structure and struggle with singular volume quadrature for Poisson problems.

Method: NHMO parameterizes the harmonic measure density as a transformer-based boundary kernel trained on Walk-on-Spheres exit samples. For Poisson problems, it uses a classical decomposition with an auxiliary network that amortizes the source-induced correction, avoiding singular volume quadrature. At inference, new boundary values and sources yield solutions via re-integration against the fitted kernel and lift, with no retraining.

Result: NHMO outperforms four prior baselines on the MCB-B 3D variable-shape Poisson benchmark across all five categories, and is competitive with major neural-operator baselines on a controlled 2D testbed.

Conclusion: Learning a geometry-only harmonic measure operator provides a reusable, retraining-free neural solver for elliptic PDEs on variable-shape domains, effectively handling both Laplace and Poisson problems.

Abstract: We introduce Neural Harmonic Measure Operator (NHMO), a neural solver for elliptic PDE problems on variable-shape domains. The harmonic measure of a domain is the boundary probability distribution that, integrated against any boundary data, returns the Dirichlet Laplace solution. It depends only on the geometry, not on the boundary data. NHMO parameterizes the density of this measure as a transformer-based boundary kernel supervised by Walk-on-Spheres exit samples, so one trained kernel handles different boundary values on a shape with no retraining. We extend it to Poisson via a classical decomposition, with an auxiliary network amortizing the source-induced correction and avoiding the singular volume quadrature that breaks direct evaluation. At inference, new boundary values and new sources both yield PDE solutions by re-integration against the fitted kernel and lift, with no retraining. NHMO improves over four prior baselines on the MCB-B 3D variable-shape Poisson benchmark across all five categories, and is competitive with major neural-operator baselines on a controlled 2D testbed.

</details>


<div id='math.CA'></div>

# math.CA [[Back]](#toc)

### [178] [Sharp lower bounds for the periodic maximal Schrödinger operator in higher dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32090)
*Inbo Gottlieb Fenves,Jiahao Tan*

Main category: math.CA

TL;DR: Sharp lower bounds for the periodic Schrödinger maximal function on T^d (d≥2) yield full resolution of Carleson's problem up to endpoint, disproving Miao-Yuan-Zhao's conjecture, via complex multiplication on abelian varieties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Carleson's problem on pointwise convergence of the Schrödinger equation was largely open in the periodic torus setting; the conjectured regularity condition of Miao-Yuan-Zhao needed verification or refutation.

Method: Sharp lower bounds for the Schrödinger maximal function on T^d, combined with complex multiplication on abelian varieties and sufficiency results of Compaan-Lucá-Staffilani.

Result: Sharp regularity conditions for pointwise convergence in all dimensions d≥2, full resolution of Carleson's problem up to endpoint, disproof of Miao-Yuan-Zhao conjecture, and sharp estimates on dimensions of divergence sets for high-dimensional tori.

Conclusion: Carleson's problem for the periodic Schrödinger equation is resolved up to endpoint for all d≥2, with the periodic case contrasting the R^d setting.

Abstract: We prove sharp lower bounds for the Schrödinger maximal function on the torus T^d for all dimensions d at least 2, and as a corollary obtain sharp regularity conditions for pointwise convergence of the periodic Schrödinger equation. Combined with sufficiency results established by Compaan-Lucá-Staffilani, this yields a full resolution of Carleson's problem up to endpoint for the periodic Schrödinger equation in all dimensions at least 2, and disproves the conjectured regularity condition of Miao-Yuan-Zhao, in contrast to the corresponding question in R^d. We also prove sharp estimates on the dimensions of divergence sets for the equation for high dimensional tori. Our approach uses complex multiplication on abelian varieties.

</details>


### [179] [Monotone Sobolev functions: approximation, critical points, and level sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34295)
*Deguang Zhong*

Main category: math.CA

TL;DR: This paper resolves Ntalampekos's planar local smoothing problem affirmatively and answers the approximation and level-set parts of his higher-dimensional question both positively and negatively, via monotone $C^{1,\alpha}$ approximation of Sobolev monotone functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ntalampekos posed a planar local smoothing problem (Question 1.7) and higher-dimensional questions on approximation and level sets (Question 1.8). The paper aims to settle these, distinguishing basic approximation properties from stronger topological and exceptional-set conclusions.

Method: For $1<p<\infty$ and $n\ge2$, continuous Lebesgue monotone $W^{1,p}$ functions on bounded open sets are approximated uniformly and strongly in $W^{1,p}$ by monotone $C^{1,\alpha}_{\loc}$ functions, preserving Sobolev boundary values and not increasing $p$-Dirichlet energy. Planar techniques give smooth local replacement at isolated $p$-harmonic critical points, resolving a gradient index $-m$ point into exactly $m$ nondegenerate saddles. Explicit Lipschitz counterexamples in $n\ge3$ and a product extension of a planar homogeneous 7-harmonic function rule out a general discrete exceptional set.

Result: Affirmative answer to the planar local smoothing problem; positive and negative answers to Question 1.8's approximation and level-set parts. Energy can be made strictly smaller unless the function is $p$-harmonic. Smooth monotone density with fixed boundary values holds in the plane; at nonsmooth $p$-harmonic minimizers the energy increase is unavoidable but tends to zero. In $n\ge3$ an explicit Lipschitz monotone function has a nonmanifold point on every level in an interval, yet admits smooth monotone approximation.

Conclusion: Question 1.8's answer distinguishes five basic approximation properties from stronger topological and exceptional-set conclusions. Applications include constrained integral functionals, strict $BV$ convergence of superlevel sets, nonlinear flux convergence, and stability of persistence diagrams under tameness assumptions.

Abstract: We give an affirmative answer to the planar local smoothing problem in Question~1.7 of D.~Ntalampekos and positive and negative answers to the basic approximation and level-set parts of his higher-dimensional Question~1.8. In every dimension $n\ge2$, each continuous Lebesgue monotone function in $W^{1,p}$ on a bounded open set admits uniform and strong $W^{1,p}$ approximation by monotone $C^{1,α}_{\loc}$ functions, with unchanged Sobolev boundary values and no increase of the $p$-Dirichlet energy, for $1<p<\infty$. The energy can be made strictly smaller unless the original function is $p$-harmonic. In the plane we obtain smooth local replacement at every isolated $p$-harmonic critical point, with arbitrarily small $C^1$ and Sobolev error. A point of gradient index $-m$ can be resolved into exactly $m$ nondegenerate saddles. Together with Ntalampekos's planar theorem, this gives smooth monotone density with fixed boundary values; at a nonsmooth $p$-harmonic minimizer the energy increase is unavoidable but can tend to zero. In dimensions $n\ge3$, an explicit Lipschitz monotone function has a nonmanifold point on every level in an interval, although it admits smooth monotone approximation. A product extension of a planar homogeneous $7$-harmonic function also rules out a general discrete exceptional set under the exact boundary and energy constraints. The answer to Question~1.8 thus distinguishes the five basic approximation properties from the stronger topological and exceptional-set conclusions. Applications include constrained integral functionals, strict $BV$ convergence of superlevel sets, nonlinear flux convergence, and stability of persistence diagrams under tameness assumptions.

</details>


### [180] [Pitt Inequalities and Logarithmic-type Uncertainty Principles for Metaplectic Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34946)
*Gianluca Giacchi,Itamar Oliveira,Amy Tierney*

Main category: math.CA

TL;DR: This work gives a complete characterization of Pitt's inequality for metaplectic operators, with isotropic and anisotropic versions, plus logarithmic and entropic uncertainty principles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Metaplectic operators mix Fourier-type (effective) and concentration-preserving (singular) directions via symplectic geometry, so classical Pitt and entropic uncertainty results do not directly apply.

Method: Adopt two complementary perspectives based on the symplectic geometry of metaplectic operators, distinguishing effective vs. singular directions; derive isotropic and anisotropic Pitt inequalities and analyze entropic corrections.

Result: Obtain isotropic and anisotropic Pitt's inequalities, Pitt's inequality for Fourier transforms along subspaces, quantitative time-dependent boundedness on homogeneous Sobolev spaces, and logarithmic/entropic uncertainty principles with an unbounded corrective entropy term.

Conclusion: The classical entropic uncertainty principle fails when both effective and singular directions exist; a corrective entropy term restores a meaningful lower bound, with applications to Schrödinger evolutions from quadratic Hamiltonians.

Abstract: In this work, we provide a complete characterisation of Pitt's inequality for metaplectic operators. The symplectic geometry underlying a metaplectic operator distinguishes effective directions, along which its action is Fourier-type, from singular directions, along which concentration is preserved. For this reason, we adopt two complementary perspectives, thereby obtaining both an isotropic Pitt's inequality, emulating the classical theorem of Pitt, and an anisotropic inequality that adapts to the geometric features of the metaplectic group. As a by-product, we obtain Pitt's inequality for Fourier transforms along subspaces of $\rd$ and new quantitative time-dependent boundedness results for metaplectic operators on homogeneous Sobolev spaces, with applications to Schrödinger evolutions generated by quadratic Hamiltonians.
  Additionally, we derive logarithmic and entropic uncertainty principles for the metaplectic group. We show that the classical entropic uncertainty principle fails when both the effective and singular directions are present. In this case, a generally unbounded corrective entropy term accounts for the concentration-preserving directions and restores a meaningful lower bound. For quadratic Schrödinger evolutions, this correction follows the time-dependent geometry of the nondispersive directions and adjusts the uncertainty estimate accordingly.

</details>


<div id='physics.flu-dyn'></div>

# physics.flu-dyn [[Back]](#toc)

### [181] [Spall Failure of Coral Skeleton beneath Gas-Laden Canopies: An Idealized Blast-Fishing Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32568)
*Sandy Hardian Susanto Herho,Agus Wahyu Jatmiko,Rizki Dimas Permana,Iwan Pramesti Anwar,Alfita Puspa Handayani,Faruq Khadami,Karina Aprilia Sujatmiko,Rusmawan Suwarman,Deny Juanda Puradimaja,Dasapta Erwin Irawan*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: An idealized bubbly-mixture model shows that gas held in a coral canopy reshapes blast shock loading, giving closed-form criteria for transparency, impulse, and a critical spall thickness, explaining why a given charge is more damaging at noon than at night.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bomb fishing on shallow Indonesian reefs shatters coral, but the damage has been characterized mainly empirically via blast radii and ecological surveys, lacking a mechanistic model of how the gas in the coral canopy affects shock delivery to the skeleton.

Method: The canopy is modeled as a relaxed bubbly mixture with shock impedance from mass and momentum conservation. The reef is a layered column (water, canopy, skeletal plate, canopy) struck at normal incidence, yielding closed-form results; bubble dynamics and a prescribed daily photosynthetic gas cycle are also considered.

Result: Three closed-form findings: above a crossover pressure set by void fraction and seawater stiffness the canopy becomes nearly transparent; transmitted impulse through a lossless layered stack is independent of the canopy; and a plate carries tension (spall) only when canopy impedance falls below a threshold defining a critical thickness. For a 1 kg charge, a gas-rich canopy more than triples the spall standoff for a 12 cm plate while shortening the crush standoff, and the same charge is more damaging at noon than at night.

Conclusion: The model converts coral canopy gas content into quantitative predictors of blast damage, with daily gas cycles making blasts markedly more damaging at noon; since the canopy cannot equilibrate within the pulse, results are best treated as upper bounds.

Abstract: Improvised explosives used for fishing on shallow Indonesian reefs shatter coral skeleton, yet the damage they cause has been described largely through empirical radii and ecological surveys. We formulate an idealized model of how free gas held within a coral canopy modifies the shock loading that such a charge delivers to skeletal plates. The canopy is treated as a relaxed bubbly mixture whose shock impedance follows from the conservation of mass and momentum, and the reef is represented as a layered column of water, canopy, skeletal plate, and canopy struck at normal incidence. Three closed-form results emerge. Above a crossover pressure set by the void fraction and the stiffness of seawater, the canopy becomes nearly transparent to the shock. The impulse transmitted through any lossless layered stack is independent of the canopy, so gas redistributes the pulse in time without changing its total push. A plate carries tension after reflection from its lower face only when the canopy impedance falls below a threshold fixed by the plate thickness and the pulse duration, which defines a critical thickness. For a one-kilogram charge directly overhead, a gas-rich canopy more than triples the standoff at which a twelve-centimeter plate spalls while shortening the standoff at which it is crushed. A prescribed daily cycle of photosynthetic gas makes the same charge markedly more damaging at noon than at night. Bubble dynamics show that the canopy does not reach equilibrium within the pulse, so the results are best read as upper bounds.

</details>


### [182] [Phase-field simulation of interfacial stability in structured gas-liquid contactors: design rules for gas diffusion electrodes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34755)
*Alexander J. Wagner,Hari Murali Krishna Varma Gottumukkala,Henning Bonart*

Main category: physics.flu-dyn

TL;DR: Phase-field simulations reveal how geometry, wettability, and 3D topology control the operating pressure window of gas-liquid interfaces in structured porous media, yielding design rules for gas diffusion electrodes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stationary gas-liquid interfaces inside structured solids (e.g., GDEs) are hard to maintain, especially in organic electrosynthesis where low surface tensions and small contact angles reduce flooding/breakthrough margins. A quantitative understanding of what sets this admissible pressure window is lacking.

Method: Cahn-Hilliard-Navier-Stokes phase-field simulations of gas-liquid interfaces in structured media. Each geometry is characterized by a Laplace pressure curve giving maximum admissible pressure difference and interface stiffness against fluctuations. Studied single flat orifices (varying aspect ratio at constant open area), equal-width slits, sheared diamond-shaped openings, and woven meshes with varying wettability.

Result: For single flat orifices, elongating the opening from aspect ratio 1 to 6 raises admissible pressure by 40% and stiffness by 3.3x. Comparison with equal-width slits shows the gain comes entirely from reduced opening width, not elongation. Sheared diamond openings are less effective. In woven meshes, wettability displaces the operating window several times more than it stretches it; less wetting trades flooding margin for breakthrough margin.

Conclusion: The geometric route (e.g., elongating flat openings) widens the operating window itself, unlike wettability changes which merely shift it. The authors derive design rules for structured gas-liquid contactors and improved GDE architectures for organic electrosynthesis.

Abstract: Many process engineering applications hold a gas-liquid interface stationary inside a structured solid by a controlled pressure difference. Gas diffusion electrodes (GDEs) are one such case, where the interface separates the liquid electrolyte from the gaseous reactant. Maintaining it is particularly challenging in organic electrosynthesis, where low surface tensions lower the admissible pressures throughout and small contact angles leave the electrode with little margin against flooding. Here, we quantify how the shape, wettability, and three-dimensional topology of a structured medium set this window, using phase-field simulations of the Cahn-Hilliard-Navier-Stokes equations. Each geometry is characterised by its Laplace pressure curve, which yields both the maximum admissible pressure difference and the stiffness of the interface against pressure fluctuations. For single flat orifices at constant open area, elongating the opening from an aspect ratio of one to six raises the admissible pressure difference by 40 % and the stiffness by a factor of 3.3. Comparison with slits of equal width shows that this gain originates entirely from the reduced opening width. Sheared, diamond shaped openings are markedly less effective. In woven meshes, the wettability displaces the operating window several times as far as it stretches it. A less wetting surface therefore buys flooding margin largely at the expense of breakthrough margin, whereas the geometric route, established here for flat openings, widens the window itself. From these results we derive design rules for structured gas-liquid contactors and improved GDE architectures for organic electrosynthesis.

</details>


<div id='cs.CE'></div>

# cs.CE [[Back]](#toc)

### [183] [Adapting neural operators for mechanics decisions under changing operating conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33978)
*Prashant K. Jha,Koffi Enakoutsa,Ian Galloway,Henry Anderson*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: Reusing high-fidelity FE loading paths to periodically update a neural operator extends its useful operating range and improves subsequent command selection, though error estimation remains inconsistent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural operators accelerate repeated nonlinear mechanics calculations but lose accuracy when operating conditions move beyond the training range. This work asks whether high-fidelity solutions acquired during use can be reused to adapt the operator and improve mechanics-based command selection.

Method: Two hard-magnetic soft-material systems are simulated with high-fidelity FE models as references. A neural operator predicts deformation from material, loading, and magnetic-field inputs; an empirical error estimator decides which predictions are usable for command selection. Selected FE evaluations supplement predictions, and their full loading paths are retained for periodic updates of both operator and estimator.

Result: The fixed operator loses substantial accuracy outside the training range for stiffness and loading. Updates using 16 acquired paths recover much of the lost accuracy while preserving nominal-regime accuracy. Under the same FE budget, updated operators improve command selection, though benefit varies by operating condition. Error estimation is inconsistent—some inaccurate predictions accepted, some accurate ones rejected.

Conclusion: Reusing high-fidelity loading paths can extend a neural operator's useful operating range. However, improved forward accuracy alone does not guarantee reliable prediction acceptance, making prediction-specific error assessment a separate requirement for trustworthy decision making.

Abstract: Neural operators can accelerate repeated nonlinear mechanics calculations, but their accuracy can deteriorate as operating conditions move beyond the training range. This work studies whether high-fidelity solutions acquired during use can be reused to adapt a neural operator and improve subsequent mechanics-based command selection. Two hard-magnetic soft-material systems are simulated using high-fidelity finite-element (FE) models, providing reference solutions for evaluating surrogate predictions and selected commands. A neural operator predicts deformation from known material, loading, and magnetic-field inputs, while an empirical error estimator determines which predictions may be used for command selection. Selected FE evaluations supplement these predictions, and their complete loading paths are retained for periodic updates of the neural operator and estimator. In both examples, the fixed operator loses substantial accuracy when stiffness and loading move outside the training range. Updates using 16 acquired paths recover much of the lost accuracy while preserving accuracy in the nominal regime. Under the same FE evaluation budget, the updated operators also improve command selection, although the benefit varies with the operating condition. Error estimation is less consistent, with inaccurate predictions sometimes accepted and accurate predictions rejected. These results demonstrate that reusing high-fidelity loading paths can extend the useful operating range of a neural operator. However, improved forward accuracy alone does not guarantee reliable prediction acceptance, highlighting prediction-specific error assessment as a separate requirement for trustworthy decision making.

</details>


### [184] [Latent Twin Operator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35531)
*Deepanshu Verma,Riley Chen,Matthias Chung*

Main category: cs.CE

TL;DR: The paper introduces the Latent Twin Operator (LTO), a latent-space surrogate model for time-evolving PDEs that handles arbitrary sensor placements and resolutions, with a derived context discretization error rate verified on benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Surrogate models on real physical systems rarely see data at fixed resolutions: sensor configurations vary across deployments and evolve over time. Existing surrogates are tied to specific discretizations, limiting flexibility across deployments and sensing infrastructure changes.

Method: LTO uses a ConvCNP-style encoder/decoder that accepts a context set of N sensor observations with arbitrary placement and can be queried at any resolution. A learned latent evolution map advances the encoded state directly between arbitrary time points, decoupling temporal evolution from observation and query discretizations. The authors derive an explicit O(N^{-2/(3D)}) rate for context discretization error in spatial dimension D under quasi-uniform refinement.

Result: The predicted error decay is verified empirically on a 2D heat-equation benchmark. Across time-dependent PDE benchmarks, LTO achieves strong accuracy under one-step comparisons and transfers from native to coarser spatial resolutions with fixed parameters. On Navier-Stokes, direct latent evolution reduces error over longer prediction horizons compared to recursive evaluation.

Conclusion: LTO provides a resolution-flexible latent surrogate for time-evolving PDEs that decouples temporal evolution from discretization, offering theoretical error guarantees and improved long-horizon prediction performance.

Abstract: Surrogate models deployed on real physical systems rarely see data at fixed resolutions: sensor configurations vary across deployments and may evolve over time as sensing infrastructure changes. We introduce the Latent Twin Operator (LTO), a latent-space surrogate for time-evolving PDEs with a Convolutional Conditional Neural Process (ConvCNP)-style encoder and decoder that accepts a context set of $N$ sensor observations with arbitrary placement and can be queried at any resolution. A learned latent evolution map advances the encoded state directly between arbitrary time points, decoupling temporal evolution from the observation and query discretizations. We derive an explicit $\mathcal{O}(N^{-2/(3D)})$ rate for the context discretization error in spatial dimension $D$ under quasi-uniform refinement. We verify the predicted decay empirically on a 2D heat-equation benchmark. Across time-dependent PDE benchmarks, LTO achieves strong accuracy under one-step comparisons and transfers from native to coarser spatial resolutions with fixed parameters. On Navier--Stokes, its direct latent evolution further reduces error over longer prediction horizons relative to recursive evaluation.

</details>


<div id='physics.optics'></div>

# physics.optics [[Back]](#toc)

### [185] [Discrete quality-factor control in a side-coupled photonic crystal microcavity: evanescent Bloch tunnelling and the finite-cell correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31952)
*Hasan Oguz*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: A point-defect microcavity side-coupled to a W1 waveguide in a silicon-rod photonic crystal immersed in water is studied, showing resonance tunability and quality-factor control by waveguide separation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need to understand and control microcavity resonance and quality factor in an aqueous environment for sensing applications, relying on predictive design rather than costly full simulations.

Method: Plane-wave expansion and finite-difference time-domain computations of a point-defect microcavity side-coupled to a W1 waveguide in a 2D photonic crystal of silicon rods in aqueous analyte. Perturbation theory and evanescent Bloch channel analysis used to predict behavior.

Result: Resonance moves with defect rod radius squared as first-order perturbation theory predicts; Q-factor controlled by cavity-waveguide separation (each lattice row multiplies Q by 7.10), weakly sensitive to rod displacement (<7%). Sensitivity of 634 nm/RIU at 1550 nm; water absorption lowers Q by a third, capping near 9200.

Conclusion: Q-factor scaling is predictable from evanescent Bloch decay without adjustable parameters. Reliable values require cladding convergence and removal of waveguide-end reflections, otherwise Q errors up to a factor of two. Water absorption limits benefits of adding more rows.

Abstract: A point-defect microcavity side-coupled to a W1 waveguide in a two-dimensional photonic crystal of silicon rods immersed in an aqueous analyte is studied by plane-wave expansion and finite-difference time-domain computation. The resonance moves continuously through the transverse-magnetic band gap with the square of the radius of the defect rod, as first-order perturbation theory predicts for the dielectric area restored to the lattice site. The quality factor is set instead by the separation between cavity and waveguide, counted in lattice rows: each added row multiplies it by 7.10 at the reference defect radius, whereas moving the defect rod by up to a tenth of a lattice period changes it by less than 7 per cent. The per-row factor follows, at two defect radii and with no adjustable parameter, from the decay of the slowest evanescent Bloch channel of the crystal at the wavevector of the guided mode. Two checks are needed before such values can be trusted in a finite cell: convergence in cladding thickness and removal of the reflections from the waveguide ends. Without them the per-row factor comes out too low and the quality factor wrong by up to a factor of two. Scaled to 1550 nm the design gives a sensitivity of 634 nm per refractive index unit, which perturbation theory reproduces, and a Fano fit to an independently normalised transmission spectrum confirms the linewidth. The absorption of water at this wavelength lowers the quality factor of the reference geometry by a third and caps it near 9200, which limits the benefit of adding further rows.

</details>


### [186] [Monolithically integrated photonic neural network at driven-dissipative criticality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34171)
*Yuming Zhang,Ruoran Wang,Jingcheng Li,Hailong Zhou,Jianji Dong,Zhipei Sun,Xinliang Zhang*

Main category: physics.optics

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates a monolithically integrated active photonic neural network (APNN) that exploits driven-dissipative criticality on a silicon photonic chip as a unified physical computational resource for classification, temporal processing, and error-resilient computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI demands architectures for adaptive representation, temporal processing, and robust operation under uncertainty, but conventional photonic neural networks rely on predefined operations and separate modules, limiting exploitation of intrinsic physical dynamics.

Method: The APNN balances optical excitation and dissipation to operate near a critical regime, integrating an optoelectronic nonlinear array with a reconfigurable symmetric optical coupling matrix on a silicon photonic chip, enabling recurrent physical computation in a compact closed loop.

Result: Achieves 95.2% nonlinear classification accuracy with only 100 training samples and 44 trainable parameters, remains robust at -30 dB SNR, and enables long-range temporal feature extraction from ECG signals and associative recovery of corrupted patterns.

Conclusion: Driven-dissipative criticality serves as a unified mechanism for representation enhancement, temporal retention, and error-resilient computation, offering a pathway toward scalable photonic systems where intelligence emerges from intrinsic physical dynamics.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence increasingly demands computing architectures capable of adaptive representation, temporal information processing, and robust operation under uncertainty. However, conventional photonic neural architectures typically rely on predefined computational operations and separate functional modules, limiting the ability of physical systems to exploit their intrinsic dynamics for intelligence. Here, we demonstrate a monolithically integrated active photonic neural network (APNN) that harnesses driven-dissipative criticality as a physical computational resource. By balancing optical excitation and dissipation, the system operates near a critical regime where weak input-dependent variations are amplified into separable representations while stable attractor dynamics are preserved. The APNN integrates an optoelectronic nonlinear array and a reconfigurable symmetric optical coupling matrix on a silicon photonic chip, enabling recurrent physical computation within a compact closed loop. We demonstrate nonlinear classification with 95.2% accuracy using only 100 training samples and 44 trainable parameters. And it remains robust at an ultralow signal-to-noise ratio of -30 dB. Beyond static recognition, the same driven-dissipative dynamics enable long-range temporal feature extraction from electrocardiogram signals and associative recovery of corrupted patterns through attractor-based relaxation. These results establish driven-dissipative criticality as a unified mechanism for representation enhancement, temporal information retention, and error-resilient computation, providing a pathway toward scalable photonic systems in which intelligence emerges from intrinsic physical dynamics.

</details>


<div id='physics.space-ph'></div>

# physics.space-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [187] [Spectral Properties of Solar Wind Charge Exchange from Earth's Magnetospheric Boundaries: MHD and Test Particle Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35098)
*A. Ramachandran,R. T. Desai,H. L. Norman,Y. Tong,D. Bewicke,S. Nitti,J. A. Carter,J. P. Eastwood,J. P. Chittenden*

Main category: physics.space-ph

TL;DR: This study simulates Solar Wind Charge Exchange (SWCX) X-ray signatures at Earth's outer boundaries using the XIM framework, combining Gorgon global MHD simulations with kinetic particle-based simulations, to predict what X-ray observatories like SMILE might observe.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SWCX generates X-rays via interactions between heavy, highly charged solar wind ions and neutral atoms, offering a way to observe large-scale energy and momentum transfer through the Sun-Earth system. Understanding these signatures supports interpretation of space-based X-ray observations.

Method: The authors combine Gorgon global magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) simulations with kinetic particle-based simulations in the X-ray Imaging of the Magnetosphere (XIM) framework to simulate SWCX signatures from Earth's outer boundaries.

Result: XIM-predicted emissivities agree with MHD predictions, showing enhanced emissions from the magnetosheath and cusp regions, but with differences: XIM shows enhanced structuring in the magnetosheath and notably enhanced emissions along the magnetopause linked to betatron acceleration. Species-specific spatial variations appear that differ from anticipated solar wind relative abundances and ion-specific properties.

Conclusion: These simulations provide new insights into SWCX dynamics at Earth's outer boundaries, useful for understanding what X-ray observatories such as the recently launched SMILE mission might observe.

Abstract: The Solar Wind Charge Exchange (SWCX) mechanism, where X-rays can be generated through interactions between heavy, highly charged solar wind ions and neutral atoms, offers an opportunity to observe the large-scale transfers of energy and momentum through the Sun-Earth system. In this study, we simulate SWCX signatures from Earth's outer boundaries using Gorgon global magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) simulations combined with kinetic particle-based simulations which we call the X-ray Imaging of the Magnetosphere (XIM) framework. The XIM-predicted emissivities agree with the predictions of the MHD model, producing enhanced emissions from the magnetosheath and cusp regions, but several differences are apparent. Compared with MHD, XIM shows enhanced structuring in the magnetosheath and notable enhanced emissions along the magnetopause associated with betatron acceleration. The results also evidence species-specific spatial variations, which are shown to vary compared to anticipated relative abundances in the solar wind and ion-specific properties. These simulations provide new insights into the underlying dynamics of SWCX at the Earth's outer boundaries, which can be used to understand what might be observed using X-ray observatories in space, such as the recently launched SMILE mission.

</details>


<div id='gr-qc'></div>

# gr-qc [[Back]](#toc)

### [188] [A construction of charged naked singularities for the Einstein--Maxwell-charged scalar field equations in 3+1 dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33168)
*Weihao Zheng*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: The paper constructs global naked singularity solutions to the (3+1)-dimensional spherically symmetric Einstein-Maxwell-charged scalar field equations from asymptotically flat characteristic initial data, yielding genuinely charged spacetimes with a curvature singularity not hidden behind an event horizon.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Naked singularities (curvature singularities not enclosed by an event horizon) challenge cosmic censorship. Constructing explicit, genuinely charged examples in the Einstein-Maxwell-charged scalar field setting is a significant open problem, motivating a systematic framework for building such solutions.

Method: The authors use characteristic initial data on a past light cone that is C^{1,α} asymptotically flat and one-ended (0<α≪1). They develop a general framework for constructing naked singularity exteriors—the causal future region of the light cone emanating from the singularity—which accommodates broad singular characteristic data, including singular Maxwell behavior near the singularity.

Result: They obtain a class of global naked singularity solutions with a nonzero Maxwell field satisfying a quantitative positive lower bound up to the singularity, and an explicit asymptotic profile. The singularity is a curvature singularity not enclosed by an event horizon.

Conclusion: The work provides a general method for constructing naked singularity exteriors and produces concrete genuinely charged naked singularity spacetimes, expanding the known examples relevant to cosmic censorship in the Einstein-Maxwell-charged scalar field system.

Abstract: We construct a class of global naked singularity solutions for the $(3+1)$-dimensional spherically symmetric Einstein--Maxwell-charged scalar field equations. The solutions arise from $C^{1,α}$ asymptotically flat one-ended characteristic initial data with $0<α\ll1$ and develop a curvature singularity that is not enclosed by an event horizon. The resulting spacetimes are genuinely charged, with a nonzero Maxwell field that satisfies a quantitative positive lower bound up to the singularity and admits an explicit asymptotic profile. As part of the construction, we develop a general framework for constructing naked singularity exteriors, namely, the causal future region of the past light cone emanating from the singularity. The framework accommodates a broad range of singular characteristic data and, in particular, allows for singular behavior of the Maxwell field near the singularity.

</details>


### [189] [Perturbations of Kerr-Newman black holes: separation and mode stability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33661)
*Peter Hintz*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: The paper proves mode stability (absence of real-frequency quasinormal modes) for linearized perturbations of subextremal Kerr-Newman black holes, overcoming the coupled spin-1/spin-2 field problem via a matrix integrating factor and a Whiting-type transform.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chandrasekhar noted an "apparent indissolubility of the coupling between the spin-1 and spin-2 fields," making the analysis of Kerr-Newman perturbations difficult since these fields cannot be easily decoupled.

Method: The authors introduce a matrix integrating factor applied to suitably transformed first-order systems describing the radiative degrees of freedom. On the separated radial ODEs, they use a Whiting-type transform to establish mode stability on the real axis.

Result: They overcome the spin-1/spin-2 coupling obstacle and prove the absence of quasinormal modes with real frequencies for the radial ODEs of the subextremal Kerr-Newman black hole.

Conclusion: Mode stability on the real axis holds for linearized Kerr-Newman perturbations, providing a key step toward understanding the stability of charged, rotating black holes.

Abstract: We study linearized perturbations of the subextremal Kerr-Newman black hole solution of the Einstein-Maxwell equations. We show how to overcome what Chandrasekhar called the "apparent indissolubility of the coupling between the spin-1 and spin-2 fields" by using a matrix integrating factor for suitably transformed first-order systems that describe the radiative degrees of freedom. For the radial ODEs obtained after angular separation, we prove mode stability on the real axis, i.e., the absence of quasinormal modes with real frequencies, using a Whiting-type transform.

</details>


<div id='math.SP'></div>

# math.SP [[Back]](#toc)

### [190] [Off-diagonal bounds for spectral projector kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33957)
*Panagiotis Dimakis*

Main category: math.SP

TL;DR: The paper bounds the growth of the spectral function of the Laplace-Beltrami operator on an n-dimensional Riemannian manifold, showing almost-sharp pointwise bounds off a measure-zero set.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the growth of the spectral function (the kernel of the spectral projector) is central to spectral geometry and relates to eigenvalue asymptotics and Weyl's law. Sharp pointwise bounds for individual points are subtle because of potential exceptional sets.

Method: The authors analyze the spectral function E_Λ(x,y) of the Laplace-Beltrami operator on a Riemannian manifold M of dimension n, establishing bounds that hold for all y in a full-measure subset Z_x of M (allowing exceptional sets of measure zero, with constants depending on M, x, y, ε).

Result: For n ≥ 3: E_Λ(x,y) = O(Λ^{n/2}(log Λ)^{3/2}(log log Λ)^{1/2+ε}) for all y in a full-measure set. For n = 2: a stronger bound E_Λ(x,y) = O(Λ^{5/6}(log Λ)^{1/6}(log log Λ)^{1/6+ε}) holds for all y in a full-measure set.

Conclusion: The spectral function obeys almost-sharp pointwise growth bounds off a measure-zero exceptional set, with a stronger result in dimension two. These characterize the typical (almost-everywhere) pointwise behavior of the spectral function.

Abstract: We study the growth of the spectral function of the Laplace-Beltrami operator on a Riemannian manifold $M$ of dimension $n$. For $n\geq 3$ we show that for any $x\in M$ there is a full measure subset $Z_x\subset M$ such that for all $y\in Z_x$ \[E_Λ(x,y) = O_{M,x,y,\varepsilon}(Λ^{n/2}(\logΛ)^{3/2}(\log\logΛ)^{1/2+\varepsilon}). \] For $n=2$ we prove a stronger statement. For any $x\in M$ there is a full measure subset $Z_x\subset M$ such that for all $y\in Z_x$ \[E_Λ(x,y) = O_{M,x,y,\varepsilon}(Λ^{5/6}(\logΛ)^{1/6}(\log\logΛ)^{1/6+\varepsilon}) .\]

</details>


<div id='physics.chem-ph'></div>

# physics.chem-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [191] [Basis Functions for Time-Dependent Kohn-Sham Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34164)
*Paul M. Zimmerman,Joshua Kammeraad,Duy-Khoi Dang,Harish S. Bhat*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Floquet theory is used to derive an inversion scheme for time-dependent Kohn-Sham DFT, recovering exchange-correlation potentials from time-dependent wavefunctions via a harmonic-based ansatz.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time-dependent Kohn-Sham DFT lacks accessible exchange-correlation potentials, hindering development of nonadiabatic functionals. An inversion approach linking time-dependent wavefunctions to these potentials is needed.

Method: Floquet theory analyzes the inversion of TD-KS DFT. Mathematical derivations show the fundamental frequencies of a time-dependent wavefunction solution are the leading-order harmonics of the TD-KS state, yielding an inversion ansatz.

Result: Numerical tests in 1D and 3D for atomic and molecular cases show low L2 errors in time-dependent density and longitudinal current, even though currents were not explicitly optimized.

Conclusion: The proposed inversion ansatz provides exchange-correlation potentials from time-dependent wavefunctions, is highly interpretable, and may significantly aid nonadiabatic density functional development.

Abstract: Floquet theory provides insight into the inversion of time-dependent Kohn-Sham density functional theory. Specifically, mathematical derivations show that the fundamental frequencies of a time-dependent wavefunction solution are the leading-order harmonics for the TD-KS state. Numerical tests of the resulting ansatz in 1D and 3D for atomic and molecular cases demonstrate its utility. In particular, low $L_{2}$ errors in the time-dependent density and longitudinal current were found, even though currents were not an explicit optimization objective. In all, the proposed inversion ansatz provides exchange-correlation potentials from time-dependent wavefunctions, is highly interpretable, and may significantly help in the development of nonadiabatic density functionals.

</details>


<div id='cs.LO'></div>

# cs.LO [[Back]](#toc)

### [192] [Developing a Numerical Algorithm with CIVL Model Checking in the Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31964)
*Youngjun Lee,Anshu Dubey,Jan Hückelheim*

Main category: cs.LO

TL;DR: This paper reports a case study using the CIVL model checker to verify a new cloud-in-cell (CIC) deposition algorithm for the Flash-X simulation framework, extracting minimal interfaces into a small C model and proving key physical and safety properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Verifying numerical algorithms within large scientific simulation frameworks is hard: the framework is too large to model-check, and unit tests only cover sampled inputs, leaving gaps in confidence.

Method: Extract only the interfaces the CIC algorithm needs into a small, self-contained C model that abstracts away unrelated Flash-X infrastructure, then build and check the algorithm within this model using CIVL with symbolic particle positions, keeping verification properties first in a bottom-up prototyping workflow.

Result: CIVL proved mass conservation and deposition location for the continuum of admissible positions, and verified memory safety and freedom from MPI deadlocks and data races across all rank distributions within bounds. It also surfaced a concurrency defect missed by random-seed-based tests.

Conclusion: The bottom-up verification workflow turns CIVL into a design guardrail, greatly increasing confidence in the extended algorithm; the paper aims to help readers understand the benefits, cost, and tradeoffs relative to testing.

Abstract: Verifying a numerical algorithm in a large scientific simulation framework is challenging: the framework is too big to model-check, and the unit tests exercise only sampled inputs. We report a case study in which we developed a new cloud-in-cell (CIC) deposition algorithm for Flash-X, a large-scale multiphysics simulation framework, keeping the CIVL model checker in the development loop. Rather than verify the algorithm within Flash-X's hefty infrastructure, we extract only the interfaces that the algorithm needs into a small, self-contained C model, which abstracts away implementation details of the Flash-X infrastructure that are unrelated to the new algorithm. The new algorithm is then built and checked within this C model. The CIVL model checker enables verification of the required physical properties of the CIC deposition algorithm using symbolic values for the particle positions. It proves two physical properties---mass conservation and the deposition location---for the continuum of admissible positions in the simulation domain. CIVL also verifies the algorithm's memory safety and freedom from MPI deadlocks and data race conditions over all rank distributions within specified bounds. Writing the verifying properties first and continuously checking them at each stage of the bottom-up prototyping workflow turned CIVL into a design guardrail that greatly increased confidence in the extended algorithm. During our case study, CIVL surfaced a concurrency defect in the algorithm that our random-seed-based tests failed to exercise. This paper shows our workflow, with the goal of helping readers understand its benefits, cost, and tradeoffs compared with testing.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [193] [Why Directly Learning Periodic Trajectories Can Fail](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32254)
*Kaixin Zheng,Anita Layton*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Operator learning of periodic solutions fails when trajectories are unaligned or recorded over fixed physical-time windows; decoupling aligned normalized waveforms from their periods avoids these error floors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard simulation-data preparation for periodic orbits (long integration to remove transients, wide windows to capture full periods) is conservative but makes trajectories hard to learn, even when orbits vary regularly with parameters.

Method: Analyze why unaligned trajectories and fixed-window predictions generalize poorly, attributing both to rapid phase variation from frequency differences accumulating over time. Derive a population MSE floor and a per-sample lower bound. Propose learning an aligned, normalized waveform and its period separately, establishing regularity of the decoupled targets under ODE assumptions.

Result: Unaligned trajectories generalize poorly even in-distribution; phase alignment fixes in-distribution generalization but fixed-window models still fail on periods outside training range. The decoupled waveform+period representation avoids both failures in ODE systems and a PDE case study.

Conclusion: Decoupling aligned normalized waveform from period is a simple, effective representation escaping the identified MSE floors, with theoretical regularity guarantees and empirical success on ODE and PDE cases.

Abstract: Operator learning of periodic solutions requires deciding how simulation data should be recorded and represented. A natural choice is to integrate long enough for transients to decay and record a window wide enough to contain at least one full period of all trajectories. We find that these conservative choices can make the resulting trajectories difficult to learn, even when the underlying periodic orbits vary regularly with system parameters. Unaligned trajectories generalize poorly even within the training distribution. Phase alignment substantially improves in-distribution generalization, but models trained on a fixed physical-time window still have large errors on trajectories with periods outside the training range. We explain both failures through a common mechanism: frequency differences accumulate over time, so the target phase varies rapidly with the parameters. Predictors that cannot track this variation incur a population MSE floor in both settings; for fixed window prediction, we also derive a per-sample lower bound. We then study one of the simplest representations that escape these floors: learning an aligned, normalized waveform and its period separately. We establish regularity of the decoupled targets under ODE assumptions and show experimentally that this approach avoids both failures in ODE systems and a PDE case study.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.stat-mech'></div>

# cond-mat.stat-mech [[Back]](#toc)

### [194] [Criticality without Temperature in an Ising Spin System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33361)
*Christos Kakalis,Fotios K. Diakonos*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Critical-like behavior in an Ising system can emerge from adaptive cluster dynamics with no temperature parameter and Hamiltonian-independent dynamics, driven purely by memory-dependent spin connectivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Criticality in the Ising model is conventionally generated by Hamiltonian dynamics and controlled by temperature. The authors aim to show that critical-like behavior can emerge without energy-based dynamics or any temperature-like parameter.

Method: Introduces an adaptive cluster dynamics where spin connectivity is controlled by a local "quiet time" variable (time since a spin was last updated). The Ising Hamiltonian enters only as an observable. The connectivity strength is tuned by a parameter alpha.

Result: As alpha is varied, the system undergoes spontaneous symmetry breaking with strong collective fluctuations. The cluster size distribution shows an extended power law regime terminated by a small finite-size hump at the largest scales, and the Ising energy exhibits a singular response to alpha.

Conclusion: Memory-dependent connectivity alone can generate collective critical behavior, revealing a route to nonequilibrium criticality without energy-based dynamics.

Abstract: Criticality in the Ising model is conventionally generated by Hamiltonian dynamics and controlled by temperature. Here we show that critical like behavior can emerge in an Ising system whose dynamics is completely independent of the Ising Hamiltonian and contains no temperature like parameter. We introduce an adaptive cluster dynamics in which spin connectivity is controlled by a local quiet time variable, the time elapsed since a spin was last updated. The Ising Hamiltonian enters only as an observable characterizing the resulting configurations. As the parameter $α$, controlling the connectivity strength, is varied, the system undergoes spontaneous symmetry breaking accompanied by strong collective fluctuations. The cluster size distribution develops an extended power law regime, terminated by a small finite size hump at the largest scales, while the Ising energy exhibits a singular response to the variation of $α$. These results show that memory dependent connectivity alone can generate collective critical behavior, revealing a route to nonequilibrium criticality without energy based dynamics.

</details>

# math.NT [[Back]](#toc)

### [195] [An improved high-low method for the parabola](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33949)
*Zane Kun Li,Po-Lam Yung*

Main category: math.NT

TL;DR: A modification of the Guth-Maldague-Wang high-low method yields a decoupling theorem giving the sharp discrete restriction constant for the parabola and a diameter-free bound for three-fold additive energy of rational points on the parabola and circle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reproduce and unify recent results (Deng-Fan-Guo-Guo-Luo; Cushman-Demeter-Wu) via a refined high-low framework.

Method: Modified Guth-Maldague-Wang high-low method, featuring an orthogonal expansion of F^3 and a new square function retaining pruned wavepackets, giving improved high and low lemmas.

Result: Sharp discrete restriction constant for the parabola and a diameter-free bound for the three-fold additive energy of rational points on the parabola and circle.

Conclusion: The modified high-low approach recovers and strengthens these recent results with more efficient arguments.

Abstract: We demonstrate a modification of the high-low method of Guth-Maldague-Wang that yields a decoupling theorem which gives the sharp estimate for the discrete restriction constant for the parabola and also a diameter-free bound for the three-fold additive energy of rational points on the parabola and circle. These results were recently obtained by Deng-Fan-Guo-Guo-Luo and Cushman-Demeter-Wu, respectively. The novel features in this argument are an orthogonal expansion of $F^3$ and a new square function that also keeps the wavepackets discarded after pruning, resulting in new and more efficient high and low lemmas.

</details>

# cs.MS [[Back]](#toc)

### [196] [A performance enhancement of the Payne-Hanek range reduction algorithm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35015)
*Tue Ly*

Main category: cs.MS

TL;DR: The paper presents a branch-free, floating-point-only variation of the Payne–Hanek algorithm for accurate range reduction of trigonometric functions over large double-precision inputs, accurate to within one ulp, suited for SIMD and FMA hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Range reduction is critical for accuracy and performance of trigonometric functions, and is the main bottleneck for large floating-point inputs. Payne–Hanek handles large inputs accurately but existing implementations have high latency due to branching, conversion overheads, and multi-word integer arithmetic, hindering SIMD vectorization.

Method: A branch-free Payne–Hanek variant using only floating-point arithmetic. It operates directly on large double-precision inputs (|x| ≥ 2^16), targets FMA hardware, formulates precision constraints via a truncation error budget, and uses a compact lookup table indexed by input exponent.

Result: The reduced argument is proven accurate to within one ulp for every input. The routine improves both latency and throughput over existing implementations, and serves both as full range reduction for a single-stage implementation and as the fast path of a correctly rounded one.

Conclusion: The proposed algorithm is efficient and vectorizable, currently implemented in the LLVM libc project.

Abstract: Range reduction plays a crucial role in the accuracy and performance of evaluating trigonometric functions, and is often the primary bottleneck for large floating-point inputs. While fast algorithms such as Cody--Waite work efficiently over narrow intervals, the Payne--Hanek algorithm remains the standard technique for accurate reduction across large floating-point inputs. However, existing implementations of Payne--Hanek suffer from high latency due to heavy branching, conversion overheads, and the use of multi-word integer arithmetic, which hinders SIMD vectorization. In this paper, we analyze and present a branch-free variation of the Payne--Hanek algorithm using only floating-point arithmetic. Our method operates directly over large double-precision inputs ($|x| \ge 2^{16}$) and is well suited to hardware with FMA instructions. We formulate the precision constraints in terms of a truncation error budget, construct a compact lookup table indexed by the input exponent, and prove that the reduced argument is accurate to within one ulp for every input. The same routine can serve both as the complete range reduction of a single-stage implementation and as the fast path of a correctly rounded one, and it improves both latency and throughput over existing implementations. The algorithm is currently implemented in the LLVM libc project.

</details>
